i think that the german presidency is right to give priority to the future of the union in the light of the treaties. however, i am disappointed in the way it has been handled. i think it is a fundamental mistake for you to let the rejected constitution prevail over the opening of new negotiations. you're wasting a lot of valuable time. i am also disappointed by the informal consultations, in which only those countries which have already ratified the constitution are welcome. i can assure you that this counterproductive approach will not make public opinion in the netherlands and france any more enthusiastic about europe. i hope you agree with me that it is precisely public opinion in these two countries that should not be given an extra dose of euroscepticism. i would like to ask you, as president-in-office of the council, whether this type of consultation is part of your strategy for the presidency. how can you be a good president of the whole council and at the same time participate in closed consultations which exclude member states? is it not particularly important at this time to consult france, the netherlands and the countries which have not yet ratified it collectively, i.e. within the council's regular assembly? council president, i wish you god's strength and god's blessing for your work.
president, thank you very much, mrs merkel, for your impressive speech. we agree with the priorities you have set for foreign policy. stability in the balkans depends on a european perspective, you said. we are in full agreement with that and it is good that you have reaffirmed that here on behalf of the council. the most urgent is, of course, the question of kosovo. you made a comment about that. some form of independence for kosovo seems inevitable. the question now is what the european union can do to make such a solution acceptable to serbia and, in a certain sense, to russia, which has declared that it will not accept an agreement in the security council that is not negotiated with serbia. i hope that the presidency can make a contribution in the form of an active dialogue with serbia after the elections next week or at the end of this week with the new government there, but also using the good relations with moscow to seek a solution acceptable to all. in any case, we reject the comparison sometimes made by moscow: the european union is also committed to the development of the kosovo issue and the conflicts in other parts of europe. that brings me to my second point. you made a number of remarks on relations with russia. we agree with the search for a balanced approach which should address economic relations, energy, human rights, but also the situation in russia itself and the question of a number of conflicts in the shared neighbourhood. i hope that you will be able to work on these three issues in a balanced way in the coming months, also in the context of the negotiations on a new partnership agreement. i think that the european union should now make an effort to help find solutions to the transnistrian problem, and then we are talking about moldova, but also about the problems in georgia. these are three key elements, and i hope that you will be able to balance these three priorities in the relationship with russia in the coming months.

president. - before the european union was established, we had a good cooperation within europe. there were long-distance cross-border train services, people could buy tickets directly in their own country to distant destinations, and an international railway book was available in every country. that good situation is threatened. the european rail network is increasingly falling apart, following the example of america. the only remaining networks will be urban networks and high-speed lines between the main cities. on 27 and 28 september 2005, at the first reading, this parliament missed a historic opportunity to maintain and strengthen good european coordination and a high level of provision. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. amendment no 37 on passenger rights replaced the right of rail passengers to buy integrated tickets for the whole rail network of the european union with a call for voluntary cooperation. this gives railway companies complete freedom to stop selling tickets and information for long-distance destinations. passenger-friendly offers for travel in and out of the country are increasingly being restricted. last year, three large member states abolished their eurodomino cards, which gave residents of other states better access to their railway networks. interrail cards for travelling around europe, which many young people in particular used to get to know other countries in europe, are increasingly less accessible to high-speed trains over long distances. the european railways are no longer a visible entity to the public. the european railway industry is now being dismantled in the absence of state monopolies and is being divided into a number of companies competing with each other in the same territory. this is the reason why the european union has been able to take a number of important decisions in the past. the european union will not do anything about this as long as it continues to believe that railway undertakings should be managed as commercial companies under article 5 of directive 91/4401 and should therefore regard cost reduction as their priority. that's how we chase the train passengers over the medium distance to the plane. the more public transport is dependent on the market, the less likely it is to survive. not only the rise of the car and the wide availability of motorways, but also the tax exemption for jet fuel and the rise of price-fighters in aviation, lead to rail transport being perceived as proportionately expensive. to survive, it must be made attractive to younger generations. if this generation takes the car and the airplane for granted and views the train as an inaccessible museum piece, the result will be that the railways will only become more loss-making and will have to cut their costs even further. those who expect the railways to be strengthened by abandoning the same competitive mechanisms as air and road transport will be deceived. this development is bad for the passengers, bad for the railway staff and bad for the protection of our environment. i am aware that mr jarzembowski has been fighting for many years for more markets and for more competitive companies to be admitted to the railway more quickly. not only in cross-border freight traffic, but also in domestic passenger traffic. if he does not get his way in the first railway package in 2001, he will fight to get his way in the third package. my group has never supported him in that. my group proposes in amendment no 35 not to follow the jarzembowski report on liberalisation and, if it is adopted, at least to allow the member states to restrict competition. that is possible by adopting the like-sounding amendments nos 33, 34 and 36. as regards the sterckx report, in our amendments nos 70 to 73 we support the wishes of the european consumer organisation. finally, mr president, the savary report alone, which makes it easier for train drivers to cross borders and work under different technical conditions, is a valuable contribution to the integration of european railways.
mr president, the last few months have shown that there is a very different opinion on the third railway package. in any case, this applies to the council and this house. i can understand the council's reluctance, given the differences between member states and the distribution of powers within europe. i can relate to that, for the most part. that does not mean that the council has not chosen the best solution for the future in all respects. the reports by mr sterckx and mr savary contribute to this. that is not the case with mr jarzembowski's report. the balance between future market opening and the existing situation which the council had found in my view is broken in this report. the amendments in this report lead to a situation where all passenger transport services can be provided simultaneously in different ways, albeit in stages. this may result in cherry-picking of profitable lines at the expense of less profitable ones, lower quality of service and potential safety risks. by allowing member states to choose between different competition models, the disadvantages mentioned above can be avoided. i have tabled two amendments to that effect on behalf of my group. in addition, it is important to respect existing agreements on rail services with limited duration. that is why i do not think amendment no 18 is desirable. i hope, mr president, that we will reach a good decision tomorrow, but i am not sure that we will.
for the time being, a large majority of this house seems to want to stick to the opening up of a railway network for both crossborder and national transport, and frankly that is not surprising. so far, further market operation in public transport has shown us that this produces 25% more public transport for the same money. i do think that amendment no 37 is very important in this context. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the railway companies are able to compete with each other. the so-called concession model is an important form of market operation which can simultaneously focus on safety, efficiency, a coherent timetable and sound financial management. i would therefore ask the rapporteur, following the example of the shadow rapporteur, my colleague mr ortuondo larrea, to support amendment no 37 in its entirety. finally, a few words on amendment no 18. unlike the rapporteur, i believe that contracts for international passenger services which have already been awarded under an open commercial and fair procedure, and therefore only in those cases, mr rapporteur, should be respected. frankly, i think this is simply a matter of good governance. if existing contracts with private parties have to be broken, member states could face a claim for damages of hundreds of millions of euros and that is frankly not exactly the message i would like to send to the european public as a parliament. the alde group will therefore reject amendment no 18 and i would ask the rapporteur to do the same.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of road users is not compromised. this is not just about roadblocks or blocking cars from residential areas and strengthening public transport. densely populated areas with many pedestrians and cyclists have different requirements for car use than sparsely populated areas with long quiet roads. vehicle visibility is different in areas with lower sunlight than in areas with high sunlight. this means that, for example, any requirement for cars to have their lights on during the day is good for road safety in one area and bad in another. for other matters, however, it is best to seek a uniform regulation at european level, such as unambiguous road signs, blind spots, guardrails which do not pose any additional danger to motorists, or protection of drivers against fatigue due to excessive driving time. i regret that the rapporteur has withdrawn the proposals on mobile phones at the wheel, road safety education and the danger of snow on car roofs. otherwise it is a good and complete report which my group supports.
i am glad, mr president, that we can vote this week in strasbourg on the first five-year review of the cotonou agreement, which we concluded in 2000 for a period of 20 years. this agreement covers 78 countries in africa, the caribbean and the pacific and covers some 750 million people. it is a partnership agreement on aid and trade and serves as a model for the eu's sustainable relations with its development partners. europe as a soft power in conflict prevention, good governance and the fight against corruption; together with the eu member states we can make a difference in the mdgs. almost eur 24 billion is available from 1 january 2008 for a period of six years. this is half of the total eu development budget. it is therefore good that parliament exercises control over the spending of the european development fund, but unfortunately, commissioner, this edf budget is not in the eu budget. that is what you would have wanted as a commission, and that is what we would have wanted as a parliament. if the role of the european parliament is to be meaningful, you will have to come to concrete agreements with us, together with the council - and unfortunately the council is not here - as we have done with the dci. let us shape the national policy documents of the acp countries, which include almost the whole of our african policy, in the same way as we have done in the development cooperation instrument. this means that parliament is informed in good time so that it can give its input to national policy documents. that means a clear and transparent process and an open dialogue between the council, the commission and parliament. this means input and control for parliament in both programming, implementation and evaluation of the country policy documents. that means that the representatives of the people are not distant here, but neither are the parliaments in those countries, nor the society there. look, ownership is wonderful, but it's not just the finance minister in such a country, it's also the parliament, the government, the society. if we do that, we get opinions from the people involved that are taken seriously, before the policy is even signed and agreed. we want to get rid of the situation where we are all talking about the mdgs, but africa is falling behind in the mdgs, and it turns out that we are actually just filling in the gaps in the road, however useful that may be. we also want to put the mdgs at the heart of the acp countries. give our parliament democratic influence over the edf, which is half the total eu development budget. that is what you as commissioner and the council are getting out of this. support democracy in europe. i ask you what steps the commission and the council are going to take to achieve this.

in writing. while the cuts continue and both regional and international rail services continue to be reduced, all political forces are now calling for more passenger and freight transport to be taken over by rail. their recipe is that of road freight and airline price-fighters. they decouple rail management from service delivery, and hope that private enterprises will lead to lower prices, customer-friendly behaviour and attract more customers. i think that approach will not ultimately achieve what is expected of it today. the focus will be on cost reduction, by closing lines, thinning services, raising fares and cutting out the many loss-making customers. the liberalization plans of mr jarzembowski are not sufficiently compensated by the defeated savary and sterckx reports. the savary report is urgently needed to reduce the technical problems with crossborder trains which have arisen since the electrification. the sterckx report throws out the possibility of forcing railway companies to make crossborder services more accessible and to maintain them. only for people with disabilities does it mean an improvement.

in writing. the vast problem of people who today are still considered unchaste and thus 'untouchable' is distressing. the social segregation that dalits are subjected to is similar to the former apartheid in south africa. the largest group of these "untouchables" live in india, over 160 million people. they are often excluded from basic services such as education, health care and clean drinking water, are denied ownership of land and are regularly subjected to violence and exploitation. i therefore vote in favour of this resolution, which urges the indian government to do more to effectively eliminate caste discrimination and promote equal opportunities. it is good that the european parliament is now making concrete proposals to do something about this structural discrimination against a population group. as the prime minister of india himself said on december 27, 2006: "dalits have faced a unique discrimination in our society that is fundamentally different from the problems of minority groups in general. the only parallel to the practice of 'untouchability' was apartheid in south africa". i hope that the eu will discuss this in its relations with india and work to eradicate this grave social evil.

member of the commission. madam president, first of all i would like to thank the european parliament and in particular the rapporteurs, mrs jackson and mr blokland, for their excellent reports, and the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. i also thank mrs gutiérrez and the committee on industry, research and energy for the opinion they delivered at first reading. waste policies play a fundamental role in reducing the environmental impact of the use of natural resources. the aim of the thematic strategy and the draft directive is, in principle, to prevent the generation of waste and, to the extent that this cannot be avoided, to minimise its impact on the environment and health. over the past thirty years the european union has developed a significant waste policy and legislation to protect public health and the environment. the commission's proposal to revise the waste directive seeks to adapt the legislative framework to today's environmental challenges. the modernisation of this policy takes account of the progress made in the field of eco-innovation and builds on our latest knowledge on the sustainable use of natural resources. the proposed package of measures aims at:- firstly, reducing the environmental impact of waste throughout its life cycle. - secondly, to make waste prevention a priority. this requires action at national, regional and local levels. member states should implement waste prevention policies through mandatory national programmes. this is expected to help reverse the current trend of increasing waste volumes. national targets will also help to reduce waste at community level. - thirdly, the promotion of recycling by setting standards to ensure the quality of recycled materials. this will create confidence in recycled materials and increase their demand within the internal market. we propose that this be done through the criteria for declassifying waste. - fourthly, the proposed measures also aim at modernising and simplifying waste legislation. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement. the aim of the strategy and the directive is to achieve a balance. maintain what has been proven to work over the last 30 years, while modernising the legislative framework for the coming decades. in conclusion, i would like to make two important remarks on the waste framework directive: - first, the text to be drawn up by the codecision procedure must be clear and provide legal certainty. some of the amendments tabled do not provide this legal clarity, particularly those concerning the distinction between recovery and disposal. - secondly, in order to be environmentally effective, this legislative act must retain its character as a framework directive. in other words, it must be a stable and clear legal framework which, at the same time, provides sufficient flexibility to adapt to the changing needs of the times.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the waste management strategy is implemented. i do not want to do this without first thanking the members for the good cooperation and the unanimous support just mentioned. you'd think waste would not be a controversial topic in this house. the resolution adopted by the committee on the environment aims to give a clear direction to future waste policy, which is primarily aimed at protecting the environment and public health. the committee on the environment has a broad consensus on the principles of the precautionary principle, the polluter pays principle, self-sufficiency, proximity and producer responsibility. i am sorry to say, however, that the drafting of the report deviates from the principles mentioned. let me give you an example. there is broad agreement on the five-step waste hierarchy, so prevention first, then reuse, then recycling, then useful use, and finally disposal. this waste hierarchy is reflected in both mr jackson's report and my report. however, amendments have been tabled which mean that the waste hierarchy can be used in such a flexible way that it can also be completely avoided. i would urge that we stick to paragraph 16 of the resolution, which states that the only way to deviate from the waste hierarchy is if a certified life cycle analysis clearly demonstrates that this is more environmentally friendly. another issue is that of changing definitions. the change of definitions is mainly intended to clarify and to provide a service to the environment. the change of definitions is not intended to make it easier to achieve objectives, to improve the image or to conquer the market. i would like to mention two important examples of this, namely by-products and the distinction between useful use and disposal. first of all, the by-products. paragraph 10 of the resolution states that we must be very careful about declassifying waste. when something is no longer waste, it is no longer covered by the waste protection legislation. then the waste can be transported anywhere. the creation of a new category of so-called by-products therefore poses an additional danger. it is not clear what regime this falls under and the use and misuse of this category will increase enormously. second point: disposal versus useful use. there has been a debate for years about the difference between recycling and disposal. the debate focuses on the question of when incineration of mainly household waste can be called recovery. the aim has always been to make a clear distinction. paragraphs 12 to 14 of the resolution contain the opinion of the committee on the environment. waste destined for energy recovery or incineration must in any case comply with the emission standards of the waste incineration directive. the committee also believes that the same environmental standards should apply to both incineration and co-incineration of waste, i.e. the same emission standards and the same criteria for energy efficiency. and i have also come to the conclusion that it is better to think of incineration as disposal. the objective of incineration is always disposal. this provides the necessary clarity and is in line with the judgments of the european court of justice. the final paragraph, as formulated in paragraph 31 of the resolution, is a ban on the dumping of combustible waste and waste that can be reused and recycled. finally, i would ask the european commission to take note of paragraph 30 of the resolution. the commission has already proposed a number of amendments to the directive on the disposal of waste. this has been in the sixth environmental action programme since 2002 and it is high time that the european commission launched these proposals. in this way we can come to a well-regulated recycling society.
mr president, commissioner, rapporteurs, waste is a growing problem in europe and beyond. we are in danger of slowly drowning in our own waste. it is therefore high time that we took strong action before the waste problem literally overwhelmed us. it is important to remember that waste has become a commercial product, and we see the negative consequences of this. one third of the most lucrative waste shipments are illegal, physical checks show. so we need a new strong policy, but we must also use the right methods. reducing, reusing, recycling waste, these are good sustainable directions. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire is a very good one. a good waste policy should solve problems and certainly not create new ones.
i have just explained my report on the waste strategy on behalf of the committee on the environment and i pointed out that it is important to keep the principles of the resolution in mind when revising the waste framework directive. this means that we don't have to start with a new confusing concept called by-product. this also means that we should not take incineration of waste too lightly as a useful application. i do not want to see all kinds of incineration plants being closed down immediately as a result of amendment no 57. i do think, however, that the idea of amendment no 57, namely to include energy efficiency criteria in a permit, can be very good for both the environment and the climate. i therefore call for support for the idea so that it can be further developed and improved. finally, i wholeheartedly support the proposals for landfill bans, for reusable and recyclable waste, and the proposals to promote the composting of organic waste.
president. - i thank the two rapporteurs. they've both done a really good job on this complicated case. our waste mountain is still growing, and it is certainly the wrong direction, because we must move towards a world without waste, a world where all discarded products and packaging materials are the raw materials for new products, where raw materials do not lose their value and where recycling is not downcycling. the new framework directive is a step in the right direction. the waste hierarchy says that we're going to start by preventing waste, and if we still generate waste, then we're going to focus on reuse, and then recycling. today, 34% of waste is recycled and this proportion can and must increase. i think there are two things that are important. first of all, the responsibility of producers. they must be given greater responsibility for the prevention and treatment of waste. they will have to focus on reuse and recycling. this producer responsibility needs to be further elaborated and may take the form, for example, of a take-back obligation. second, a world without waste is unfortunately still a long way off. the waste that is generated despite recycling and prevention must be used as efficiently as possible. landfilling is certainly not efficient, but unfortunately 50% of the waste in the european union is still landfilled. this has got to stop. in any case, incineration may be more efficient than dumping. modern waste incineration plants can use waste to make useful use of it by extracting energy from it. so the choice is not recycling or incineration. we need to maximize recycling. but in the coming years, it can't be 100% recycled, so the choice is between dumping or incineration. and then it's better to go for combustion with energy recovery. and that has to be done very efficiently, so we can at least save a lot of co2.
the european parliament today adopted the waste directive, which requires the amount of waste to be stabilised between 2008 and 2012 and the european waste production to be significantly reduced by 2020. i agree with the conclusions, but i also urge the european parliament itself to set the right example. all citizens have a responsibility here. the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european parliament is not excluded from the european union. firstly, it would be a great step forward if we reduced the number of working languages from 23 to 2 or 3, because despite all the technologies we use, the european parliament still produces large quantities of paper, 99 per cent of which is destined for the waste basket. secondly, there is now all the more reason to designate brussels as the seat of the european parliament. the monthly relocation of parliamentarians and staff to strasbourg is not only a waste of money, it also creates co2 emissions. thirdly, we must replace the staff cars for the european parliament. now the group chairmen are driving mercedes and audi's. however, there are many more fuel-efficient cars. i propose we get rid of these polluting cars and replace them with their fuel-efficient hybrid brothers. i hope that this will also be an incentive for german car manufacturers to comply with european standards.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to support the development of the common agricultural policy. i cannot think of a better valentine's day present for women in turkey than the adoption of the report on women's rights tomorrow. there are 40 million women in turkey. they want the chance to develop and create a better situation for themselves. my report alone will not achieve that, but it is a means for the women and the women's movement in turkey to claim their rights vis-à-vis husbands and fathers, the turkish government and the european union. my report builds on the findings of the past year. i've been looking at what's been done and what hasn't been done. the legislation is now largely in order, although there is still work to be done in this area as well. the second step is to create a framework in which the legislation can be implemented, i.e. sufficient funds and a well-trained public administration. a government that is committed and shows it publicly. only when the second step has been taken can the third step be taken properly. this means that women also experience an improvement in their lives. so we're not there yet. the second step is still being worked on and women are therefore still seeing too little of the improvements in practice. the government's commitment is there. two years ago, women's rights weren't really an issue. now prime minister erdogan opens almost every speech with it. the various ministries are also paying more attention to women's rights. our report last year contributed to this and we as a parliament can be quite proud of that. there are also better government instructions on how civil servants and police should deal with women's rights. police and military are trained and circulars give clear instructions to ministries and municipalities. these instructions focus on honour killing and violence against women and that is very much needed. the number of convictions for honour killing is another mixed picture. some judges do punish more severely, others still use reduced sentences. it has to stop. there are other things to be worked on in the report. the fight against violence against women must be the number one priority in public policy for women. we need more shelters. i intend to visit them next year to see if these shelters also meet the quality standards. a committee on women's rights and gender equality is due to be established in the turkish parliament later this year. such a committee is indispensable if legislation is to become truly gender-friendly and is also an important and necessary interlocutor for the european parliament and the parliamentary assembly of the council of europe. more objective and accurate data on the situation of women in turkey, in particular on violence, illiteracy and discrimination, is needed. fortunately, turkey and the european commission are currently working together on this. finally, a strong anti-discrimination policy is needed to give women full access to education and the labour market. i would like to say a few words about one compromise amendment tabled jointly by the ppe, pse, alde and gue. i'm sure you've received many letters about it. the use of the words 'kurdish minority' in the report is a matter of concern. the kurds themselves and the turkish government have indicated that they have difficulty with these terms. to avoid confusion, we have now chosen to say that all women, regardless of race, regardless of language, regardless of origin, religion or political belief, should have access to women's rights programmes. i therefore call on you to support the compromise amendment. ladies and gentlemen, 2007 will be an important test for turkey to see how seriously this country takes the emancipation of women. there are parliamentary elections in november. there are far too few women participating in politics at the moment. it's not like they're not there. i have spoken to women from almost all political parties two weeks ago, but they are not on the electoral rolls yet. how turkey handles this is up to turkey. but if women's political participation does not increase, turkey cannot say that it did not know what to do. the report makes suggestions including temporary positive discrimination measures. temporarily, because i am convinced that once women become more visible in politics, this will have a snowball effect. girls need role models so they can see that a career in politics is also for them. i have hope for the women in turkey. turkey is on the right track, but it should not now turn back. that is why i call for reforms to be accelerated and also for them to be implemented. the accession negotiations with the eu can act as a catalyst. i hope that our report will also contribute something. i want to thank you all in advance for your support. i'm sure the women in turkey will be grateful to you, too.
deputy author mr president, commissioner, i am here to ask questions on behalf of martine roure, said el khadraoui, edith mastenbroek and willi piecyk. i'm just filling in for edith mastenbroek who couldn't be here tonight. the council has adopted commission regulation (ec) no 1448/2006 on aviation security, which contains a secret annex with restrictive measures on liquids that passengers are allowed to carry on aircraft in their hand luggage. can the commission explain and illustrate, firstly, how the secrecy of the annex can be brought into line with the democratic principle of publicity of the law? what sanctions are applied in the case of refusal to follow or of breach of the rules? how is it intended to avoid arbitrariness in the practical implementation of a secret rule and its exceptions? secondly, does this text provide for the passengers' right to information and right of appeal? how can airline passengers challenge decisions and sanctions taken on the basis of the new hand luggage rules on the spot or at a later stage and look for legal remedy? thirdly, what is the justification for these measures? did the commission carry out an evaluation of the information and evidence presented by the united kingdom's secret services when requesting these restrictions? was specialized expertise gathered before the decision was made? are the measures based on proper risk assessment and will they yield an appropriate reduction of security risks? will they be evaluated after implementation? fourthly, have these measures been subject to an impact assessment on fundamental rights? have they been deemed compatible with citizens' rights, the free movement of persons and goods? on what grounds are these measures to be considered proportionate and necessary in a democratic society in compliance with the european convention on human rights? have these measures been subject to an impact assessment on consumer rights and the practical impact on airport operations and passenger flows? was there any prior assessment of the extra costs consumers and airports will face after the implementation of the new rules? fifthly, the aviation security implementing rules are developed by the aviation security regulatory committee, established by commission regulation (ec) no 2320/2002. this committee only gets external advice from the stakeholder advisory group on aviation security. it is an informal body of aviation industry stakeholders. would the commission agree that this body needs to be formalised as requested by parliament during the first reading of the ongoing revision of regulation (ec) no 2320/2002? would the commission accept the idea that this committee also needs to systematically take into account appropriate expertise on civil rights and consumer interests? last but not least, if i may add one personal question, commissioner: what happened to my almost-new shaving gel that was confiscated at brussels zaventem airport?
on behalf of the alde group. - mr president, a secret and meaningless regulation with a secret annex has meant that since 6 november 2006 the liquids can only be carried in very limited quantities in hand luggage. air passengers who are dependent on medication may carry it on board the aircraft as necessary. these passengers must carry proof of this and if, on arrival, it turns out that the luggage has not arrived yet again and you cannot therefore have the rest of the medicines you need, then you are out of luck. you'll be on heavy medication. there is no indication of where passengers can possibly lodge a complaint or appeal in the event of misconduct by, for example, security staff. there is no single interpretation in the 27 member states at present. if mozzarella is considered suspect in member state a, there is no problem in member state b. and so, commissioner, that confusion of the passenger you just mentioned, it still exists despite the fact that there are european regulations. clearly, the measures are far from watertight. i try again and again at random airports and still manage to get a bottle of about 200 ml through security. democratic societies are open societies. and that openness makes them attractive and successful, but unfortunately also vulnerable. the tendency to reduce vulnerability to terrorist attacks by imposing more rules on society and allowing less privacy here and there seems understandable to many people in the first instance. but where's the line? the european union is not going to be able to do anything about this. hundreds of thousands of people have been killed, two million people have been displaced, looting, murder, rape are part of daily life in darfur. it's almost impossible to truly comprehend the extent of that suffering. in the meantime, despite all the fine agreements, despite all the agreements, human rights violations are getting out of hand. in recent months, the situation has reached a low point for both the population and the aid workers. despite the agreement reached at the beginning of january, large parts of north darfur have recently been deliberately bombed by the sudanese air force, as the commissioner has also said. even aid workers are no longer safe from violence. aid agencies are increasingly feeling the impact of this violence. last month 15 un agencies, including unicef, reported that they could no longer continue to provide adequate aid to the people of darfur. two weeks ago, a first aid organisation (médecins du monde) withdrew, and oxfam and care international are threatening to do the same, while three and a half million darfurians are dependent on humanitarian aid. the council: not present. how long are we going to let this untenable situation continue? are you prepared to do everything possible to put an end to impunity and finally enforce and implement the sanctions agreed in the security council? are you prepared to enforce the presence of the hybrid peacekeeping force of african union soldiers and blue helmets? finally, are you prepared to impose economic sanctions, including an oil embargo? we did not intervene when the genocide in rwanda was taking place and we watched as the men in srebenica met their end, that while the un was created in '45 to enforce our promise of 'never again'. we can't leave the powerless to their fate. i hope that europe will make a difference. i am speaking in particular to the council, which is not here, and to the commissioner: take action!

member of the commission. two weeks ago the european year of equal opportunities for all was launched. equal opportunities and the right to live a life free from discrimination are core values on policy-making or cultural life. the commission is therefore committed to tackling discrimination and exclusion of the roma minority with all the instruments at its disposal. i would like to mention two in particular: firstly, the implementation of the 2000 directive on protection against discrimination on racial or ethnic grounds. the commission wants to ensure that it is incorporated into legislation and correctly applied in all member states. secondly, i would like to mention the use of financial instruments, in particular the structural funds and the european social fund in particular. the commission has asked member states to take account of the roma inclusion objective when drawing up their operational plans for the 2007-2013 funding period. a great effort is needed to raise awareness among the roma population, and the general public, of the right to non-discrimination and to appreciate the benefits of diversity. in this respect, the european year of equal opportunities for all and the ongoing information campaign for diversity and against discrimination are, in our view, particularly useful tools. the commission welcomes the achievements to date of the decade of roma inclusion 2005-2015, which currently involves nine governments from central and eastern european and western balkan countries, including five member states of the european union. for european policy on the inclusion of ethnic minorities, including the roma population. finally, madam president, i would like to point out that the group of commissioners on fundamental rights will be discussing all these issues at its meeting scheduled for july this year.
on behalf of the verts/ale group . – madam president, i will start with a question. when will the eu finally take seriously the situation of its largest minority? fifteen million people in europe remain excluded from basic human rights, such as the right to education, employment or physical integrity, merely because they are roma, or gypsies as most people still call them. one month ago two more countries joined the eu. the treatment given to roma in these countries is far from fulfilling eu norms. as we know from the latest report of the european monitoring centre on racism and xenophobia, the situation is not better in many of the old member states. if we talk about the migration of roma, most of us call to mind pictures of poor, hunger-ridden people who come to disturb us in our comfortable existence, rather than remembering that they have rights. in 1990, under the finnish presidency, the eu adopted a recommendation to the member states that specific attention should be given to the situation of minorities, in particular those who have no home country, meaning the roma. the commission has spent tens of thousands of euros for the improvement of the situation of roma without developing a clear strategy and targeted approach, following the model of other intergovernmental organisations. under these circumstances migration often becomes the last resort for roma.

author. - mr president, it is not a good idea for china to see tibet as an integral part of china and for the outside world to take that for granted for a long time. the tibetans represent their own culture in inhospitable habitat, isolated and high in the mountains. china has long been tempted to see this area as a reserve that can accommodate a part of the very large chinese population. this is a problem not only for the people of tibet but also for those of the adjacent and more accessible xinjiang, where the uyghurs, who are related to the turkic-speaking peoples such as the kazakhs, kyrgyz and uzbeks, live. they fear becoming a minority in their own country and losing everything they held dear. the traditional living conditions of tibetans and uyghurs should not be idealized. there is a need for radical innovation, but not for innovation imposed from outside and serving primarily outside interests. because china is the most populous country in the world and a rising economic power, it is highly unlikely that tibet will have the chance to develop into an independent state. this makes it all the more important that, half a century after the great conflict, there should be a reconciliation in which the tibetans in china can retain the parts of their identity which are most precious to them and are no longer in danger of becoming victims of state violence. the ideology of progress that has dominated china for nearly 60 years is now focused primarily on economic growth. it has its drawbacks, but it does offer more room for tolerance and recognition of diversity than it has for a long time. dialogue or cultural identity and self-government within china must be able to produce results. my group supports everything that can contribute to that dialogue and to peaceful solutions.
when france agreed in 1958 to self-determination for its west african colonies, guinea was the only country to opt for immediate and complete independence. in the other eleven colonial-controlled territories, the elite, often outside their own people, chose to continue a close administrative, military and economic link with france. guinea, on the other hand, took its cue from the former british colony of ghana, which had gained independence a year earlier, and which wanted to build an african model of socialism with the strong participation of its own people. leaders sékou touré and kwame n'krumah inspired many africans in their struggle for a better future, where equality and development would replace colonialism and poverty. unfortunately, in both countries, a military coup has put an end to this development. guinea has been ruled by the dictator conté since 1984. the elections organised under his control were boycotted by most parties and the official opposition, which was represented in parliament, was forced to leave parliament. the cntg and ustg are now the main force in the struggle for democracy. the presidential security forces, led by the dictator's son, responded to their demonstration on 22 january by killing 59 people and wounding 150 others. the dictator is increasingly treating the land as his private property. like many other dictatorial countries, guinea is the victim of the struggle for mineral resources between groups who want to appropriate the proceeds. in this case, the natural resources are gold, iron and bauxite. my group supports the resolution, which takes up the demands of the democratic opposition.
president. - i am beginning to get a little tired. i have the impression that we have had this debate a hundred times and that the same arguments are being put forward every time. we've been doing that for years. we do not differ on the importance of social services of general interest. we don't have to discuss that. but i do not share the automatic conclusion that because it is labelled 'social' the market rules should not apply. we are all in favour of subsidiarity, but what does subsidiarity mean? does this mean that countries or local authorities - as we understand it - can decide for themselves how they organise their services, or does it mean - as some people think - that the rules of the market, the rules of fair competition, should not apply? mr president, i am not in favour of legislation where it is not needed. we must identify where the problems are and tackle them. with legislation if necessary. but there is no need for a kind of general call, which i have heard in this parliament for years now, for a legislative framework and a sectoral directive. let us first provide legal clarity, and i refer to the contribution of the committee on legal affairs, which i fully endorse. another thing is that many people think that when the label social is on it, it is always about small services that cannot provide themselves. of course, we should support such services. the treaty does allow that. it is also a question of social housing, for example. it's a billion-dollar business. it may be social, with a social purpose, but it is a lot of money and a lot of interests at stake. then it's about corporations, unions, employers, politicians and so on. the only thing i have not heard about so far is the interest of the citizen as a consumer, as a customer of these services. because that's got to be my priority. competition has in many cases led to better services and better rates, and where necessary, the government can always step in or protect. that is what the treaty says and i see no reason to make a general adjustment now.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. mr president, i am speaking here as the shadow rapporteur on behalf of the alde group in the committee on legal affairs. we broadly support the levai report as it has been adopted. we have a few things to add, because the recommendation is aimed at breaking up the monopolies of management companies and making them more transparent through the controls that should be exercised over them. the compromise amendments are flawed and in some places unclear or inconsistent and even limit the scope of their application. our amendments, which we are voting on tomorrow, are in addition to the report that has been adopted and are designed to make it easier to break up monopolies, to make the market more transparent and yet to ensure that it is workable and that authors and rightholders are entitled to their rights and can recover their royalties. in amendment no 5 we ask the member states to open a public tender for the licences every few years in order to put pressure on the monopolies of the management companies. these monopolies are not a problem in themselves, but at the moment there is no control. a study by the dutch competition authority also shows that there are different starting points in each country. as they are monopoly exposures, there is no control which prevents the identification of exorbitant fees and public expenditure. still, this should be possible. i hope that the amendments we have tabled will succeed. we are also proposing amendments to broaden the scope of the directive. because broadcasting, for example, when we talk about broadcasting over the internet, ipods and so on, falls under copy broadcasting. that's now being restricted. we want to bring it back in order to get as close as possible to the original commission proposal. we now feel that too much attention has been paid to the management companies and too little to the rights holders and authors.
the commissioner said that the idea of crossborder patient mobility was a good idea. patient mobility, however, is something entirely different from commercial healthcare mobility. too often the two are confused. the debate on patient mobility must in no way lead to the undermining of national health systems or to the shifting of responsibilities by member states to ensure good quality and quantity of healthcare in the member states. nor should it lead to the liberalisation of the health market or force patients to seek care outside their own country. patient mobility is a right of the patient and should certainly not be used as an excuse for applying the services directive to health services. this parliament has spoken very clearly against this and would be making itself untrustworthy if it now took a different position.
this morning, dutch newspapers reported that iss, the company we are talking about here, is advising its customers to block the upcoming shareholders' meeting of dsm, a large dutch multinational, from distributing an additional loyalty dividend to shareholders who hold their shares in that company for three years or more. i think that dsm's plan is a great idea to encourage responsible shareholder. the extra dividend may do that, but so can a higher voting weight. that could be another legitimate form. i have therefore always opposed the insistence on the so-called shareholder democracy of the one share, one vote principle. this was first raised in the debate on the takeover directive. the original intention of the 13th directive was not to establish the celestial kingdom of shareholder control on earth, but simply to protect minority shareholders and to give them the right to a fair sale. but a number of fanatical stakeholders have made that one share, one vote idea an absolute, and the biggest advocate is iss. not only for ideological reasons, but also because their business is simply to collect voting rights and provide proxy services to shareholders. with all due respect to the commission's statement, i find it most remarkable that you should put such an investigation in the hands of a party so clearly biased and interested in the matter. i also briefed myself on the conduct of the investigation. i've seen the questionnaires that are going around right now with investment firms on the one hand and corporations on the other. the companies are only asked whether they use certain cems (control enhancing mechanisms) such as pyramid structures, certification, gold shares, etc. however, the approach to investors is very different. they are asked a number of very suggestive questions about their views on all these bad measures. it also allows me to predict the outcome of that investigation. in short, i have already had serious reservations about the outcome of the study and i do not think it can be a good basis for further conclusions and for intentions such as the drafting of a recommendation. let us first examine the reasons why companies have such cems and why, in the present age of hedge fund shareholder activism, there is a renewed need for protection against short-term investor activism. a fundamental debate with this parliament is necessary before the commission intends to develop any measure or any further activity.
mr swoboda, mr andersson and mr guy-quint have called for more initiatives on the subject of a social europe. that is what they lack in these proposals. i would like to continue along the same lines: europe must not only be socially inward looking but also socially outward looking. europe as a global partner dan points out that some of the themes covered in the 2008 work programme lack the development element and the link with development cooperation. i mention agriculture, the revision of the european framework legislation for pharmaceutical companies and maritime policy. we have set the general objective in the european consensus on development, also for this year, to reduce poverty in africa and to achieve the millennium development goals. the fact is that the millennium development goals for africa are going in the wrong direction. what is the commission doing now? president. - the nuclear aspirations of pyongyang and tehran are a direct threat to the non-proliferation treaty, the so-called npt. in fact, both in northeast asia and in the middle east, the respective neighbouring countries may feel compelled to join the nuclear powers in the same way. in addition, if the npt is eroded, the same negative effect is to be feared for the conventions on biological and chemical weapons. in short, the entire multilateral arms control system is in danger of collapsing - a true global nightmare. meanwhile, the question arises as to what authority the united nations security council still has when un members arm themselves with nuclear weapons against its declared will. the council and the commission share my view that the crisis of the npt is also a crisis for the highest international authority. in view of this very serious situation, i expect the council and the commission to make every effort to persuade the un security council to reverse the sinister nuclear course of pyongyang and tehran. after all, time is of the essence.
the non-proliferation treaty is, in fact, the main pillar of the international consensus that the proliferation of nuclear weapons must be prevented, with the ultimate aim of course, as my group chairman has just emphasized, of general nuclear disarmament. the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction poses a growing threat to international peace and security, and we must re-evaluate, revitalize and strengthen the treaty. we are also very concerned about developments in iran. despite repeated warnings from the international community, iran continues its efforts to develop a system of uranium enrichment. at the same time, we also know that there is a real danger that terrorist groups will have access to nuclear weapons or similar weapons. to counter the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction, an effective multilateral approach is essential. in fact, the non-proliferation treaty stands or falls with it. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. they are also responsible in particular for the credibility of the non-proliferation treaty as we know it today. it is precisely in this light that we question the recent request by the united states to poland and the czech republic to station elements of a missile shield on their territory. the missile defense system, according to the americans, should provide protection against possible attacks from north korea and iran. this is in fact contrary to the intention to prevent those countries from developing a nuclear arsenal. the americans' proposal also ignores the fear of russia, which - rightly or wrongly - sees the missile shield as a provocation and even a threat to its internal security. the protective shield which then leads to mistrust between the three main partners - the us, russia and the eu - who want to prevent proliferation is not the multilateral action we want. we also wonder how such bilateral cooperation fits into the european security strategy and the nato partnership. we therefore have serious reservations about the way things are going and we call on the united states and the eu member states concerned to reconsider the plans and to look for multilateral alternatives that are consistent with what we have agreed in the european union on security.
commissioner, welcome and thank you for your forward-looking approach to this proposal. we agree with the coordinators in the committee on the internal market, mrs gebhardt and mr harbour, that the internal market must be attractive and that people must be at the centre of it. as a market liberal, i think the market is made up of supply and demand. who's the question? that's the consumer. i am afraid, however, that in the past we have placed too much emphasis on the paternalistic approach, on protecting too much the consumer who is transported with information and who is blinded by the trees to the forest. your style, the new wind you are blowing, suits me well because you want to put a little less emphasis on that. i hope that the action plan will not include additional restrictions and additional rules, but i am confident that it will not. i have a question for you. are you also going to look at the existing legislation to see if it can be simplified so that the single market is attractive and remains so in the future, especially for consumers? because information is fine, freedom of choice is fine, but we shouldn't overwhelm people with information and we should treat them as adults.
the fact that the european council has decided to hold a spring break in the winter indicates that the council is recognising climate change. i am glad that the council is taking up the commission's proposals on combating climate change. but this means that the council is only prepared to reduce by 30% if the other developed countries join in this effort. however, a 30% reduction in global greenhouse gas emissions by 2020 is necessary to achieve the 2°c temperature increase limit. that is why the european parliament voted for the 30% a few weeks ago. so there is a first step. i am well aware that a 30% reduction is not easy. in the netherlands, the environmental and natural planning office has indicated that this can only be achieved through major changes in technology and in the behaviour of citizens and businesses. the new dutch government has this ambition. you're right to be. to achieve these ambitious objectives, the many possibilities available will have to be exploited simultaneously. so we cannot blindly focus on one solution. the council conclusions do not clearly indicate which energy sources are to be used to ensure security of supply. the danger is that the only thing on the minds of the people is to get enough gas, oil and coal. if we cling to fossil fuels, we will be hampering the development of renewable energy.
the european union is not a party to the conflict, but it is a party to the peace process. the only way to achieve this is to make this country a protectorate with a powerful high representative of the european union and a continuing military presence. my group wants to go in a different direction. a peaceful and democratic solution must respect the fact that bosnia is a yugoslavia in miniature and that the majority of the population feels serb or croat rather than bosnian. the turkish period was favourable for the bosniaks, the austrian period for the croats and the yugoslav period for the serbs. people now want to get rid of that old inequality and of the domination of one ethnic group over another. a federal solution along the lines of the belgian or swiss model is the best way of keeping the three parts of this country together in good understanding, while recognizing their differences. it is fitting that the control of regional police is vested in the entities and especially not in a central state. the resolution proposed does not, unfortunately, solve the real problems and does not offer a better perspective than the dayton agreement.
the rapporteur rightly points out that the integration of bosnia-herzegovina into europe can only be achieved if fundamental constitutional changes are made. it is equally correct that the rapporteur stresses that internal problems such as ethnic segregation are a threat to national stability. the report does not mention external threats. however, past incidents and the recent unrest in kalesija, central bosnia, caused by some radical wahhabis, cannot be overlooked. it is not without reason that the highest police officer of the muslim croatian federation, zlatko miletic, warned on 10 march of the influence of this radical, relatively young movement among the muslims in bosnia-herzegovina. i would also like to ask the council and the commission whether they have taken note of this trend towards a more general islamization. i expect you to do your utmost to ensure that bosnia-herzegovina enjoys national stability, both internally and externally. the european perspective for the western balkans, as expressed in the thessaloniki agenda in 2003, is clear. but how can the association and stabilisation process succeed unless all the problems, both internal and external, are seriously addressed? here is a task for the new, and i hope effective, high representative.

on behalf of the alde group . – mr president, first of all, i will not dwell on mr voggenhuber’s excellent report except to say that my group will support it wholeheartedly. in principle, we very much welcome the commission’s proposals, but my problem is that they risk becoming the emperor’s clothes. they are full of good intentions but there is a big risk that they will be passive, bureaucratic exercises, because there is such a gap between reality and the proposals in your paper, commissioner. i have a problem, mr mandelson. how do i explain your wonderful proposals to my voters, when the commission does not speak out when a minister for education in an eu member state is on a crusade against homosexuals? why does the commission not speak out in the case of the cia flights, as mr fava just reminded us? why does the commission not speak out in the case of the flagrant discrimination and abominable treatment of roma people? there are so many cases where the commission is passive and even in some cases hiding behind the rules. lately, every time we ask about these cases we are told: ‘just wait and see when the agency for fundamental rights is set up’. commissioner, why does the commission, the champion of fundamental rights, as mr barroso promised us in october 2004, not seek the limits of its powers? why do you hide behind the rules? people expect you to protect and promote their fundamental rights. i, too, would like the annual report, but frankly i consider it much more important that you should act and speak out. that is political leadership. one of president barroso’s favourite phrases is ‘the europe of results’ and i very much like that phrase. but why is it restricted to the economic area? why is it not applied to the area of fundamental rights? soon we will be celebrating 50 years of european integration. after the second world war the idea was that people would never have to fear for their lives again, that everybody would be free, safe, equal and live in a democracy. so fundamental rights should be the first priority of the commission and that should be the meaning of the ‘europe of results’. i hope that this commission in the next two years will create that europe of results and of fundamental rights. i see this proposal only as a basis for that, but i expect action now.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rights of the citizens are respected. but we disagree on the instruments. it is striking that the european union is once again seeking to establish itself on the basis of fundamental rights. the union now has a charter and a fundamental rights office, both of which were not created by unanimity. there is a significant minority, including in this parliament, who have serious objections to the charter of fundamental rights and the way in which it is being developed. i would like to draw attention to the fact that the charter of fundamental rights is an unnecessary duplication of the european convention for the protection of human rights. we have the same duplicate with the office of fundamental rights. both of these duplications can be avoided without infringing the rights of citizens. the european court of human rights here in strasbourg can guarantee that, provided it is given the necessary staff and financial resources. it also takes account of the specific nature of the national legal system. i think that is a sufficient guarantee of our freedoms and fundamental rights. i therefore advocate a limited role for the union in the field of fundamental rights. if the union accedes to the european convention on human rights, there will be a debate on the commission's legislative role in respecting and monitoring fundamental rights.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 2052/88 on the common organization of the market in milk and milk products (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). their numbers have been reduced not only by disease or by pollution of seawater along densely populated coasts but also by increased hunting. for a time, seals seemed to be extinct in some coastal areas. even though some recovery in seal populations is being observed in some areas, the need for continued protection remains. the opposite of protection is seen where people's desire for possessions is given free rein. the skin of seals is coveted for decoration of clothing and toys. kneading dead and skinning live young seals is the shameful means of satisfying that desire for possession. some coastal residents have become economically dependent on this repulsive activity. the ability to export seal skins and to find buyers far from the crime scene is what sustains this system. that is why it has been necessary for many years to ensure that these products are no longer available for sale, especially in the richest parts of the world, such as europe. the first part of commissioner dimas's statement shows that he shares the concern of the european parliament and of public opinion on this matter. the result after that, however, is disappointing. there is no objection to an impact study and it is good that there should be more on-the-spot inspections in canada and namibia. but that cannot be an excuse to wait any longer. some member states of the european union have already taken measures against imports, as has the united states. why should some european countries be left behind?
