president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). the great russian empire with its many minorities gave way to regional self-government for the other peoples, with education and government in the vernacular. eleven countries, including ukraine, georgia, and uzbekistan, have now gained their independence because of that choice. the many smaller peoples, such as the chechens, tatars, ossetians, karelians, or mari, were granted only an autonomous territory within russia or one of the other federal states. these peoples have been in a bad way since the collapse of the soviet union. no one recognizes their right to independence and all hope that they can nevertheless retain the rights granted to them 85 years ago. unfortunately, both in georgia and in russia, a nationalism has arisen which seeks to put an end to these rights. the bad political climate in russia is the price for holding on to chechnya, against the wishes of its inhabitants, with extremely violent methods. intimidation and murder are also promoted within the military, where young soldiers are systematically severely abused by older soldiers and then have to comply with their duty of silence. in putin's russia, there is less and less room for contradiction, critical analysis and change of power. the stability of the current regime and the increasing concentration of control over the various regions in moscow are given absolute priority. opponents are imprisoned, abused or excluded from elections. even more profound is the sudden death of critics, especially of people suspected of revealing important information. europe must help russia to avoid further sliding into a situation which is becoming unlivable for more and more people. the protection of the people who stand up for the rights of the mari republic will help to achieve this.
the european union has always been a party to the peace process, and it is not just the united states that is responsible for this. since then, relations have become hardened and extremely intolerant. first, with american support, a right-wing regime came to power with no other objective than to counter china and vietnam. then came the years of khmer rouge supremacy, which had the illusion that humanity would be happier if the urban population were driven back to the countryside and intellectuals turned into farmers. that chinese-backed regime has cost a huge number of lives. all these intolerant regimes have turned what was once a relatively peaceful and tolerant society into a land of murder and violence. even now, when cambodia is a parliamentary democracy, various political parties continue this ancient tradition. they would rather ban each other and exclude in advance any possibility of taking power. people are being attacked without the real culprits being tracked down and convicted. the crimes of the recent past are still too much for us to deal with. these crimes are covered up as much as possible. in that climate, violent forms of intimidation are given every opportunity, and may even result in the murder of people who advocate different opinions and different interests. recently, the attacks have targeted trade unionists, particularly in the clothing industry. this seems to be aimed at the right of workers to organise themselves and to defend their interests. it is important that europe does not remain silent on this but consistently stands up for human rights. the council and the commission should involve and discuss their contacts with cambodia and, above all, not subordinate them to any economic considerations such as offshore oil.
author. president, nigeria is a country of diversity. many peoples live there, with diverse languages and cultures. it was only through british colonization that they were brought together in a single state. separatist movements such as biafra have long since been suppressed by a vast military superiority. it has contributed to the militarization of the country. we are now seeing the development of the relatively large individual freedom in the christian south, where european standards have penetrated most, being subjected to the strict rules of life from the traditionally isolated orthodox-islamic north. even more than in the united states, where president bush tried in vain to enshrine a constitutional ban on same-sex marriage, nigeria is trying to crack down on homosexuality. in fact, it is no longer just a matter of discouragement, but an attempt at extermination. this not only leads to the prohibition of same-sex marriages or marriages of a more or less equal legal status, but also to the criminalization of all persons involved in organisations representing the interests of homosexual men and lesbians. rights that previously seemed self-evident are threatened with being abolished in the new legislation. banning and persecuting homosexuality does not make it go away. it only leads to justification of violence and oppression against a section of the population. this violence fits with previous attempts to impose the death penalty on raped women who gave birth to a child out of wedlock. this advance of legal injustice must be fought. we must make it clear to those forces in nigeria who are pushing for such laws that they are leading to unacceptable situations and, in this case, to the further spread of sexually transmitted diseases, including aids. europe must not be silent about this, even if we are keen to import nigerian oil.
- a writer. president, i agree with mr gahler that there are many more problems in nigeria and in other countries as well, but sometimes problems are so sensitive and so difficult that you really have to put the spotlight on them and that you have to make a special effort to eradicate those problems. the situation of homosexuals is problematic all over the world. this is a violation of fundamental human rights and i think it is really important that the european parliament and the european union take the lead in the world in the fight against the criminalisation of homosexuality. that is why i am very pleased that this resolution is on the table and i also hope that we will draw the consequences, that is to say that if nigeria does not follow the call of the european parliament, we really have to start reviewing our relations with nigeria. because we cannot be here all the time promoting human rights and then, if our partners do not comply with them, close our eyes. we really need to be consistent about that. finally, i would like to address once again directly to president obasanjo, who was once awarded the freedom prize by the liberals for his work for democracy in his country. i would like to call on him once again to commit himself to all the citizens of his country, including the gay nigerians.
the european parliament has been involved in the negotiations on the kosovo declaration since 1999, and i think there is only one conclusion to be drawn. the report before us is the logical outcome of a long process of deliberation in this house, in which the european parliament - that is, we - have actually reached two conclusions in recent years. firstly, that maintaining the current status quo in kosovo is not an option. that is undesirable, highly undesirable. the second conclusion is that some form of independence - how exactly it is defined is open to debate - is inevitable for kosovo, whether we like it or not. i have tried in my report to outline what this general statement, this general conclusion, means in practice. in other words, what do we think, what does the european parliament think should be the most desirable outcome of the discussions in the security council on the status of kosovo? a few highlights. kosovo must have access to institutions such as the world bank and the imf in order to finally tackle the economic problems, to bring the economy of kosovo out of the slump. secondly, the multi-ethnic character of kosovo must be preserved. the best way to achieve this for the time being is through the presence of an international presence, both military and civilian. read the following: the european union. this leads to the conclusion that the european union has a key role to play once that status has been established. it is the european union - and we are the ones - who will have to see to it that the kosovo authorities continue to guide their country on the road to a democratic multiethnic state which can eventually become a member of the european union. the european parliament is prepared to provide the necessary budget for this role, for this task, if we are informed in time and sufficiently. finally, kosovo is a unique case because of the nato intervention in 1990, but especially because for almost eight years that part of serbia has been under un administration. this also means that solutions to the current situation, which are being sought, are unique and cannot be used to resolve conflicts elsewhere in the world. i think that the majority of the house would agree with me on that point. what has happened in the past week is that the debate on this report is not focused on the content i have just outlined, but on the question of what label, what label should be affixed to that desired situation. in other words, what word do we want to use to describe that desired post-independence situation? is that independence under control, is that sovereignty under control or should we perhaps refrain from putting a clear label, a clear label on that situation at all? i would like to say the following to those who do not want to make a statement on this at the moment, who do not want to make a statement on how to define this situation. i think it is very important that the european union should speak with one voice, act with one voice! here in brussels, but also in the security council in new york and also, ladies and gentlemen, in the european parliament. if we agree on the end goal, why don't we just say so? that will have a positive impact on the difficult discussions in brussels and new york and will make it more difficult for russia - the great opponent of eventual independence - to tear the european union apart. another argument against giving clarity now is why we as the european parliament should be leading the way now? why should we, as the first european institution, be so clear about the final stage? i would like to make the following objection. since last monday, someone else has been leading the way. that is martti ahtisaari, the secretary-general's special envoy, who in his recommendation to the security council said the following: "the status of kosovo should be: independence under the supervision of the international community.' this conclusion is fully endorsed by ban ki-moon, secretary-general of the united nations. in other words, others are ahead, and i think it would be good for the european parliament if we too were to be clear about what we want. that is to say, support the recommendation of ahtisaari by clearly stating that, as far as we are concerned, the best outcome of the process is sovereignty under supervision. we are parliamentarians, we are politicians, we are not diplomats. i shall be very pleased to have your support in this plenary for my report tomorrow. i am not satisfied until parliament too has made clear what we think the final aim should be. that is sovereignty under eu supervision as far as i am concerned. that is the clarity to which the kosovars are entitled, to which the serbs are entitled and to which the citizens of europe are entitled.
the commission is therefore proposing that the council should adopt a decision on the matter. a key aspect such as citizenship based on kosovo's multilingual and multiethnic character passes the test. it is remarkable that the present report does not mention the explicit status of kosovo (see amendment no 13, which we will support). if not un mediator ahtisaari. he presented his final report to the security council with a clear recommendation the day before yesterday. independence under international supervision for kosovo. however, prime minister kostunica said last week that independence for kosovo will never be an option for serbia. he even hopes for a russian veto in the security council. this wish is in direct opposition to the demand of the albanian movement for self-determination. the fact is that leader kurti wants nothing more than unconditional independence. the danger of ethnic disintegration and regional instability is lurking anyway. the great challenge, mr president, for the international community is therefore obvious. regional stability combined with multi-ethnic citizenship in a sovereign kosovo. the european commissioner rehn spoke in parliament last week about a major litmus test for the eu. i wish the commission and the council every success, wisdom and support.

in writing. the issue of kosovo divides political currents. my group, the european united left, and the parties associated with it have unanimously opposed the war that nato waged against yugoslavia in 1999. that war was not about liberating kosovo, but about more outside control of serbia and montenegro. some of my colleagues in the group of the european people's party are still afraid that the united states is using the problem of kosovo to divide european countries into small military protectorates. they also invoke international law, which provides that no new states can be formed without the prior consent of the state which loses territory. if we follow that line of reasoning, many of the present european states, such as greece, belgium, bulgaria, ireland, poland, the czech republic and slovenia, are also illegal. i prefer to compare it to the independence of former european colonies such as indonesia, algeria or angola. the left in europe has supported their independence struggle. if democracy and the equality of the people of kosovo require that they become independent, the left must be at the forefront of that, not behind. i also recognise the right of self-determination of the serb-majority inhabitants of kosovska mitrovica and the northern tip of kosovo who wish to return permanently to serbia.

mr president, it is 2007, so you can address me as ‘miss’! i would also like to congratulate my colleague mr zingaretti, although he knows that we agree to disagree on this issue. the legal base of the proposal derives from the court of justice. the commission interprets this ruling to be applicable beyond the case, and sees this ruling as a legal base for the harmonisation of certain criminal sanctions in measures aimed at protecting the internal market. the reason i consider rejection of this directive as a whole is not because i do not believe in expanding the powers of the eu to include criminal sanctions. i do believe in that. however, i have serious doubts about the process leading to this increase of power, because a legal base is not the same as a political mandate. fundamental steps like this demand a thorough political debate and a clear choice on the matter in question. instead, we are about to take a great leap forward as a by-product of a single policy measure in a highly specialised and limited field. i do not think this is the best way of pushing integration forward. it also enforces the popular opinion held by many who see the european union as an organisation which caters mainly for the interests of big businesses, which is not the case.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. on the one hand, we have adopted the regulations, generally accepted here in this parliament, between the institutions for 2007-2013 and we are fully engaged in the operational programmes. cohesion policy is the most important basis for solidarity in the european union and for competitiveness. all european regions can benefit from this through the three objectives we have agreed. europe close to its citizens in all regions. and yet there is a need for an early rethinking of the sustainability of this successful policy. as mrs hübner has just said, regional policy must actively prepare for the midterm review, which is only realistic when it comes to the necessary financial frameworks resulting from the enlargement envisaged. the pieper report opens up that debate and invites the commission to present targeted financial effects. it is not just a question of money, as the pieper report shows, but also of more flexibility, targeted phasing, more creativity and a different approach to cofinancing. i would also like to say that the epp group, while maintaining the foundations of regional policy in the member states, wants to think about another, more flexible approach and we will have the opportunity to do so in the fourth cohesion report, already next month, and later in the fifth cohesion report, but the signal is clear: the commission has already made a number of proposals. that is why it is good to involve parliament in this early part of the parliamentary term, this legislature, by means of an own-initiative report and to show the consequences. i would like to conclude by saying that i hope that parliament will be involved in this debate much more intensively, particularly in the next year and a half, before we start the midterm review.
i am therefore going to speak on potatoes, on starch potatoes, after being sworn in this afternoon. this can hardly be a coincidence, since i grew up with a father who was quite fond of reading german children's books - perhaps mr graefe zu baringdorf should be interested in that - and there was always a story about "die bedeuting der kartoffel". i don't know who wrote it, but my father loved to read it to me, and so my maiden speech about potatoes can hardly be a coincidence. but let's put all the crazy aside for a moment: the commission's proposal is of course of great importance to a number of regions in europe, including the north-east of my own country where the potato starch industry provides significant employment and production. this sector, and therefore the producers and their region, have an interest in clarity and certainty as regards the planning and management of their individual holdings, especially after the fundamental reform of the common agricultural policy in 2003 and in view of the uncertainties associated with the ongoing wto negotiations. it 's been said before: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission is now again proposing to extend it by two years, the rapporteur made it three years and finally the committee on agriculture voted for four years, so i think that is a clear signal to the commission and the council. i would like to ask the commissioner, in principle, to support the proposed extension, but preferably for a slightly longer period, so that those concerned know where they stand in the years ahead. i think that knowing your limits also means that you do not abuse the existing quotas during this period. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
mr president, i also congratulate the rapporteur. i would also like to congratulate my new dutch colleague, esther de lange, on her maiden speech. i didn't realize the potatoes were such a relevant topic. i recommend to the commission a four-year period. why four years? i think everybody wants a little security, including farmers. four years is also a good period to evaluate the sugar reform. that is not at all clear at the moment; there is a clear link between isoglucose and potato starch. i think we will not know where we stand for another four years, and that is why i very much support those four years. it has been said by a number of people: the graefe zu baringdorf amendment is ideal. i don't know, man. how can you predict this year's harvest based on last year's? quotas are not transferable from one year to another. if you do, even if the quota is too little one year and too much the next, you are not doing much good, because a farmer can never predict production. so i don't know if it's technically feasible.
i think that the commission has a duty to ensure that the commission is able to take account of the interests of the european union in the field of competition policy. i don't know what it is, but it's very remarkable. apparently, this is considered a kind of nighttime topic. mr president, as rapporteur for the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights, i would like to make a few comments. the question of principle is: should citizens and companies who suffer damage as a result of infringements of antitrust law be entitled to compensation? yes, and the legal affairs committee is in full agreement with that. we believe that in such cases there should be a right to compensation in the member states. member states should provide for such a procedure. how they decorate it is up to them. in some member states, the first step will be to establish that there is indeed an infringement, which will then be carried out by the public authorities. in other member states, the procedure is different, but it is all governed by the procedures of the member states. the question is also: i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission is not in a position to impose such procedures on the member states from brussels. you can convince them that they should introduce them, but whether you can impose them is something we have serious doubts about. this is not a question of cartel law, it is a question of civil law. civil law, like criminal law, is reserved to the member states. the european union cannot intervene in this. we therefore also have serious doubts about the possible legal basis for european legislative measures to introduce such procedures. the same applies in fact to all those other questions and comments in the green paper which relate to the taking of evidence, for example the hiring of experts, the class actions, all of which are matters which belong to national civil law and the european union cannot in fact legislate on them. these are the main points we have made as the legal affairs committee.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption by the end of the year. that is why we are calling for the possibility of not being examined further on a number of points. we would like the commission to be given the opportunity to examine a number of things in a proper way and to include an impact assessment to see what the effects are. i can imagine that we too will conclude in the green paper that this is not a good way forward and that some things do not need to be developed further or implemented. but we are not ready to make a decision on that at the moment. we therefore call on the epp to reconsider some of these points or perhaps to provide for a formulation which leaves a little more room for what the conclusion should be, in order to at least give the commission the opportunity to examine it further. we shall then be critical of our own amendments. i would like to invite you to look at this again tomorrow. i would also like to speak on this point from the dutch point of view, because we are having a very exciting debate in the netherlands. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the commission's proposals are implemented. that is, of course, a subject that the whole of the netherlands thinks it can talk about. it was a very illustrative example, because the beer cartel involved price agreements between beer brewers, who also have a great deal of influence on the catering industry and which have naturally caused people, the final users, to pay far too much for their beer, something that was already a topic of discussion in the netherlands, even at the time when the euro was introduced; however, this example also showed that the fines that are then distributed go first to the european commission and then back to the member states, and do not go directly to the consumer. the catering companies may sue the brewers to see if they can recover any damage, but the consumer, the end user, is not going to benefit from that. in that sense, it's very interesting as an example to see how you can move forward; the final end user is duped by this type of cartel. so, from my situation, this is a good illustration. i therefore call on this house to keep that door open and to explore how we can also make the consumer himself, the end user himself, more a part of this cartel policy.

president, madam president-in-office, commissioner barrot, i am very pleased with the european commitment to independent investigation of shipping accidents. the epp therefore strongly supports this, since the purpose of such an inquiry is to establish the cause of the disaster so that measures can be taken to prevent a recurrence. we have had very good experience with this in air disasters. after the disasters of the oil tankers erika and prestige, independent investigations failed and regions and member states threatened to shift the blame mainly to the other. when a major disaster occurs in our international waters, it is not long before several member states and many parties are involved. this european directive will help us to get the bottom of this and prevent the member states from cheating each other in such a case. we have, as the committee on transport, emphasised the independence of the inquiry. the research information may only be used to improve safety at sea. the epp therefore disagrees with the european commission. information from the investigation into the cause of the disaster should not be made available for criminal proceedings in the member states, as there is a high risk that those involved will not be able to bring important information to the table for fear of criminal prosecution. a strict separation between criminal investigation and investigation into the cause of the disaster is therefore very important. mr president, apart from amendments on this point and amendments to speed up the examination, there are some amendments which are a little more detailed and which we do not attach particular importance to, but overall i think that there is a good report. i would also like to make a comment on the directive on passenger rights. it was intended for maritime transport, but now all of a sudden inland waterway transport is also being accommodated there and i understand that even commissioner barrot supports this. i do not understand that support, because a passenger ship sailing on the danube, the meuse or the rhine is not comparable to a seagoing vessel on the ocean. you might compare that to a train or a touring car. so i want to point out that the risk profile is very different. in the context of the naiades action programme, you, commissioner barrot, have promised to harmonize european legislation on inland waterway transport, and we are talking about ccr legislation; the first case that arises now is that you want to apply maritime law to inland navigation. that's the wrong way. i still hope that you will harmonize ccr legislation - you may add a little to that, making it stricter - and that you will do so. we have tabled the amendments again to get inland navigation out of this and i hope that it does not get a majority and at least not a qualified majority and that the council gets its way on this point.
i should like to concentrate on the costa report. that seems to contain two important points. firstly, the liability regime in the event of terrorist activities. this point has been discussed and dealt with at length in the imo. a good example of the right solution at the right level. that brings me to my second point of interest. that is the scope of the regulation. the athens convention is designed for seafaring. the extension of the scope to inland navigation has therefore surprised me. the major differences between maritime and inland waterway transport do not justify a uniform liability regime. neither do the consequences of equalization. the increase in liability for inland waterway carriers is expected to have an impact on the financial viability of the service. i think that is unacceptable, given the social importance of passenger transport by water in a number of countries. for this reason, my group for inland navigation proposes that this proposal be deleted and that, as a consequence, the improvement of the liability regime for inland navigation in the context of ccr be supported. i welcome the epp's position on this matter and i hope very much that the other groups will join in.

president, commissioner dimas, ladies and gentlemen, the netherlands has played an important role in the development of the high seas directive as it stands today. it is important to have a legislative framework for the assessment and management of flood risks, respecting the principle of subsidiarity. what can be done locally, we do locally, such as measures to prevent the overflow of sewage. it's a way of protecting the environment, public health and the economy. a low-lying country like the netherlands is dependent on many surrounding countries for water management. it is therefore important that member states do not take measures that lead to higher flood risks in other countries. a coordinated approach is therefore required. i therefore attach great importance to the concept of solidarity, which is reflected in this proposed directive and is mentioned even more explicitly in amendments nos 48 and 61 of the compromise package, which i wholeheartedly support. in addition to the concept of solidarity, it is also important that we include the expected climate change and its potential effects in the preparation of flood risk management plans, in order to be well prepared for the future. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr seeber, for the good cooperation and congratulate him on this result.
- i'm the reporter. president. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i think that at this late hour, the qualitative part of the house is different from the quantitative part. that's how you'd have to see it. the commission has spent five years on producing a strategy for the sustainable use of natural resources. that's a long time. it is therefore extremely disappointing that the commission has not put forward such an ambitious proposal. the commission is right to point out the problem, which is that if we continue in the present way, we will inevitably be faced with the exhaustion of our resources in the near future. and with that, a serious threat to our economy and to our quality of life. but the commission then fails to come up with concrete action points. it calls for more research and more data. that's fine, but we can't wait years for that. like climate change, the problem of natural resources is now acute and we must take concrete action now. however, when you read the proposal, it seems that the commission lacks a sense of urgency. this does not invite the citizens to think about these problems, but they are the ones who have to do it. the core of the problem is simple. our ecological footprint, the environmental impact of our consumption, is far greater than the earth can handle. to give you a clear example: if this baby shoe is the ecological footprint that we should have to have a good balance in our natural resources, then this big men's shoe is the consumption that we have right now. you can see that that's really way too big. so let me give you an example. this is what we're doing right now. i'll be very honest with you, commissioner. the first tendency in the environment committee of parliament was to send the whole thing back to you and let the commission do its homework. but i do not think that this is very constructive, and we do not have the time to wait another five years for action. i therefore decided to make a serious proposal and called on all my colleagues to help me. and, i can tell you, my colleagues have been living it up. together we have been able to produce a large number of ideas for improving the commission's work excluded from the strategy, such as transport, fisheries and construction. we will have to intervene there too. in short, it must be a strategy for all policy areas. the structure of the strategy starts to a large extent with the consumer and the producer. we also proposed a life-cycle approach, a top 10 priority list of endangered natural resources, fiscal incentives, information provision, involving ngos and experts, the use of alternative recycling and reuse. in short, too many to mention here. i propose that the eu in general should aim to halve resource use by 2030. this is not science fiction, this is real necessity. of course, european policy should not prevent member states from doing even better. by setting the best-performing member state as an example for others, this we believe that the european union and the countries of the european union can work together with the americans in the framework of nato on projects that provide both security and reconstruction. there is also a debate going on there about missile defence. what struck us is that there is a greater desire for dialogue with the europeans, but also with the russians, but we remain critical of the outcome of that process. another important point we have raised is the middle east conflict. we want to stress again - and we hope the presidency will do so - that we must ensure that the new palestinian unity government is not left in the cold and we are looking for ways of supporting this new development. what we also noticed is that there is a search, especially by the democrats, for a new social agenda for the united states that puts a lot of emphasis on the health care problem, but also looks at doha. what can we do together to ensure that environmental and working conditions play an important role in trade negotiations? there are, of course, still criticisms. we've also reported them when it comes to returns and secret prison camps, when it comes to agreements on data protection. these are matters that must continue to be of concern to us. finally, when you add it all up, there is an important agenda for cooperation, based also on the common values already mentioned here. and finally, one last little thing: i hope that the presidency will also bring up the wolfowitz case at the summit if necessary, because we believe that this man has become untenable as the director of the world bank, given the important role of the world bank in the fight against corruption. president. - the zimbabwe i knew was a proud country, a leader in the region, proud of its hard-fought freedom, a beautiful country where black and white zimbabweans believed in the future, where black and white together tried to overcome the painful racist wounds of the rhodesia period. a country where citizens worked to build a democracy. and now for zimbabwe with today's hard numbers. life expectancy in zimbabwe is now one of the lowest in the world: 37 years for men and no more than 34 years for women. 80% of zimbabweans live below the poverty line. a third of the population has fled their own country, millions have sought refuge in neighbouring countries. every month, another 50,000 refugees are added to that number. the economy of the country, once the breadbasket of africa, has shrunk by 40% in one decade. improvement is not yet in sight. the zimbabwean economy is still shrinking by 5.7% and zimbabwe is the only country in africa where the economy is not growing. there is hyperinflation, as has been said before, which makes it impossible for many zimbabweans who have remained in zimbabwe with massive unemployment to meet their basic daily needs. the european union has rightly decided to impose even more extensive personal sanctions on the zimbabwean leaders who have brought about this destruction by means of state terror. as we all know, it takes a lot more to find a way out. that solution is set out in our joint resolution. it is about transition in the post-mugabe period, describes concrete steps and actions and rightly makes a dramatic appeal to mbeki and the sadc members to block the door of state terror, if necessary with force, and open the way to dialogue in the post-mugabe period. president, we support the broadest possible opposition to mugabe and to zimbabwe. we ask the council to broaden the scope of sanctions. anyone responsible for the ever-tightening slide in this once proud country must be dealt with, including ministers, deputies, military leaders, the police and secret service, and the governor of the central bank. we ask the uk to use its presidency of the security council to put zimbabwe on the agenda. finally, mr president, we really hope that mbeki will play an even greater role in bringing the mugabe regime to an end. mugabe's role in zimbabwe must be played out for good after nearly three decades.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). what does the president of zimbabwe have to do with this extraordinary profession? all of it! because in the immediate vicinity of mugabe's estate - the largest private residence in africa - honest citizens are keeping their heads above water by a daily mousetrap. the animals are like beef to us, they said. so deep has zimbabwe, until recently the wheat barn of africa, sunk under the tyrannical rule of robert mugabe. this is the basis of the foolish as well as criminal campaign which has seen thousands of productive farms of white owners confiscated since the year 2000 and handed over to mugabe's incompetent and indifferent henchmen with the resulting widespread famine. and incidentally, mugabe's zanu-pf party is using food shortages as a weapon against the opposition. the ultimate criticism of mugabe's lust for power was recently voiced by correspondent r.w. johnson. he reported that in zimbabwe people are being massacred like animals and that the vast majority of deaths are the direct result of deliberate government policies. this genocide, ignored by the un, is perhaps ten times greater than in darfur. similarly, arnold tsunga, chairman of the human rights organization crisis in zimbabwe coalition, said: "we are not going to let the situation get worse. tsunga called mugabe's policies "smart genocide" because they go unnoticed by governments, aid organizations and the press. the council, the commission and the members of this house must not and must never let this accusation of a clever, silent genocide stand until it is withdrawn.
the european parliament has been a party to the treaty of rome since the beginning of the twentieth century, and it is not the council that has been the party to the treaty of rome. i remember that the long-dead german radical student leader rudi dutschke, known as the bürgerschreck, warned the left in western europe against this attitude. in his view, what was happening in russia at the time had nothing to do with socialism or communism, but everything to do with a tradition he called "asian". it is a tradition in which a small group manages to maintain control by the most brutal means in a large and sparsely populated country. if someone comes to power in russia who wants to break this bad state of affairs, there is still a good chance that he will be succeeded by someone who will resort to the old abuses. khrushchev in the '50s and '60s and gorbachev in the '80s and '90s couldn't change russia from the top down. the current president putin, like the tsars and stalin before him, is again fully in line with that old russian tradition. he was able to come to power when his chaotic predecessor, yeltsin, was no longer able to govern, was able to make himself popular with the russians by destroying chechnya and is now able to deprive the russians of their democracy. unfortunately, it's the country that owns oil or natural gas. that possession and the wealth of exports gives the rulers a strengthened position of power and makes domestic changes almost impossible. the commission has also been criticized by a large part of the outside world for wanting to buy energy. after saudi arabia and iran, russia is now in that position. even in the two largest cities, where so far there has been the greatest room for criticism and diversity, demonstrations by opponents are being made impossible. the european countries and their citizens must not rest on their laurels, but must show solidarity with those who want to make russia a decent democracy.
author. mr president, gaza is a small but highly urbanized area, virtually cut off from the outside world. the port and airport, which were prepared with european union money, have not been able to develop thanks to the opposition of the neighbouring state of israel. nor has there been any open link with the much larger part of the future palestinian state along the jordan river. israel, which has long been a place of employment, has largely cut off that opportunity for fear of attacks and is allowing far fewer people through the erez border post. israel also controls the border crossing with egypt. in this isolated area, a large part of the palestinian population lives in a cramped situation, without work, without income and without adequate housing. the wise decision to withdraw the israeli settler villages from this coastal strip did solve something for the villagers hated by their neighbors, but hardly anything for all the other inhabitants. gaza is a disaster area full of people with no prospects for the future. that makes it a kind of prison, a breeding ground for desperate acts. for a long time, these acts of desperation were mainly suicide bombings against israeli targets, but kidnappings are also a part of it. alan johnston is the 15th journalist to be kidnapped in gaza and in the month since his abduction, nothing has been heard from him for a long time. all in writing. the debate on zimbabwe was brought to the table this afternoon by mr pafilis on behalf of my group. i know that he and his greek party want to emphasize the longstanding conflict between the former british colonial empire and the independence movement led by the current president, mr mogabe. it is emphasized as a conflict between absolute evil and absolute good. with my colleague pafilis, i am voting for the moegabe of the 1970s and against colonialism. on the other hand, we have very different views on the present. i have made this clear in previous urgent debates. i am in favour of land reform, i.e. the division of land from large farmers to small farmers. however, the way this is done in zimbabwe has led to violence, intimidation and insecurity of livelihoods, including for the black farmers and farm workers. since then, the violent approach has spread to the cities. moegabe no longer serves his people against the colonial exploiters, but plunges his people into chaos, oppression and poverty. such a regime now relies on violence and intimidation and does not deserve to exist any longer. i therefore vote in favour of the resolution on zimbabwe.

- i'm the reporter. mr president, i asked to speak before amendment no 67 was put to the vote. that might have cleared up a misunderstanding. the german presidency of the council agreed during the negotiations between 12 and 24 april that amendments nos 66 and 67 refer to 'significant' as a condition for subcontracting. the presidency offered the alternative term 'substantial', but the two largest groups ultimately did not need it at the time. the rapporteur and the other members of the house then decided that the amendments tabled jointly by six groups should use the word 'major'. i agree with them on the substance, but i have always warned that this is not part of the compromise reached and that in theory it could lead to the need for a third reading. the christian democrats and the social democrats were confident that they could persuade the council, and especially germany, which was the main opponent, to accept this amendment, and i was happy to follow them. and i now see that they are also condemned to maintain that, given that so many people have stood up against a vote on an oral amendment in which they would like to have all this reversed.
in writing. the european commission's proposal in 2000 to make all public transport compulsory to tender was in line with the political fashion of the day. the idea then prevailed that the government should withdraw from many tasks, that this could reduce taxes and that the market could regulate everything in an adequate way. this was part of the agreements made at the lisbon summit of heads of government a few months before this proposal, in the expectation that more markets and more profits would also lead to greater economic growth and even better and cheaper services for citizens. this neo-liberal ideology has since collided with reality. the lisbon strategy is not delivering what was expected. the market is not the answer, particularly in the case of services which are both necessary and unprofitable, such as public transport. it would lead to the disappearance of integrated networks, leaving only the busiest lines. over the past seven years, experience with privatisation and tendering has increased, and the disappointments experienced have contributed to the growth of counter-forces. my objective as rapporteur, the preservation of municipal transport companies and freedom of choice, was partly achieved by this.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has adopted a report on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. mr president, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, i would like to make a number of points in this debate. it is clear that health services are a fundamental part of the european social infrastructure. if there is anything specific about europe, if there is anything specific about europe that distinguishes it from others, it is that we make a high level of health care available to all citizens, regardless of their personal background. our starting point in the whole discussion is therefore that patients should ultimately be helped at a high level and preferably as close as possible to where they live, because it also turns out that most patients need it. but of course there are situations where you cannot do otherwise, for example when there are waiting lists or when it comes to rare diseases that can only be solved at european level. and while health is still primarily the responsibility of the member states, the committee on the environment, public health and food safety welcomes the commission's initiative to start the consultation procedure to see what is the best form of action for the european union.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioner, thank you for your introduction. i would also like to thank mrs vergnaud for the pleasant cooperation. what is this about, because i have heard a few times that health services should not be covered by the services directive. the proposal for the present article is the result of the compromises that have been reached and thus takes account of the condition laid down by the ppe-de, pse and alde group, namely that health services should be included in the services directive as a lex specialis. but i understand that there has been a lot of commotion, and that is why i have tabled a substitute amendment which in fact provides for what the commissioner has just said. the right balance must be struck between the free movement of services, respect for patients' rights, freedom to practise medicine in europe and freedom of establishment. and that is what the new amendment provides for, and i hope that the pse group and the ppe-de group can accept that too, that we adopt the amendment together and that we will finally have a new proposal which, mr president, ensures equal treatment and solidarity for all europeans, and i mean for all europeans, and for all european patients. and not that medical services are simply considered a service of general interest, which puts them outside the european treaty, so that each member state sets up its own system again, closes its borders, there is no freedom, patient rights are not recognized and the rich do not get treatment in europe, but go back to beijing and buy the best quality they want there. if that is the aim of europe, then i think we are going back to the 17th century - that was a golden age - but i do not think that is the aim of europe. i hope therefore that the amendment tabled by the liberal group to replace article 71 will receive broad support, so that there can be a separate directive on health services, and that all the compromise amendments will be supported, at least by us, it is a good package. i hope that we will succeed in our endeavour.
president. - mr president, i was the health rapporteur for this parliament two years ago when we were discussing the services directive. then parliament followed my advice to exclude health services from the services directive. i am deeply saddened that, now that there is a proposal to undo this, parliament, by agreeing to it, is making itself completely untrustworthy. it is not just mr manders's pernicious amendment to bring health services back into the services directive - he is now also backing down on that, but he is still essentially saying the same thing - the whole idea of an eu health services directive is too intrusive for me. of course, there must be a proposal to ensure in a proper way the right of patients to seek care across borders, but this must not lead to member states neglecting their responsibilities for quality and quantity of good care. patients prefer to receive good care close to home and family. legal wrangling should certainly not be an excuse for liberalising eu health services. health services occupy a specific place in society. accessibility and quality must always be priorities, not profit-making. health care is not a market, and europe should not want to make it one. article 152 of the treaty states that health care is a matter for the member states and i believe that it should certainly remain so in the interests of patients and health care workers.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on agriculture and rural development. mr president, with this important report by mr adamo, parliament is sounding the alarm. it's just a pattern that's too familiar. the commission and parliament show their ambition for the environment and then the heads of government and ministers make solemn declarations and even make agreements, but then they do not implement the plans. the member states block where the general interest of europe calls for action. the european council welcomes the commission's proposal to establish a european union-wide network of national authorities responsible for the protection of the environment. the protection of biodiversity must be a priority in all policy areas. the committee on agriculture and rural development sees the 2008 review of agricultural policy as a major new opportunity to tackle the decline in biodiversity. but that requires a bigger budget for rural development and a greater emphasis on nature and landscape protection. this also requires a critical evaluation of cross compliance measures. it is fine and logical to pay farmers for their services to the environment, but this must prove its effectiveness for biodiversity and for a sustainable europe.
on behalf of the ind/dem group president, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs laperrouze, for her work. i would like to mention two other points. firstly, cases where compliance with environmental quality standards in a member state is technically impossible or entails disproportionate costs, both social and economic. i want to see a derogation for this, as in the water framework directive, article 4. my second comment concerns the inventory of losses. for example, shipping and tides release pollutants from the sediment. these substances were discharged at an earlier time. they should not be considered losses. the reason for this is that these substances have already been discharged at an earlier stage. to achieve all this, we have tabled two amendments on behalf of the ind/dem group, and i therefore trust that you will support amendments nos 66 and 67.
- i'm the reporter. president. - i do not think i need five minutes. the commissioner's introduction is clear. the report by my predecessor, albert jan maat, is, for the most part, concerned with the objective of fisheries policy in general. sustainable fishing, and the term 'sustainable' is clear and its meaning is also clear from what mr borg has just said and from what is in the agreement and the protocol. the main objective of the partnership agreement with greenland is to strengthen the relations between the union and greenland. cooperation based on mutual trust is now more necessary than ever, mr president. we have all known for some time that the fisheries sector is going to undergo major changes in the years to come. the decline in fish stocks and the movement of fish stocks - particularly from sea warming, think of cod - but also the reduction in fishing quotas, which is being implemented, are forcing the fisheries sector to operate differently. that has been made clear to the industry and is becoming clear and the industry is also increasingly taking action. for many fishermen, it is a profound change in their entrepreneurial spirit. the new entrepreneurship must eventually lead to an improvement in fish yields in the future, but in the longer term. sustainable fishing, in other words, where the catch in the sea is in balance with the production there and where fishing also has the least possible impact on the underwater environment. fishermen, i read in the report, must become more of a manager of the sea than just a hunter of fishing grounds and fish stocks. this requires cooperation, and not just between each other, and by 'amongst' i mean between member states and even with partners like greenland, particularly in order to achieve long-term improvements in the production chain. as rapporteur, i note that this is the commission's, and the commissioner's, commitment, and i have noted that our committee on fisheries has also reached this conclusion. i thank my colleagues for their constructive contribution and i warmly commend the report which albert jan maat prepared for me at the time. that was my contribution and i hope that the report will be adopted at the next plenary session.
i would like to begin by congratulating mr gaspari on his balanced report. i shall be voting in favour of this text tomorrow. as rapporteur for relations between the european union and china, i was particularly struck by the paragraphs on trade relations with the people's republic. i was surprised by the wording of paragraph 44, because i do not see china as the first and most obvious example of the positive effect of trade liberalization and active participation in global and competitive markets. the rapporteur agrees with me on this point, given his long list of concerns and points of disagreement which follows. in addition to the social and environmental problems and our concerns about the protection of european intellectual property, i would also like to mention the lack of access of european companies to the chinese market and dumping practices in china. this too is damaging to european industry. the chinese are indeed shielding their own market from foreign competition and at the same time favouring their own producers. mr president, an open economy in a globalising world is often wrongly confused with an economy without barriers. fortunately, the rapporteur does not take this line. in paragraph 17 of his report, mr gaspari rightly refers to the damage to the european economy caused by the behaviour of countries which do not respect the rules of trade. in response, the european union must protect itself against unfair trade practices. i therefore call on the commission to make it clear, within the wto and beyond, that the union does not tolerate unfair behaviour. however, this implies that the eu must also be prepared to impose effective sanctions with adequate trade defence instruments. not out of a protectionist reflex, but because in some cases only coercion helps to guarantee a level playing field for eu business.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. mr president, trade and development cooperation are very closely intertwined, as the commissioner rightly said. if we are to achieve the mdgs by 2015, aid and trade can and must complement each other. the developing countries need our help to develop their regional markets further. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the products. europe and its development partners must jointly seek a development task force capable of supporting this process of building up local markets. this task force should be composed of experts in the field of trade and development policy and could assist in the development and implementation of concrete trade-enhancing measures. we must also consider alternative tax revenues, the strengthening of production capacity, standardization and the creation of common external border controls. the human resources in particular need to be further developed. such practical support would be a real help in the flourishing of local markets and mutual trade. the eib is more likely to be considered for major infrastructure projects. but this trade-oriented aid must not be a cigar from its own box. so they're not going to be funded from the existing development budget. the pse group wants new money, otherwise there would be less money available to achieve the millennium goals, and that is, after all, the central objective which the commissioner has just set himself and which we wish him success with.
president. - mr president, we have been supporting developing countries for a long time in all areas except trade. this is one reason why countries and their economies have remained heavily subsidized. economic growth is proving to be indispensable to combating poverty effectively. look at the tremendous progress that countries in asia have gone through. korea is a good example. we are therefore increasingly finding that trade offers exceptional opportunities for economic growth in poor countries. developing countries often do not yet have the right infrastructure to operate in the world market. that is why these countries need our support and why trade-related aid exists. the aim is to enable developing countries to function in the international market. for example, through trade-related assistance, countries can improve regulation in areas that affect trade, such as taxation and customs. it is about improving roads and waterways, fighting fraud and corruption, strengthening production capacity and diversification. after all, many countries depend on only one agricultural product. diversification of products promotes a more stable economy. capacity building is also needed to train good negotiators. mr president, since trade-related aid does this, it helps poor countries to strengthen their economies and increase their independence, and that is why i welcome this initiative. trade-related aid, as has been said, is not a panacea for development, but it is necessary to enable developing countries to enter the international market. i congratulate the rapporteur, mr david martin, on the content and also on the pleasant cooperation which has resulted in a report with a strong content and broad support. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. everyone agrees that trade can help to combat poverty. unfortunately, the trade advantages for the acp countries provided for in the cotonou agreement are not working in practice. the same is true for everything but guns. this is all due to the poor trading conditions in the developing regions in question, the high standardised european import requirements and the still unreformed and practical rules of origin. these problems mean that the acp countries are not yet able to participate in the world economy. in fact, they're pushing it even further away. the debate on economic partnership agreements should therefore not be primarily about the conclusion of free trade agreements but about the conclusion of development contracts. the development of trade in the region, including the institutional framework and human resources which are difficult to develop, and the millennium development goals should be central to these development contracts. it is only in the longer term that we can talk about opening up the eu's markets. that is the problem as far as we are concerned. everything seems, and this is of course logical, because the deadline expires on 1 january, everything seems to be aimed at 1 january 2008. but in the meantime the flexibility offered in the negotiations is not really sufficiently linked to development indicators and much more to rather vague time indications. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. is the commission prepared to link eu access to the markets in question to a development benchmark so that we can be sure that the local markets are ready for it? moreover, is the commission prepared to accept explicitly the alternative of sap+ for those regions which do not want to conclude an epa - and again, we fully support the negotiations on this - but which decide not to conclude an epa? we are aware of the technical debate, but we believe that if we do not look at bananas and sugar - which have already been put in brackets in practice, including by ourselves - that for 98% it is indeed a real alternative to the current commercial advantages. if the eu is also prepared to work on a rapid reform of the rules of origin - and the commissioner has just confirmed this in another debate - then that could mean that we are going a long way in this. on the singapore issues: it is a good thing, of course, if the regions themselves want to do something about it at a certain moment. there's nothing wrong with that. just, let's not get ahead of ourselves. it is of the utmost importance, mr president, to meet the time pressure of the epo negotiations. i told you that already. and that is why we want to focus on the development contract thinking, where the pse group says: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. if the epo comes out like that, it's great, and if it doesn't, it's just not a good offer. and be prepared to bring sap+ on the table as a real alternative and to speak openly and frankly about it in the discussions.
madam president, the hamas charter is a sacred text with three islamic seals. unlike the secular plo, hamas does not recognise the concept of peace. the plo may want a two-state solution, but hamas wants to eliminate israel. what does the hamas charter say? in article 7 it mentions an islamic world order and the destruction of all jews. articles 22 and 28 say that the jewish people created the french and russian revolutions, the first and second world wars, but also the united nations, drugs and alcohol abuse, and control of the mass media. stopping the conflict with israel is high treason and perpetrators will be cursed, according to article 32. cooperation with hamas would make the eu morally responsible for a peace process leading to the elimination of israel. we must help the palestinians, but not by facilitating the hamas agenda.

president. - mr prodi, the italian prime minister, yesterday said that the members of this parliament must defend the old draft european constitution in their countries and that this draft already contains so many compromises that further compromises are almost impossible. and that's a story that's going to do well here, because this is a place where democracy is primarily seen from a large-scale level and from the top down. it is proving to be a very difficult match for a small-scale democracy from below, where many more people feel involved. in 2005, the member states' parliaments also had a large majority in favour of the constitution. in the netherlands, the figure was 85%. unlike in france, where only the president decides on the subject of a referendum, in the netherlands the decision is taken by the parliament. the referendum was proposed by three parties that were pro-constitutional and that sincerely expected voters to share their views. nevertheless, at a high turnout, 62% said "no". in belgium, germany, great britain, sweden and denmark, too, a majority of the electorate would very likely have said 'no' if they had been given the chance. the dutch prime minister balkenende is having a harder time here today than his italian counterpart did yesterday. at the beginning of this year, in accordance with the wishes of the voters, the following was agreed in the coalition agreement for his government - president, i would like to welcome prime minister balkenende to the european parliament. we have, of course, a rather massive 'no' in the netherlands on the one hand and a rather massive 'yes' in the european parliament on the other. i think it is to your credit that in your contribution and in the replies you have tried to go beyond the 'no' and the 'yes' and to see how we can reach an agreement. it is not a treaty, i agree with the idea of a treaty, that is disguised, but a treaty that answers some of the essential questions we are facing. sometimes that means even more than the original constitution says, sometimes a little less or different. i would like to ask you a few very specific questions to try to get this debate going a little further. you have said that you support the idea of criteria for services of general interest so that countries can regulate housing, health care or other things more themselves. are you prepared to include some kind of basis for this in a protocol or in the treaty, so that there is also some kind of legal basis? secondly, you said something about the charter. i understand that you are saying the following about the charter: we, the dutch government, agree with the content of the charter, we also want it to be binding, but it does not have to be incorporated in its entirety if the new treaty merely contains a text which creates that binding character. my question is: do you have a formula that can do that? then i agree with your reasoning. a third point: the commission has already done so. your plea for subsidiarity is to create more space, to keep what is local local. do you have a formula for tightening subsidiarity, and what is it, compared with the formula contained in the draft constitution? are you prepared to give more room to regions as a test bed? a fourth point. you say: i want to abolish a lot of vetoes and then you as the european parliament have the right to co-decision, you have explicitly stated that, but in some areas you do not want that. is it mainly about criminal law? perhaps you could clarify your formula there. you also said: "minister of foreign affairs", that's not what we call it. what do we call it? finally, mr president, if the netherlands can have a treaty and if the netherlands can have a strong treaty, then i hope that the prime minister will take the lead in his own country, with the leaders of the other coalition parties, to inform and involve the dutch people in good time, so that a real debate can provide a basis for positive support for europe. (applause from the left)
prime minister balkenende, you have shown the courage to come here to the lion's den to explain the dutch position. i think that you have, in your first answer just now, emphasized with conviction the european vocation of the netherlands. it is a step towards a new treaty, and that is important, because we need that new treaty. because right now, it's just not going well enough. it must be more transparent, it must be more democratic, and therefore more powers for the european parliament and, as you rightly say, for the national parliaments. it must be more effective so that we can really deal with climate change, with energy policy, with immigration and with strengthening europe's role in the world. i understand your principles and your commitment to these talks. in the end, a pragmatic approach is also needed. because it is in the european and national interest that we get out of this impasse. i have confidence that you can, because, in response to what was said earlier, i know you a little. you're not a nationalist, and i know you're not lying. one final point. you say you support the single market. i am glad you say that, but then the deed must be added to the word. if all 27 member states are going to make exceptions to the single market, and the netherlands is going in that direction, as the debate there shows, then there will be no single market left and that is not good for european consumers and not good for the european economy.
i have just noticed that in the debate you are supported by the sp, vlaams belang, haider's party, the polish league of families and the eurosceptics from sweden and denmark. i'm just going to mention it. the commitment of the government is a sad indication of emptiness and a lack of responsibility for the future of europe. because with a mini-convention, we get mini-democracy, mini-influence for the citizens, mini-responsibility against climate change and terrorism. a mini-treaty would make europe a political dwarf. the fundamental rights must be legally enforceable. and is the labour party in this government really prepared to sacrifice fundamental rights to avoid a referendum? the national parliaments must be more involved. but not with paralyzing red-card procedures, but by finally developing active interest and knowledge. they can now, but they're just letting it slide. finally, there is a lot of demagogic talk about a superstate. let's stop with that, because i didn't hear your party talk about that when you campaigned for the referendum on the constitution. of course, matters such as security measures against terrorism must be taken at european level. but not as it is now, in back rooms, but simply in public, democratically and with majority decision-making. that's not a superstate, that's a democracy. prime minister balkenende, the netherlands can do so much better than this in europe. please show that.
on behalf of the pse group mr president, please do not take on the role of election observation chief, because that is a different role. the socialist group will now take up the matter. the european election observation mission found that the elections in nigeria were not credible. unfortunately, despite the improved atmosphere in which freedom of expression was respected during the campaign and despite the courts playing a positive and independent role, the electoral process, and therefore the results, cannot be considered credible. the council has said it, the commissioner has said it, and that is indeed the situation. the nigerian elections were poorly organised, not transparent, there were far-reaching procedural irregularities and clear evidence of fraud. the process was far below international and regional standards for democratic elections. the point just made by the council: the failure to publish the results at the polling station level and beyond, and the total lack of evidence and transparency, is the most serious problem and remains the most serious problem, because it leaves nothing to be proved or contradicted and the election result is therefore not credible. that is - and we note that - simply one of the harshest judgments we have made as an election observation mission in the various countries. this was especially painful because after 2003 there was real hope among the people that there would be real improvements. there was a commitment from the people. a lot of people had worked hard for it, were involved in it, so it's extra hard. if you look at april 14th compared to april 21st: the 14th, the governor's race, you had a lot of hope, and the 21st, it was just a demoralized situation. that explains the very low turnout, despite the fine figures that were later published. our european conclusion should be explicit in order to remain committed. i share the view of the council and the commission on this point. because after all, it is the nigerian people we are talking about who deserve a better future and also serious elections. that means that we cannot pretend in our diplomatic relations that the newly appointed government was democratically elected. that is a very difficult point, of course. we can do business as usual, but we have to stay involved there, be active. business as usual would undermine the credibility of our european election observation missions, but even more so our own values that we stand for when we act in the world and in such a country. so we have to recognize that there is a democratic crisis at the moment. it takes some form in court, where a number of people object and try to prove that things are not right. it is also taking shape and can also take shape through working groups of the senate and the house of representatives, which themselves are examining a number of things and trying to come up with new proposals. and of course we have to see how people in nigeria themselves come up with proposals on how to have credible elections. i have said before that it is not for us to regulate this precisely, but that is the essence and it would be good if our resources could be used in this respect, preferably not with the federal government and the state, but with all those actors in society who can promote governance and democracy in this way.

president. - mr president, commissioner, mr president-in-office of the council, it has already been said: it is the first time that the cites conference has been held in europe. this is an opportunity to raise awareness of the protection of wildlife. cites is one of the most effective and practical instruments for protecting international wildlife. yet, the decline in biodiversity is alarming. for example, a large number of fish species are not doing well. over-exploitation is one of the causes. the demand for fish is huge and still rising. the sad result is that many deep-sea fish species are now seriously threatened and that in many developing countries the sea has already been emptied, by europe, or at least by european boats. sadly, the story is similar for tropical forests. despite all the campaigns, the demand for tropical wood remains high. in addition, the demand for agricultural land is also increasing because of the need for food and because of the increasing demand for biofuels. that's at the expense of ancient forests. the european initiative to list a large number of fish species as protected species deserves all the support, as does the initiative to list a number of tree species. but we have to ask ourselves how we can do better. worldwide, the pressure on nature is increasing. increasing wealth is one cause, but in a number of developing countries poverty is the driving force. so we have to change course and we have to realise that protecting nature also creates wealth and jobs. we need to take steps to ensure that trade is truly sustainable and that illegal trade is really tackled and disappears.
the commission's proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment is a very important one. that is why i wholeheartedly support the resolution. i would like to draw attention to one point, however, which has been raised several times this afternoon. it concerns the elephants in botswana and namibia. three quarters of africa's 400 000 elephants live in this region, posing risks to the population, nature and biodiversity. these countries have therefore put forward their own proposals to maintain the population and reduce the risks. unfortunately, the resolution rejects these proposals. on the one hand, it is understandable that people want to stop the trade in elephant products, and i agree with that, but on the other hand it is important to look at the situation in botswana and namibia, where there is such a huge increase in elephant numbers. we need to think this through. we cannot simply reject solutions without proposing alternatives.
