member of the commission. madam president, i followed with great interest the very lively debate in the committee on foreign affairs on the kashmir report by baroness nicholson. there is much food for thought in this report. it is too comprehensive for me to be able to respond in detail today, but i should like to reflect on some of the issues which it raises. the focus on kashmir is timely. on the political front, there have been many positive developments, which, for the first time in many years, give some hope that this long-standing issue is getting closer to a solution. both india and pakistan have indicated that the line of control could become a ‘soft border’. the european union has expressed its firm support for the reconciliation process between india and pakistan. but the road is still full of pitfalls. it is encouraging that the composite dialogue process remains on track despite terrorism. i was in delhi when the terrible attacks on the daily lahore-samjhota express train took place last february. it would indeed be tragic if the peace process could be taken hostage by terrorists. i see no alternative to both countries addressing their differences through dialogue. this will, hopefully, lead eventually to settlement of all bilateral issues. i also feel it is important that the kashmiris themselves have become more involved in the arrangements for the peace process. i would like to say a few words about the terrible earthquake that struck kashmir on the morning of 8 october 2005 and which, as baroness nicholson’s report describes, had a devastating impact on the lives of the kashmiris on both sides of the line of control, especially in pakistan-administered kashmir and the north west frontier province. i had the opportunity to visit the earthquake-affected areas in person when i visited pakistan to represent the european commission at the earthquake reconstruction conference in november that year. the european commission responded to this tragedy by providing humanitarian aid of eur 48 million and by committing, in december 2005, a eur 50 million programme on ‘earthquake recovery and reconstruction support to pakistan’, which covers rehabilitation of education and health facilities in the affected areas, an emergency education programme and community-based livelihood recovery schemes. thus, we are aiming to do what the report recommends, namely help the most vulnerable and restore economic activities in the affected area. this earthquake response comes in addition to other development programmes in pakistan under our country strategy. although the commission has been quick to react to this challenge, i nevertheless expect that the reconstruction effort will still take a number of years. the commission’s reconstruction programme will be implemented over a period of five years. as far as the northern areas of pakistan are concerned, the commission has been active there over many years in developing human resources especially, in improving the public school system and in cooperating with the aga khan education services. while implementing our programmes there, we have ensured that all communities – and government schools – benefit from project activities. before i conclude, i would like to thank baroness nicholson for the encouraging words she found to recognise the work of the commission’s delegations in islamabad and new delhi. this is much appreciated. i would just like to add that i would have loved to have stayed for the whole debate but, as communicated, i have to leave before the end due to long-standing commitments which i could not alter after parliament’s change to the agenda, but my colleague mrs grybauskaitė will take over and stay on in the debate.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0176/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0177/97). one of the consequences of this is that all the states in a large part of south asia were merged into one huge british colonial empire. in part, it was directly administered by colonial officials, but in part it was also indirectly administered. there, heirs to traditional rulers who had submitted to colonial supremacy ruled. in 1947 the choice of the future of the former states ruled by these princes was left to the preference of these princes themselves. they could join one of the two new states that became independent that year, dividing the colonial empire: there was a big secular india and a small muslim pakistan, which at that time also included bangladesh. restoration of independence of the former states was ruled out and a referendum on the choice between india and pakistan was not held. in most areas this has not created insurmountable problems. because the preference of the prince and most of the inhabitants coincided. in kashmir, that was not the case. the people would probably have chosen pakistan and the prince would have chosen india. this explains the problems of the last 60 years. india is the largest democracy in the world, but that democracy is seriously damaged by the fact that a large part of kashmir is part of india. without the support of the majority of the population, kashmir can only be held by the indian army and police. that is not the way democracy works in the country. india has great advantages as a secular democracy, which distinguishes it favorably from pakistan, where religious fanatics and military men have traditionally had far too much influence. the benefits of india are not being felt by the people of kashmir. in the contacts between the european union and india, we must not ignore this problem, but call for attention to it. i missed that in the rapporteur's original text. it was very much focused on three elements: the european union is also committed to the restoration of peace and stability in the region, the strengthening of relations between the european union and india and the development of better understanding between the leaders of india and pakistan through increased trade and transport. the people of kashmir, on the other hand, played no part. the longstanding desire for a referendum on the future and the international support for it were emphatically ignored. the final result now looks more balanced.
the european parliament has also called for the establishment of a european union-kashmir week in early june. given the human rights abuses in the area, this is an important initiative. we need help on both sides of the line of control. just think of the actions of militant groups both in the indian state of jammu and kashmir and in the pakistani part of azad kashmir. it is necessary that european aid be provided to all the victims inside kashmir. but humanitarian aid does not mean political interference. the united nations found that early in may, this month, relief operations were still difficult in the area. 83 employees had to be evacuated. local groups that had not delayed reconstruction projects would have turned the population against the united nations, a un spokesman said. the rapporteur also rightly points out that the european union has not been asked to act as an external mediator in the kashmir conflict. the position of saarc in the report, however, is totally contrary to this view. the eu must not lose sight of the value of international intervention, assistance and mediation. it is an omission that this value is so poorly recognised in this report. how do you intend to help all the kashmiris and the asian regional partners?
president. - my group supports the commission in this dispute with the polish government. the european union has taken an essential step towards the sustainable development of our continent with its environmental directives, such as the habitats directive and the birds directive. the guidelines have been a great success. the areas where they apply are often included in natura 2000. and this european network of protected natural areas of the member states is at the heart of eu environmental policy and for the conservation and restoration of biodiversity. it will greatly enhance the protection of nature and the environment. that sometimes goes against local and national pressures. politicians easily succumb to the pressure of short-term economic interests, rather than showing courage for a clean environment for future generations. of course, the via baltica is of great economic importance. no one will deny that. the opening up of northern and eastern europe, the linking of the baltic states to western europe: it is essential. the growth of prosperity and well-being in poland is in the interest of all europe. that is why billions of euros from the structural funds are being invested in the country and everyone understands that the people of the town of augustov want the dangerous, noisy traffic jams of lorries to be removed quickly. there's gonna have to be a detour around the city center. but at what cost? the rospuda valley is a unique area, a special protection area under the birds directive and, moreover, the area should be covered by the habitats directive because of its exceptional value. poland has an enormous wealth of nature, with landscapes of extraordinary beauty. this wealth is also crucial for poland's economic future. in the netherlands we had a similar situation not so long ago, with the construction - which has been stopped - of a new motorway through a section of the naardermeer. the naardermeer is the oldest nature reserve in our country. it's covered by the european bird directive. the situation is very similar to this polish nature reserve on the via baltica. the commission has rightly chosen to warn the polish government against the construction of this section of motorway. as a european member state, poland has an obligation to protect the rospuda valley. the polish government has proposed measures to compensate for the destruction of part of the natural area, but this compensation is not sufficient. the investigation of possible alternative routes has been incomplete. there are other routes that could be considered that would prevent irreparable damage to the area. the european parliament's committee on petitions will send a fact-finding mission in mid-june to investigate the complaints of polish citizens against the destruction of the valley. we'll hear all sides, we'll weigh all arguments, but one thing comes first: the via baltica should not affect one of europe's most beautiful natural areas. and the construction of this road must not be contrary to european environmental laws, which poland has also signed. the pact is the servant. the european union is not a party to the treaties.
president, most of the member states of the european union that joined in 2004 and 2007 have a well-developed rail network. unfortunately, the railway network has shrunk over the last 15 years and there have been cuts in maintenance. moreover, the old railway companies often do not receive the compensation from the state for their operating deficits that they had previously thought they could count on. this experience discourages new firms from participating in tendering procedures. this is likely to lead to a decline in the use of the rail network and the obsolescence of rolling stock. the great advantage these countries had over western europe in rail transport is thus lost. this also puts their environment at risk. less rail doesn't mean less traffic. to cope with this traffic, all attention is now being directed to investments in motorways. these roads were there 15 years ago, but then they were limited to the busiest routes, especially around the big cities. such roads are now being added at a rapid pace. in order to reduce costs, the interests of nature and the countryside are often not sufficiently taken into account. the shortest possible route through areas considered empty is preferred and there is no money for detours, tunnels or game viaducts. poland is not the first or the only country to have to deal with this. the same problem existed years ago in the czech republic on the prague-dresden route and in bulgaria on the sofia-thessaloniki route. the way in which valuable natural areas are being managed there is attracting attention not only at home but also abroad. international nature and environmental organizations are drawing attention to it. they also point out that the european union is becoming partly responsible for the destruction of nature if it co-finances such bad projects. i have been urging for many years that in such cases we should refuse any responsibility. on 2 july 2002, in reply to my written questions, commissioner wallström stated that poland should change its plans for the via baltica. i would remind you that the original plan provided for the via baltica to go beyond this vulnerable area, but that those plans were later revised to better serve other cities. the return to the original plan, which was developed long ago, must be the aim of the action of the european union.
it is a good thing that despite the increase in prosperity and the urbanisation that accompanies it, we still have valuable natural areas within the borders of the european union. these are part of the natura 2000 network. this network ensures that nature in the european union is given a protected status, which is very important. for example, it contributes to the maintenance of biodiversity. we have just this week approved the proposal put forward by mr adamou. it is a fight for the conservation of biodiversity. natura 2000 status also ensures that the natural areas remain guaranteed for our descendants. at least, it should be. not every government seems to realize this, as we have seen over the past year. the polish government wants to build the new road, the via baltica, through the natura 2000 area of the rospuda valley. i myself was with a delegation from the environment committee in the rospuda valley and saw firsthand how unique and valuable this polish natural area is. it is absolutely incomprehensible to me that the polish minister for the environment should be the one to support this project when there are good alternatives available. he should be the one to see the disastrous effects of this project. to compensate for the construction of the road, a million trees were to be planted north of the area. but this also has adverse effects on nature. i know that the issue is very sensitive in poland. the road safety aspect requires that the via baltica be built quickly, but in a well-designed way with the least possible negative impact on the environment. i am glad that the commission has issued warnings to the polish government and that it has taken the matter to the european court of justice when the polish government did not comply with its demands. the court's ruling on 18 april ordering the polish government to stop the compensatory measures immediately is hopefully a signal for the future. a signal that cannot be easily diverted by member states to valuable natural areas.
author. syria is a recurring item on our agenda. the european union is keen to establish good and intensive relations with that country in the framework of its neighbourhood policy with the mediterranean countries. stability in syria is also appreciated because of the chaos in neighbouring iraq. at the same time, syria is a dictatorship that does not allow internal opposition. it is also an unpleasant country for christians and kurds, to say the least. it is not a theocratic state that is positive towards muslim fundamentalism, but it does regard the theocratic dictatorship in iran as an ally. in lebanon, the country supports lebanese and palestinian muslim fundamentalists as long as they can contribute to syria's greater control over that small neighboring country. lebanon is seen as an area unjustly separated from syria by european intervention. the conflict with israel is being carefully cultivated because the tensions it creates could contribute to the syrians considering the survival of the regime the lesser of two evils. there's no sign of improvement. both an aggressive attitude towards syria and the involvement of this regime in friendly relations are counterproductive. only support for syrian refugees and syrian oppositionists in prison and exile can help make things better in the long run. my final point, mr president: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
the question of whether this very large area with its very different peoples should become independent as one state was already being discussed when the present state of sudan was still known as anglo-egyptian sudan. a united state would be the state of the arab population in the north, east and centre. the black muslim population in the west and the black christian and animist population in the south would be hard put on an equal footing. these then much less developed areas were far from the sea and received little international attention. in the end, no one took them into account. after years of war, a compromise seems to have been reached for the non-islamic south, which has been granted self-government and representation in the central government. as we know, that does not apply to western darfur. the fundamentalist attitude is rampant in the predominantly arab population, especially in the administrative authorities and the judiciary. death sentences for adultery and even the use of stoning are now the worst of these. the resolution rightly draws attention to international agreements and the need to abolish the death penalty. we must continue to pay attention to this, because a terrible situation is developing in sudan.
author. mr president, the appointment of the head of the world bank should not be the prerogative of the leader of the state with the largest share of the stock. that has been the norm so far. the candidate on behalf of the united states did not need to be compared with other candidates and there was no need to weigh candidates. and who the candidate of the united states is is decided by one man. wolfowitz was not the choice of the most involved in the world bank, but only that of the american president bush. for bush he was a loyal ally, for others a wreckage maker who is always trying in vain to push through a wrong policy. he was extremely controversial when he joined the world bank and he is even more controversial now that he is leaving. the promotion of his girlfriend was not an accidental mistake, but the reason to put an end to his self-serving and unacceptable way of working. in choosing his successor, such mistakes must not be repeated. a transparent appointment procedure with public hearings for candidates is badly needed.

the report by mr barón crespo and mr brok is characterized by great internal tension and ambiguity. on the one hand, the rapporteurs acknowledge - albeit with the greatest possible reluctance - the need to amend the constitution. i am therefore pleased to see that even the european parliament is becoming aware of this reality. on the other hand, i am disappointed to note that paragraph 6 - as mr wurtz has already said - shows how great, or rather how little, their real willingness to amend the constitution is: the content of the constitution cannot be changed, only the packaging. i readily admit that it is the members of the council (including my own prime minister) who give rise to the idea that cosmetic amendment of the constitution would be acceptable. i would like to ask the commissioner to give us a clear answer to this question. the council is giving increasing signals that it will indeed discuss the content. if the european parliament really wants to contribute to a successful igc, as the rapporteurs have pointed out in their report on several occasions, it must be prepared to compromise on the content, otherwise the european parliament will only play a marginal role in the next igc. that is a scenario which will not appeal to me or to the two rapporteurs.
i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteurs for their efforts. the report is a two-sided report. it is strongly in favour of the core elements of the constitutional treaty, but it also takes account of developments in recent years, although it could perhaps have been more generous. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of europe. it is very important that this parliament too should take account of this criticism. but fortunately, there is now a growing support in europe for the idea that things should be different. there is no constitution. the report can count on our support, although we have problems with some passages. we support the report because it defends the main points of the constitutional treaty. but this report also opens the door to change - although some members here deny this: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but that role must be balanced with respect for the role of this parliament. there is no such thing as a europe on our own terms. we have to come together and under the leadership of a woman, we have to do it. i wish angela merkel, but also our own hans-gert poettering and commission president barosso every success and we must all support them in bringing this to a good solution.
the european union must be able to take action in the face of the challenges of the 21st century. the dutch no in 2005 was not a no to europe: 72% of the dutch think that european cooperation is a good thing. it would therefore be a disaster for the netherlands if we were to continue to be stuck in a no without an alternative, as the sp is threatening to do. that is why i have put forward my five-point plan for a new treaty to justify the dutch 'no' and to get the majority of the dutch back on the 'yes' side. one: the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european parliament is represented in the european parliament, and that the european parliament is represented in the european parliament. two , you 're not . a more social europe. include a social clause which states that public and semi-public services are not subordinated to the market but can be arranged according to the wishes of the national member states and regions. make the charter of fundamental rights binding on the people. three , please . the european union is committed to a europe of the people and the regions through greater decentralisation and strengthened subsidiarity. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. give european citizens the right to petition. fourth . the commission is also proposing to include in the new treaty stricter accession criteria to prevent fraud. the european parliament must be the first to decide on any further enlargement. five , please . the new treaty will be considerably shorter by incorporating all the simplifications by reference, which will save 322 articles. and let it be a treaty indeed, because the constitution gave the dutch the idea that they were losing their own constitution. in this way the netherlands can regain its place at the forefront of europe. that's where we belong, for our own sake and for our own ideals.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, security and defence policy, on the situation in the middle east. the first timid steps have been taken. however, in 2007 there is still a lot of noise about who is responsible for rescuing drowning people, legally or illegally, and when. it is astonishing that moral and legal duty is not apparently taken as seriously by everyone. of course, as martine roure has just said, malta is not having an easy time dealing with the large influx of illegal immigrants. no one will deny that. the european union must therefore act quickly to achieve the much-desired and much-talked-about solidarity. the rapid introduction of a burden-sharing mechanism therefore seems appropriate. but this fact in no way justifies the way malta feels it should deal with its moral and legal obligation. we are talking about an eu member state and that makes us as a union responsible. while malta and libya cannot agree on the exact location of the drowning, 27 people are hanging from a fishing net for three days. unfortunately, this example is not stand alone. - that is absurd. as we know, libya is not really the paradise of human rights or humanistic views, far from it. it is a grave scandal that we as a union are threatening to adopt a similar attitude towards these drowning people. yesterday i found in my minutes for the transport committee a study on the status of illegal migrants at sea. the actions proposed in this study should be implemented as a matter of priority. commissioner frattini, you will have to join forces with commissioner barrot. money alone is not the answer. you know very well that the council is the key when it comes to the conditions for the return of illegal immigrants. at the same time, and above all, let it be clear that no one, no one, can and must hide behind so-called legal ambiguities in, for example, the international law of the sea. where there is a political will, there is a way. i would like to conclude by saying to the council: berlin declarations, honourable council presidents, you may record them until you weigh an ounce. european values are at the heart of this. but if nothing is done about it in practice, then the reality is quite different and you, as president of the council, are putting the credibility of the european union on the line. put to work the fine words you so love to proclaim, as you did just now. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. do what you promised us. with all due respect, a debate next week is great, but it's really not good enough. the problem is not new. action is necessary and immediate.
president, i am very pleased that the german presidency has made data protection in the third pillar one of its priorities. however, i am less pleased if i look at the various proposals that have been circulated. i would urge the council to take on board not only the recommendations of the european parliament, but also the criticisms and concerns expressed by the european data protection supervisor. first of all, i am a bit mystified by the fact that we still seem to have 27 different regimes, which creates a lot of uncertainty for citizens and businesses. secondly, there are so many vague and open definitions, for example on purpose limitation; there are so many exceptions to citizens’ rights and to safeguards, that in the end it seems that this proposal will undermine the position of citizens rather than strengthen it. to give an example, article 7 on the exchange of sensitive data such as political preference, medical data or data on sex life are supposed to be exchanged ‘only when strictly necessary and with adequate safeguards’. what does that mean? who is to define what is strictly necessary? what are the criteria? what are the adequate safeguards? that is dangerously open. and a final question to which we have never had a straight answer: we know that the council and the commission are having talks with the us on a transatlantic regime for data protection. that in itself is a good thing, but right up to now and despite repeated questions, it is not clear to me what the basis is for those talks. if the basis is the low standards in your proposal for eu data protection in the third pillar, then i am very concerned about the rights of european citizens in our dealings with the us.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. mr president, on behalf of the alde group, first of all, we would like to thank the rapporteur. i admire her for finding a way through the many amendments that have been tabled. we in the alde group can broadly support the result and that means we can broadly support the commission's proposals. strengthening producer organizations is a good thing. the market for horticultural products is increasingly dominated by large supermarkets and other trade organizations and it is necessary for producers to unite to counterbalance this. the majority of the alde group disagrees with the rapporteur and the committee on agriculture on the already much-debated crisis fund and crisis management in general. we think the commission's proposals are better. first of all, i wouldn't know where the money was supposed to come from either. but even in the long term it is impossible to assume that any government could be responsible for crisis management. there will always be a contribution from the public authorities - that is foreseen - but the main responsibility will have to lie with the producers. if we encourage crisis management through the producer organisation, this will also attract more members and at the same time it may also mean that the power of producer organisations vis-à-vis traders and supermarkets will be strengthened. so on that point we shall vote differently from what the committee on agriculture has proposed. i would like to make another point about a quality policy. the commissioner knows that this is a subject which is very close to my heart. i was not entirely optimistic about this after the last conference, but i am encouraged by the fact that an amendment by the alde group in the committee on agriculture on the need for a quality label for horticultural products was unanimously adopted by all the parties. that's pretty unique. so perhaps with the support of the committee on agriculture the commissioner can think again about a quality policy for certain agricultural products, because we still think it is necessary. i do not know what the latest developments are in the field of school fruit, but we found the commissioner's thoughts on this point sympathetic.

the german presidency is right to listen to the differences of opinion that have been expressed in europe on the constitution. i hope, however, that this does not mean that the backsliders will be taking the lead instead of the forwards. i hope that does not mean that we are giving in to paralysis, but that we are choosing progress. i hope that doesn't mean we're going to be guided by fear, but that we're going to be guided by ambition. i have heard people say that the constitution was too ambitious. but how can you be that ambitious when you look at the challenges we face in the 21st century? and if we are ambitious, it doesn't mean we aspire to a superstate. i wish we could stop using that terminology. the term superstate is a load of crap. there's talk of a mini-treaty. what's a mini-covenant for? for a small europe? for a political midget? i hope that you will try to keep the charter of fundamental rights in it. fundamental rights are not a purely legal issue, but the omission of the charter is also a political signal, even if fundamental rights become legally enforceable. i hope that no new paralysing vetoes will be introduced before national parliaments. finally, it is often said that we listen to what the citizens have to say, especially in the netherlands and france - the netherlands, my own country. one of the things that the dutch people have said is that they are tired of decisions being made behind their backs and over their heads behind closed doors. is it not strange that we are choosing to hold an igc behind closed doors? for confessional work? for bilateral negotiations? i hope that the presidency will have the courage to do something revolutionary and the courage to propose that the next igc be held in public.
mr president, last week seven people, including a dutchman, mr dick nicolaas, and a frenchman, mr serge atlaoui, were sentenced to death in jakarta for the manufacture of xtc and methamphetamine. the european union has already called on jakarta to stop issuing death sentences and to refrain from carrying out executions. it shows, of course, that involvement in drug trafficking is a big deal. you just don't want to start that. but the death penalty is in no way justifiable, and that is why the european union has been advocating it for years. mr president, various statistics indicate that things are moving in the right direction. the number of executions worldwide has fallen from 2,148 in 2005 to 1,591 last year. but it is estimated that there are still 20,000 people on death row worldwide. it is therefore of the utmost importance that europe continues to pull the cart and use all the means at its disposal to bring more countries over the moratorium line. this is particularly true in south east asia, where indonesia suspended the moratorium on the death penalty in 2004, but the example of the philippines, which recently abolished the death penalty, deserves to be followed, especially there.
president. - the debate on the schnellhardt report is dominated by the debate on vodka. that is a pity, because it is a report which in many ways says so many other things which are terribly important for europe and for this industry in europe. i would therefore like to deal first with some of the other points. the directive is, of course, primarily concerned with the quality of the european beverage industry. it also says something about europe, because wherever you go in the world, you see european spirits, european spirits, in all kinds of forms for sale and with a recognition of the quality that we produce in europe. this is important in a situation where not everything that is said about the european union is always positive. this is something that europe is very different from. the rapporteur has done just that, by strengthening this very element. in fact, in his original report he was even more concerned than in the compromise which was finally reached with the council. i think that's a formidable achievement and i think that's something we should hold on to. distilled is a craft, a truly old-fashioned craft. i have recently visited some gin distilleries in my own country. it's a craft, it's an art to make these products in a good way and in a way that keeps the quality constant. that is without doubt the most important point of this report. we must not simply add sugar or flavourings, but we must define these drinks clearly, even very strictly, even at the request of industry. this also gives consumers good information about the product. they know what they are buying and they can be sure that it is of good quality. the directive also enables the european beverage industry to remain competitive internationally thanks to this quality. it also says something about europe, and it does say something about the regional attachment to many of these kinds of drinks. in this respect, this directive is almost a microcosm of the european union, it shows the diversity of the european union and how we can all be european together, precisely in that diversity. that is also very important to me. now, a word about the vodka. the rapporteur has tried to reach a compromise on this point, which i myself wholeheartedly supported in the debate. i think he has made a courageous attempt to try to strike a fair compromise between the old vodka industry in the european union, which existed at the time the definition was made - before sweden, finland, poland and all the other countries had joined the union - and those newer member states, and i think he has done a reasonable thing. my group is in favour of a much stricter definition of vodka, and i must say that it is doing so with good reason. i therefore think it is good that this is on the table, so that it is part of the discussion. firstly, this consumer information: my group believes that every consumer should be able to accept that vodka is made from potatoes or from cereals, that is the essence, perhaps from sugar beet. the consumer must be able to buy it from the start, in accordance with the wishes of the majority of my group. but it goes beyond that. it's not just about the product. there is also a debate about originality and about the identity of the countries where it is made. because what would we have done if this discussion with these countries had been played out before? what if we had said: for the purpose of consumer information, you may also place on the market cognac made from potatoes or whiskey made from grapes. no, of course we would never have done that and my group wants us to be consistent on the vodka issue as well. of course there's something to that. i would have liked to have had agreement at first reading, and i am glad that this argument is now on the table, and i support my group's amendment in that respect.
i would like to congratulate mr schnellhardt on the compromise he has presented. it took a while, but with this result, there's a good chance of a first-reading agreement. i am glad that the point i raised in the debate in the committee on the environment has been accepted. it concerns the vague passage of the impression that a certain age is mentioned on the label. i am glad that that passage has now been deleted, because that is how the survival of the young and old dutch genever is guaranteed. the designation of 'genever' refers to the method of preparation of the drink and not to its age. i would like to make a brief comment on the vodka. as a dutchman, i am not used to drinking vodka very often, but i do sympathize with my eastern european colleagues. i have therefore considered supporting the definition of vodka, which would allow only potatoes, cereals and sugar beet to be used as raw materials. i shall give this matter some serious thought after the rapporteur has called for it.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on foreign affairs. madam president, listening to the rapporteur and also to mrs kroes, the commissioner, i must say that i am a little bit in between. i share the rapporteur's concerns, but i also welcome the commissioner's pragmatism, because it is true that economic relations between the eu and russia are flourishing. as i said, the eu is russia's most important trading partner and trade is growing, as is investment. last weekend's st petersburg economic forum was a good deal and reportedly showed little of the chill that dominated the eu-russia summit in may. but we cannot just dissociate politics from economics, and on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs i have looked particularly at the political dimension of trade and economic relations between the european union and russia. i wanted to emphasize the political significance of economic relations. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the negotiations. but that is not going to happen by itself. energy is perhaps the most important example. on the one hand, it is one of the main pillars of the special political bond between the european union and russia, but at the same time the current difficulties indicate that a number of agreements are urgently needed. in this context, we support russia's accession to the world trade organisation. it would make a particularly important contribution to the transparency of russian market rules and would greatly improve the investment climate. at the same time, it would bind russia to a set of global rules, reducing the risk of trade policy being used as a political weapon. the russians themselves do realise that they have an interest in good trade relations with the european union. economic relations provide a stable basis for political relations and there is still enormous potential for growth. this will also help russia to realise that it will also have to find common ground in europe on the political level. not only in trade, but also in human rights, democracy and the latent conflicts between our neighbours. it is absolutely necessary to find a balance here. the eu should enshrine these agreements in an improved agreement that will replace the current partnership and cooperation agreement. this is another example of the close relationship between economics and politics. finally, the negotiations on this subject are stalled by a trade conflict which is translating politically into a conflict between the european union and russia. in short, we are in favour of a pragmatic approach, but the political cannot be separated from the economic, even when it comes to trade relations.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. russia is - as the rapporteur has already said - one of our most important trading partners and she has shown this with figures. however, there is also a lot of potential in our relations with russia. in fact, if you look at trade relations, there's a paradox: the eu has a number of member states with good bilateral relations, but eu-russia relations need to be improved. both partners must play a role in this. the member states must therefore find a common response to the challenges. that is why, mr president, the negotiations on a new partnership agreement must begin as soon as possible. a new treaty based on the experience of the last decade and also shaping a new dialogue for the years to come. there are a number of important elements that should be there: reciprocity and transparency. president putin said two weeks ago at the st. petersburg economic forum: economic partners can feel a sense of security only when they have reliable and predictable relations. yes, mr president, but our entrepreneurs also want to be able to do business in a clear and legal framework. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union. i am therefore very interested - and perhaps the commissioner can say something about this - in the new law on foreign investment. mr president, enhanced economic cooperation can also contribute to a more diversified economic development in russia itself. president, then the wto. i'm glad we did that. it's the only way to deal with counterfeiting, too. everything has been said about energy, so that's why i'm stopping now. i would just like to express my appreciation to mrs quisthoudt-rowohl, who has done an excellent job with her colleagues.
the commission's analysis of the problems of economic and commercial relations with russia is fair. however, this report does not provide a satisfactory answer to the question of how europe should respond to the growing politicisation of the russian economy. in the absence of a clear answer, russia continues to refer to bilateral relations with individual member states. the enthusiasm with which these member states are cooperating betrays not only their inability to achieve a single european policy, but also their political unwillingness to do so. the report criticises russia's lack of anticipation of wto rules. in that light, i am surprised that paragraph 15 already mentions a possible free trade agreement. i believe that after russia's accession to the wto a period of consolidation should be observed in order to analyse the consequences of this accession. the commission is right to devote its efforts to such an analysis rather than considering a free trade agreement now.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, who is absent, for having drafted an excellent report which i believe has received broad support in our committee. i also commend the work of mrs kroes, because mrs berès has just said very rightly that competition policy is not an end in itself, and i think that the european commission has, under mrs kroes, taken a new pragmatic approach, which we greatly appreciate. it is sometimes forgotten, but competition rules are not there to bully companies or to annoy national governments; competition rules exist to ensure that everyone can operate under the same rules and with the same opportunities in the internal market; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. so we must not compete with each other, we must ensure that we have a well-functioning internal market. i too am a strong supporter of sectoral inquiries and when it comes to energy policy you have the support of this parliament. but i'd like to make a note. at the moment, everyone is pushing from the member states to prepare for the summit next weekend, and there too, energy policy is one of the new spearheads on the agenda. one of the few positive points in the whole discussion. at the same time, i see that while the leaders of national governments say 'we must have a common energy policy', they are not prepared to give up their protection of the national champions. perhaps they should put into practice what they profess with their mouths. as regards state aid: as rapporteur on state aid for public services and innovation, we have had the opportunity to discuss this before. i am very curious to see whether there is already a first evaluation of the impact of these exemption regulations and whether there is more generally an evaluation of the effectiveness, or not, of state aid. finally, as regards the matching clause. we've been over that, too. of course, it is a problem when generous state aid is made available outside the european union. at the same time, i would prefer to strengthen european competitiveness by other means. i think it is very inappropriate to start giving very generous state aid, subsidies, when we have no other measures in place such as patents, flexibility of the labour market, opening up the market for services and other such things; we cannot correct these kinds of mistakes with state aid.

the so-called palestinian unity government of fatah and hamas collapsed in blood last week. the house is therefore faced with two palestinian governments which deny each other's right to exist. the council and the commission have now made a clear choice for the crisis cabinet of salam fayyad in the west bank. i would like to support this position in its entirety, because unlike the fatah movement led by mahmoud abbas, the hamas leadership inside and outside the gaza strip persists in denying israel's right to exist. hamas opposes a two-state solution to the israeli-palestinian conflict on purely religious grounds. the question mr schulz has to look into again is the religious basis of hamas. the fact that this destructive attitude of hamas is being actively encouraged by the islamic republic of iran and syria is reasonable in view of the permanent regional destabilisation policy of damascus and tehran. on the other hand, i find it quite astonishing that colleagues or even groups in this house want to trust and accept hamas, which is the archenemy of the state of israel, as an equal partner in the dialogue. to this day, in fact. let us not allow the politically-sound two-state solution to become unbelievable lip service in this house. in the meantime, i warmly wish the palestinian people a selfless leadership that demonstrates a strong internal governance and a sincere external modus vivendi with israel. the emergence of the fayyad emergency cabinet provides that opportunity. i am counting on the helpful, encouraging and controlling hands of all the european institutions to make this happen.
in writing. as a member of the committee of inquiry, i have dealt in particular with the quality aspects of regulation. the committee of inquiry found that the commission, while looking at formal transposition, did not pay sufficient attention to implementation. is legislation transposed correctly and is it transposed in such a way that its application is consistent with the objective of european legislation? we as members of the european parliament must also be much more aware of what happens in the member states with legislation adopted in this parliament. i think that the rapporteur responsible should keep an eye on what happens next in the member states. the rapporteur must ring the bell when things go wrong and activate the commission where necessary. the national parliaments should also be involved. there is a serious lack of structured cooperation between member states in the field of national supervisory authorities. this is not only the case in financial market supervision, but also in many other areas. the number of independent supervisors in the member states is increasing. the big question is who's watching over these regulators. and who oversees the quality of cross-border cooperation between the supervisors? is there not a very important role for the european commission in this?
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0210/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0203 - c4-0210/97). they are ambitious plans to reduce world poverty by 2015. at the end of this month we will be halfway through, but unfortunately we are still far from halfway through in achieving those objectives. the development agenda is lagging far behind. there are still too many people dying of hunger, too many children without access to education, too many women disadvantaged and too many victims of aids, malaria and tuberculosis. i could go on like this for a while, unfortunately. it is absolutely essential that the quality and quantity of aid be improved and much depends on the political will in the countries. of course, all countries must honour their pledge to set aside 0.7% of their gross national product for development aid. but it's not just about money. the main issues are better and more effective aid and better coordination of aid. phantom aid, where money is mainly spent on consultants, evaluation reports and studies, should be reduced. i would also like to raise a number of points on behalf of our group. first of all, i would like to stress the importance of transparency in financial flows. it should be clear where the money is going. secondly, debt relief. debt is a big problem for many countries. however, debt relief cannot and should not be a reward for government mismanagement. therefore, debt relief can only be granted under strict conditions such as good governance and transparency. there must be a guarantee that the funds released are actually spent on poverty reduction. thirdly, i would like to stress the importance of trade. trade and the opening up of markets can be a great engine for economic growth under certain conditions. countries must be able to count on our support in this. as far as intellectual property rights are concerned, i would like to say: let's not throw the baby out with the bath water. it must remain attractive for industry to continue to carry out research into poverty eradication. a certain degree of protection of intellectual property rights is necessary for this. finally, sexual and reproductive health. many women in developing countries face high risks, even life-threatening risks, during and after pregnancy. a lot of women die from it unnecessarily. help in this is extremely urgent. that is why we are in favour of more aid to these women.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). the socialist group is very disappointed in this. in 2000, 191 world leaders committed themselves to the millennium declaration to end extreme poverty worldwide by 2015 and to improve the health and well-being of the poorest groups. right now, one in five of the world's citizens lack access to basic social services like education and clean drinking water. in exactly two and a half weeks, we will be halfway to the mdg deadline. the excellent report by glenys kinnock makes the point. we fully support the report. the last seven and a half years have seen intensive work towards these objectives. a number of major successes have been achieved. in asia in particular, there has been a sharp fall in poverty. the number of people living on less than a dollar a day has fallen by more than a quarter of a billion since 1990. in latin america too, a clear upward trend is being observed. in both regions, the number of malnourished children has fallen dramatically. infant mortality has been reduced by many percent. hundreds of thousands of people in latin america and asia have been lifted out of poverty. that is something that these regions and the whole world can be proud of. what's still going wrong in asia and latin america is the huge disparities between the rich and the poor. we need to work together on strategies for a fairer distribution on behalf of the ppe-de group, i would like to congratulate mr cornillet on his report on the activities of the acp-ep joint parliamentary assembly in 2006. it was another fruitful year. the main subjects discussed were energy, tourism, water in the acp countries, the promotion of peace and security, problems of fisheries in the countries and, of course, the epas, the new trade agreements. the formula of this parliamentary delegation, mr president, remains special and unique. it is important that 77 meps and 77 acp parliamentarians - one from each acp country - meet a few times a year to discuss development policy. the preparation, which is a co-production between european and acp countries, is also included. it also remains extremely important for our parliamentary work, because we are not only talking about them, but also with them when it comes to implementing the cotonou convention and development policy in general. this is what the europe-africa strategy should have been from the outset. but it was too one-sidedly drawn up from a european perspective. fortunately, a joint strategy of the european union and the african union for the development of africa is now emerging. this one is a priority, and rightly so. poverty is the greatest and most worrying in africa. it is good to see that the joint parliamentary assembly is growing and that the dialogue between the acp countries and the european union is gaining momentum and that it is becoming increasingly possible to discuss sensitive issues such as corruption or worrying situations, such as in the horn of africa and sudan. zimbabwe remains a difficult issue. the situation there is unacceptable and we must do everything possible to protect the position of civilians there and to stop the violation of human rights. next week we meet again, this time in germany, in wiesbaden. i look forward to this meeting and hope that we can have a good dialogue again, including on difficult issues.
the rapporteur. - mr president, to say that this is a difficult dossier is an understatement. we've been working on nine articles for almost two years. european agreements on supplementary pensions are needed. i'll give you three reasons. first of all: the first pillar, statutory pensions, is increasingly under pressure from deforestation and ageing. it is therefore important that second pillar pensions are properly regulated. at present, 1 in 10 workers in europe has a supplementary pension. in the united kingdom and germany, the figure is well over 50%, and in denmark, sweden and the netherlands, as many as 90% of employees are members of a supplementary pension scheme. this is reason enough to encourage member states and also the social partners to take up their responsibilities and start building a system of good old age provision. the second reason, mr president, is that i meet almost daily people who have changed jobs and are then punished for exercising their right to mobility; they have built up a pension, they have paid premiums, but if the capital does not change, it is of no use for the old age provision. this is the problem that this directive seeks to tackle. thirdly: in the context of lisbon, we are saying that we need to encourage further the mobility of workers. we all want to see this principle, but we have problems with its implementation. my aim was and is to come up with legislation that does something, including for workers, that is feasible for member states and the social partners and therefore also offers sufficient opportunities for supplementary pension scheme managers to adapt to it. legislation that only has exceptions or a law that does not apply to all member states and therefore does not enter into force until 2018 is pointless. we have to deal with the problems now before we have to deal with them later. i would like to thank mr ettl and mr cocilovo, the other shadow rapporteurs on social affairs, mrs lulling for the opinion, and also the rapporteurs and shadow rapporteurs on previous pension dossiers, othmar karas and ieke van den burg. we are united in our efforts to achieve a result and we also take responsibility for this report, the report of the committee on social affairs. we can make progress on this basis without jeopardizing the existing systems, and i say this to the presidency. this directive is also about minimum standards. there is nothing to prevent the social partners from providing more protection in an agreement. i'll get to the point quickly. acquisition the conditions of acquisition set by parliament have implications for some systems. the time needed for adaptation is the additional five years provided for in amendment no 22. the dormant rights: the german presidency has devised the current formula, so we shall vote on a basic principle which the member states can implement in their own way. the transfer: it was very soon clear that that bridge was too far; we didn't let it come back. information to be provided by: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive. so, mr president, member states may need more time to adapt their systems. parliament gives them that time. for both acquisition and fair treatment of dormant rights, we give member states until 2013 to adapt their systems together with the social partners. ladies and gentlemen, after two years of almost continuous consultation, it is now time for parliament to define its position. i can still say that the signals i have received, also from the forthcoming portuguese presidency and from my own member state, the netherlands, are absolutely positive, because this report offers many points of reference for the second reading. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). it's not finished today. we will negotiate with the council and we will continue to act as constructively as possible as parliament and as rapporteur to find a solution, because it is really necessary.
i would like to congratulate mrs oomen-ruijten on her report. the taking of supplementary pension rights is not a matter of course. if the netherlands - a forerunner - is already considering a veto on new pension legislation in the pension debate, that certainly raises some questions. it is essential that new steps are taken to ensure better coordination of the different pension systems. in addition to coordination, the financial sustainability of different systems is a particular concern. it is clear that not everyone can agree with this report, because in relation to the commission's proposal the rapporteur proposes that no more exceptions should be allowed in the transfer. i do have one more question. if the exemption from article 9 (3) is removed, will this provide an incentive for member states to switch to a capitalised pension system? it should be. the european union, in which people are free to work in different member states, should have an appropriate social security system. the minimum requirement is to take home accumulated pension rights.
president. - i have a few remarks to make. firstly, the german presidency's analysis of why it was so difficult to bring this dossier to a successful conclusion. i think that was missing the fact that exceptions had been made from the start, in particular by your own country, and that this set in motion a process which led to other countries also making exceptions and which eventually led to nothing remaining in the directive. i think that should be mentioned in the analysis. secondly, the role of employers and social partners and of the pension funds themselves. i am also very much in favour of them taking the necessary steps themselves, but as commissioner špidla has already said, they have missed their turn. i do think that if we adopt this directive now without that element of transfer of value, which will be an incentive, they will take it up again themselves and i would like to make that signal sound again very strongly. i was also against article 6, as formulated by the commission, providing an individual right to transfer value, but it has to be regulated, so the field has to be set in motion. the rights of the sleeper: mr mann, it is not that the netherlands did not want that part because it would not be financiable. we have done that very well in our own country: the amount of the premium is calculated on the basis of the average of the two years' duration of the insurance contract. we are concerned that dutch people who have worked in germany or other countries and have been mobile have nothing to do with the rights they have acquired there. that's not fair treatment. i think that's the importance of article 5 on sleeping rights; i am therefore very disappointed that both the german presidency and mr mann are now trying to arrange these rights for future sleeping rights only. the argument of retroactive force is really nonsense. pension rights are determined each year, including for active members. for sleeping participants, this can happen in exactly the same way. that has nothing to do with retroactive force at all, that is simply adjusting arrangements and that is a fair share of what they should have themselves. so, full support for the report by mrs oomen-ruijten and also for our part, i hope that it will not remain, like the temporary workers directive, in the swamp mentioned by mr cocilovo, but will now be really caught up by the council.
mr president, i also have to apologise to my cypriot and maltese colleagues for spoiling this historical moment of accession to the eurozone with this debate on our institutional problems with dealing with accession issues. however, it does not take anything away from our support for their adoption of the euro and for both mr langen’s reports. we did not want to burden the accession of these two countries with the issue of the future procedure, but i hope that we can come to an agreement with the commission and the council today to ensure that, in future, there is a better procedure and a more serious opportunity for parliament to give its opinion on this issue. that is why we have drawn up this non-legislative motion for a resolution on the procedure. we would like to be invited to meet with the other two institutions to produce an interinstitutional agreement on several issues, which are listed in our motion for a resolution. this text calls for a pre-announcement system, whereby those member states wishing to accede notify us in advance so that we can begin studying the situation, and concerns the assessment of the quality of the statistics and data provided. my last point concerns the scheduling of the dates for the formal procedure. we recognise that everything has to take place in a very short period of time. however, if we are better prepared then we will have a better opportunity for serious discussion. the commissioner said that he was willing to sit together with us to conclude an interinstitutional agreement of this kind. i would like to ask mr gloser to give us that commitment as well, on behalf of the council, the german presidency and future presidencies.

mr president, about a million people are injured every year by guns, machine guns or grenade launchers. the scale of gun violence worldwide is enormous, as is the human suffering. in recent armed conflicts, small arms and light weapons are often the only weapons used. in southern africa alone, there are some 30 million small arms in circulation. in the middle east, estimates range from 60 million to 110 million. the majority of those weapons are made and sold legally. international treaties to control biological, chemical and nuclear weapons and their proliferation have existed for many decades, but despite the enormous number of casualties, the sale and trade of conventional weapons have not succeeded; the result is human rights violations, protracted wars and increasingly impoverished countries. the vast majority of these weapons are produced by five rich countries. in 2005, these were russia, the usa, france, germany and the uk; together they are responsible for 81% of the world arms trade. more than two thirds of the arms trade is directed to africa, asia, the middle east and latin america. not only are lives being destroyed, but economies, infrastructure, health care and education are also being destroyed; in northern uganda alone, 2,500 children have been out of school in the last 20 years of gun violence. president, the un resolution of 6 december 2006 sets out the basis for a convention against this free movement and control of the whole. of course, there are some things that are of the utmost importance in order to control conventional weapons. i therefore call on the council - and i also understand from president gloser that something is happening - to get a massive, rapid and positive response from the european side and to address on our part the serious shortcomings in the application of the european code of conduct of 1998. eu arms are still finding their way smoothly into countries such as sudan, china and sierra leone. it is time to make this code legally binding. that would be a great stepping stone to the u.n. process.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, first of all i note that the council presidency is absent during this important debate, which is remarkable as it was responsible for the negotiations. i am therefore thankful for the presence of mr frattini. however, i would like to start by objecting to the implicit link that mr frattini makes with the failed terrorist attacks in the uk last week. i find that distasteful: it has nothing to do with pnr. that brings me to an important point that this house has emphasised many times and that is the need for an evaluation. we need evidence that the use of pnr data leads to greater security and that they are not used only to catch people who commit document fraud, drug smuggling or whatever. we need evidence and not just anecdotes. mr frattini says that this is a good agreement. well, it serves two purposes: firstly, to legalise the transfer of data by carriers and, secondly, to provide, as he has said, a high level of data protection. well, it fails miserably on the second objective; it is not legally binding, it explicitly states that it does not confer any rights on any person or any party. well, how much clearer can it get? then, it looks good superficially, but it is full of loopholes, open definitions and exceptions, when it comes to purpose limitation, for example, or the retention periods, which are going up to 15 years and maybe even more, and they will be applied retroactively. i am not a lawyer, but that strikes me as distinctly funny. the reduction from 34 to 19 data is an insult to our intelligence. if you look at the data, it is not a reduction: the 34 are merged into 19 data fields. i am not stupid. we may not have any powers here but we are not stupid. then with pull to push: we got that promise back in 2004. we still do not have it! it is technically feasible, so why do we not have it?
