rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. president. - first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur. the report adopted by the committee on economic and monetary affairs is in fact entirely in line with your position, and largely in line with the commission's position and the position i had set out as rapporteur. it is clear that in the 21st century we need a real full-fledged internal energy market. that was expressed at the summit two weeks ago, and that means that we are striving for effective competition. so protectionism is out of the question. it is very good that mr vidal-quadras' report says that there must be reciprocity, because all too often we see countries that want to protect their own national champions on the one hand, but go shopping in other countries, and of course that is not possible. of course, the interests of the citizens must be protected, because you are right to say that energy is a specific commodity. but this must be done through public service obligations, through the government taking responsibility and not through protectionism. protecting him? - i know. protection of markets? - no, i did not. finally, i am very pleased that the report now expresses very clearly a longstanding wish of the liberals, namely that by creating a genuine level playing field and incorporating the external environmental costs into the price, renewable energy sources will finally be able to compete well with other energy sources, because that will certainly benefit our environment.
president. - thank you to mr vidal-quadras for his excellent report. tomorrow we shall vote on the ownership unbundling of the electricity grids and the production of electricity. it just so happens that the upper house of the dutch parliament is also voting on this matter tomorrow. the conclusion in the netherlands is that we will move towards a separation of energy supply and network management. if this happens in the rest of europe, it will be good news for dutch energy companies. they no longer have to feel like the best boy in the class. it is also good news for the development of renewable energy. because only through full ownership can we open up the energy market to new suppliers, and that is badly needed. there is currently too little network capacity to allow new entrants to the networks. the energy giants are reluctant to invest in capacity expansion for new entrants. access to networks is often difficult due to a lack of transparency, leading to huge delays, for example in the wind energy sector. splitting up is the answer!
i would like to thank mr sornosa martínez, on behalf of the pse group. you have our warm and full support. mercury, and in particular mercury compounds and mercury vapours, are toxic. mercury can accumulate in the brain tissue and nervous system, causing great damage there. this can lead to reduced intelligence. a wise policy is therefore to prohibit the use of mercury in unnecessary applications. there are alternatives available, and that is why it is good that the mercury thermometer is a thing of the past. there's a lot of debate about barometers. in the netherlands, the use of mercury has been banned since 2003. however, an exception was made for barometers until 2005 and then until 1 january 2006 pending new european rules. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the current crisis. that would be january 1, 2010. there is therefore a lot of extra time and plenty of time to develop alternatives, many of which already exist, as the commissioner confirmed. of course, i can imagine that it is still a harsh message for the manufacturers of barometers. but if we want to eliminate mercury, then we should at least ban mercury-containing products for consumers. barometers can break or leak and mercury still gets into the environment. i agree with the manufacturers of barometers that the use of mercury in incandescent lamps is also very harmful. it is a good idea to switch to led lamps as soon as possible, but this directive is about mercury in measuring equipment and not about incandescent lamps. i therefore wholeheartedly support mrs sornosa martínez again and i think she has chosen a very good approach.
the commission has already been asked by the council in june 2004 to prepare a general strategy for the protection of critical infrastructure. the commission has therefore not lost sight of this subject in the last three years, and rightly so. in accordance with the wishes of the council and also of the european parliament, the commission has finally come forward with a proposal for a european programme for the protection of critical infrastructure, or the directive we are now discussing. as rapporteur, i support the idea of a common framework in this area. effective protection of vulnerable critical infrastructures, as well as of services, requires communication, coordination and cooperation involving all stakeholders, both at national and european level. the complex processes and interfaces of critical infrastructure with a transnational dimension are also, in my view, legitimate concerns. and as commissioner frattini has made clear on several occasions: damage to or loss of a particular infrastructure facility in one member state may have negative consequences for several other member states and even for the european economy as a whole. new technologies, such as the internet, and the further liberalisation of the market, for example in the supply of electricity and gas, mean that many infrastructure services are now integrated into a larger network. and indeed, in those circumstances, the effectiveness of all those protective measures is determined by the weakest link. i do think, and mr frattini has already said so, that the commission has been a little too forward or a little too enthusiastic on certain aspects of the directive. it should be clear that the primary and ultimate responsibility lies with the member states and the owners of that critical infrastructure. in this respect, i believe that a bottom-up approach is crucial. in my view, a joint action can only be justified if at least three member states would be adversely affected, or at least two other member states than the one where the critical infrastructure is located. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. i also believe that overlaps or contradictions with existing laws and/or regulations should be avoided at all costs. existing criteria and mechanisms should therefore be respected. i also think it is important that the private sector should not be subjected to unnecessary administrative burdens. make use of the expertise already available and do not try to reinvent the wheel! i therefore advocate a pragmatic but structural approach. after the debate in the committees, certain groups in parliament agreed to focus on the so-called priority sectors. it was also decided to scrap the proposed comitology procedure. the use of the comitology procedure has brought us to the brink too often in the past. i am therefore very grateful to the commissioner for his comments on this and the other amendments and for his satisfaction. i would like to hear a further comment on the definition of two to three member states, because that is the most important amendment in my view. finally, a word to the council. this one shines through absence. the agreement on a common position is still a bridge too far. this is remarkable, because the council itself has asked for this common framework. it is also strange, because if something happens, the council is all too happy to take the lead in announcing all kinds of rules immediately without really looking at the quality of the proposals, their implications for the internal market, for example, or their implications for the european citizens. the council is expected to show vision and decisiveness in this. and at the opening of the plenary session earlier today, president pöttering spoke words of wisdom. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. on the other hand, a structural approach, respecting the principles of the rule of law - and this is extremely important - is very welcome. thank you and thank you to the commissioner.
the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one. they can now invoke the emergence of a new kind of terrorism. everyone is frightened by this terrorism and it creates room for immature solutions. at all levels of government, proposals have been made to subordinate democracy, freedom of association, freedom of demonstration, right to strike, freedom of travel and privacy to the supposed guarantees of security. the weakness of that approach is that it does not remove the breeding ground for terrorism, such as the stark inequality in wealth and power that divides the world. instead, we're going to collect more intelligence, we're going to guard more objects, we're going to organize more bureaucracy, and we're going to incite more discontent. the european union already has 32 directives, regulations, conventions and decisions in the field of critical infrastructure, which enable a european approach. the addition of a new directive with more powers and obligations therefore raises questions. the committee on subsidiarity of the dutch parliament drew my attention to this in january. the committee calls into question the legal basis of article 308 of the ec treaty, which is aimed at the interim extension of powers, and considers that the protection of vital infrastructure is primarily a national matter. as the shadow rapporteur on this subject in the committee on transport i was very pleased that this committee decided to call on the committee on civil liberties to reject the proposal altogether. the main reason for this request was that everything in the draft directive could be better regulated at a small scale, i.e. by the member states or their subdivisions. in this case, more european union involvement means more unproductive bureaucracy. unfortunately, the groups which voted unanimously against in the committee on transport were divided in the committee on civil liberties. that is also true of my group. most of the smaller national delegations think it is a bad proposal. on the one hand, because it makes the division of responsibilities between the member states and the union less clear by unnecessary interference. on the other hand, because it could be misused to restrict civil rights, such as the freedom of demonstration and assembly, with a reference to the protection of infrastructure. it is not international terrorism that is at stake here, but domestic democracy. in contrast, the members of our larger delegations from germany and italy also see positive points in the proposal. they expect a reduction in the powers already exercised by the commission in practice and improved parliamentary control over the application of the remaining powers. the amendments are welcomed by supporters and opponents in my group because they essentially weaken the draft and limit its application to matters affecting at least three member states.
mr president, the failure of private postal companies led to takeover by the state more than a century ago. since then, it has been the best guarantee of delivering mail on time and at the same rate everywhere. differences between densely populated areas, where postal delivery is profitable, and sparsely populated or remote areas, where delivery is expensive, were eliminated. sorting and delivering mail was left in the hands of professionals who delivered quality. the postman and the post office became in many cases an indispensable contact not only for rural residents but also for the economically weakest urban residents. for many years we have seen private companies eager to take over the most profitable parts. they prefer to employ temporary workers, such as students, housewives and the elderly, for whom postal delivery is not the main means of livelihood. these people are preferably paid by letter rather than by the hour. post offices are replacing them with contracts with supermarkets. the result is that the quality of the customers is reduced and the staff is greatly reduced. politicians who participate in it create a problem rather than solve it. the risk is that the government will have to subsidize the most loss-making parts of the postal service, while the profitable parts will be taken over by large international companies. the compromise reached by the three largest political groups does provide a delay and a relaxation of the original proposal, but no lasting solutions. my group has tabled proposals for improvement and supports those of others, but we are far more in favour of rejecting the proposal and continuing the present situation.
first of all, mr president, i should like to congratulate mr ferber on the result achieved. after years of study and debate, the free postal market is in sight. the main point of this report is the fixing of a definitive date for the opening of the postal market. that means providers know what they're up against. it gives them the opportunity to orient their business operations to this, if they have not already done so. this will increase the strength of the sector as a whole. that is necessary to be able to fight the battle with electronic communications. another advantage is that attention is paid to the specific situation in the member states. the proposal recognises the differences in social, geographical and economic terms. member states shall reserve the right to deal with those national concerns in the most appropriate manner. i do not deny that some companies in europe are facing difficult times. however, experience has shown that market-oriented operations are useful for sharpening the mind and exploiting opportunities. i hope that all the postal companies in europe will be willing and able to make that strike.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, it has been said many times in this house. what's happening now, what we're talking about now, is inevitable, but, consumers, you need not fear: the services will be improved and the prices will be lowered. fellas, let's be honest with each other. we all know that there are no guarantees. we know this by looking at the experience, for example, in sweden and england. in particular, in smaller postal markets, the replacement of the state monopoly will mean the creation of a private monopoly, and we all know that this means that there are no guarantees of better service and lower prices. but what i am most concerned about, frankly, is the position, the future of the workers who now work at the post office. i say this on the basis of experience, for example in the already liberalized part of the postal market in the netherlands. we see that many fixed jobs have been transformed into insecure parttime jobs. and let us be honest with the citizens of europe. liberalisation does not necessarily make things better. i'm also saying this based on my own personal history. my father worked for the post office for 40 years. so did my uncles and my cousins. those were jobs to be proud of. jobs that many people were proud of. and i know that that world of old, the world of old certainties, is not coming back. i'm not saying out of nostalgia or some sort of false romance that we should go back to that time. but it does create a, let me say, uncomfortable feeling about the uncertain future of many people who now work at the post office, who are proud of it or people who are proud of their postman. it also raises serious doubts as to whether this is the message we as a european parliament should be conveying. great promises to the consumers we know are butter-smooth. on the other hand, announcements of sweeping changes for the employees we know can be very tough. that is not my idea of a social europe.
i would like to thank mr ferber for his work as rapporteur on this sensitive subject which affects many citizens in europe. the provision of a comprehensive and efficient service to consumers and businesses is no longer possible with old state monopolies without market incentives. it is therefore good that these monopolies will disappear by the end of 2010 and that more room will be created for innovation and new services. president. - the council is divided, but under the leadership of mr ferber, parliament is once again taking the lead in finding a solution to a sensitive issue. the proposal now before us is, in my view, a balanced proposal. it includes a clear date for the abolition of monopolies and the fixing of reciprocity. however, a level playing field is essential. and as regards the conditions for market access, various interests must be and are being taken into account. this opens up a range of possibilities for member states to finance services. but there is a risk in that too: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the market is not distorted. the european commission therefore has a major task to perform in assessing very critically the financing arrangements and conditions of the member states and to check whether there is any fair competition. mr president, mr meijer is still living in the last century. because the postal market has been undergoing major changes for many years. e-mail, the internet and other communication technologies have brought about many changes and fundamentally changed the postal market. this is an irrevocable cost to employment, but a liberalised market also challenges us to be creative. experience shows that new services and new activities also lead to new jobs. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect.
mr president, to be honest, for a liberal the compromise before us is not a party package. after 15 years of debate, the date of entry into force has been postponed. the compromise also contains the necessary protectionist provisions which prevent the market from being fully opened and create a great deal of legal uncertainty. the debates were and are often national in character. the member states which have not reformed sufficiently in recent years are particularly clearly opposed to a rapid and full market operation. in this way, they deny businesses and consumers the benefits of competition and thus increase efficiency, quality and innovation. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the work of the european parliament. i am therefore even more astonished to see the council debates in this dossier. the council, which is again conspicuous by its absence. despite all the good intentions and strategies such as the lisbon strategy, the trend in 2007 is to isolate the internal market. i cannot therefore join the colleagues who applaud and praise the compromise, nor those who speak of all sorts of horror scenarios and/or social dumping.
rapporteur. – mr president, in drafting this report for the european parliament, i have wholeheartedly congratulated commissioner mccreevy and his predecessor, mr bolkestein, on the success of the financial markets legislation and regulation and the executive work of the european commission in this respect. we have no disagreements about that. we may differ, though, with respect to the assessment of further steps and the priorities for the follow-up. i think this is linked to a difference in perspective. for me, it is not enough just to look at the success of this financial sector as such. i see this sector as instrumental, and want to assess whether it provides the right infrastructure for economic growth, wealth and well-being. that is why in my report i focus first on the strategic question of who is profiting from this success. are end users profiting to the same extent as the few large multinational financial conglomerates that have acquired dominant positions at the top of the financial markets? who is profiting most from the increased liquidity in the markets? is it kkr, blackstone and the other private equity firms that do billion-dollar deals, or is it the companies that are covered because they see credit ratings tumble down to junk status? do entrepreneurs still dare to invest in long-term research and development projects for the development of innovative new products and services, or are they squeezed in ever shorter cycles of financial planning and reporting? is the huge increase of complex financial products derived from the traditional bonds and equities really leading to a better allocation of capital, or does it contribute mainly to greater speculative profits for the hedge funds that master the rules of the pyramid game? are citizens in the recently-acceded member states better off now with the invasion of foreign banks and insurance companies in their countries, or are they confronted again with monopolistic structures that only provide them with standard, mediocre-quality products at prices higher than in the countries where they have their headquarters? these and similar questions seem to me to be fundamental to answering the question of what kind of follow-up is required, and the first preliminary message in my report is thus to make more in-depth economic analysis about that. i shall turn now to the priorities outlined in my report. the first message deals with one of my great concerns, the enormous market concentration at the top of the market, with 30 to 40 major cross-border financial players and a high concentration in several member states. in the competition report that we adopted at the last part-session, we voiced our concerns and suggested that commissioner kroes put on her list of possible enquiries and further investigations the activities at the top of the financial market of investment banks, credit rating agencies, accounting firms, etc. we repeated this suggestion to her last week. the second issue is the financial stability risks of these new developments of alternative investments – hedge funds, private equity. i do not have to go into detail there because the papers daily show that there are currently more concerns at the developments. prudential supervisors, central banks, the ecb and our counterparts in the usa, whom we will visit next week, also raised these concerns and i think it is high time that we dealt with these risks. the third issue is the retail markets. my answer to the lack of integration at the retail side is not just to overhaul this system and open up the borders but to make a more precise focused strategy, particularly for mobile users, who have a real need in terms of accessing and working with service providers in different members states, so that could be more of a stimulation for pan-european financial products such as the pension product that was presented some weeks ago by the european banking federation. demand for microcredit is an important issue, as are pension systems, access to basic services, financial education and stronger consumer input and, finally, better regulation and the future architecture of supervision. on this issue, i think the main difference is that your mantra is ‘less is more’, as you said last week. we think that we need a focused regulation. as regards the architecture of supervision i would say that it will be high time to discuss the future architecture in the autumn and not just wait and see what will happen, although there are a lot of good developments.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women's rights and gender equality. commission rapporteur, i am not against the reform of labour law if it leads to more security for adequate health care, unemployment benefits, pensions for everyone and therefore also for the lowest paid. the commission is turning this around. instead of advocating the strengthening of workers' rights, the commission wants to make europe an employer's paradise, where the right not to be dismissed will be a nostalgic thing of the past. where you no longer have the right to refuse overtime without pay and cao's are considered old-fashioned. the commission calls this flexicurity. - that sounds good. but the translation of what the commission is trying to achieve is simple. more flexibility for the employer and more uncertainty for the employee. this will be particularly disastrous for women, young people and minorities who are already lagging behind in the labour market and have a disproportionate share of parttime jobs and temporary contracts. they will be the first victims if this proposal is passed unchanged. then the rest will follow.
you can see how our eye contact is still working, and that's fine. mr president, it is a good thing that we are having a debate today on employment law. - why? - why? because there are some things changing in the labour market in europe and in all our member states. we see that there is a great need for flexibility. flexibility to respond better to the needs of the market on the one hand, but also flexibility requested by the workers themselves because they often want to combine work with family, family and care duties. this flexibility is necessary, especially in a society which is aging and deteriorating, and in which the people who are aging may no longer want to function in the labour market for the full 40 or 36 hours and may want to maintain their participation in the labour market with a smaller job. but if we want this flexibility and if we recognise it as a good thing - and that is why this debate is so good - then from the outset - and i am sorry that mrs matsouka has left the chamber because she was talking about a debate between the right and the left, but she is not listening at all - we will also be providing the security of those who want to work in smaller and more flexible jobs and thus also make the labour market work better. it is a shortcoming - and we may be able to make up for it with the amendments which will be adopted shortly - that the great good of flexibility is no longer recognised. i'm sorry to hear that. i also believe, mr president, that we cannot leave it to the market alone, because the market regulates itself. we must try to reinstate flexibility combined with good safeguards.
the importance of the oceans is well known. we live, eat and enjoy. that is why attention to good ocean management is vital. the piecyk report covers this in a comprehensive way. good governance requires a balanced approach that balances environmental and economic aspects at global, european and national levels. in this way we will be able to reap the benefits of the oceans in the future. i would like to make a comment on the development of ports. in order to make a substantial contribution to the well-being of citizens, it is important that they can develop within the framework of their strengths. i believe that europe should be reluctant to influence this development, both in spatial and economic terms. member states are responsible for the design of their ports and the market will ensure efficient handling of cargo flows. to make this visible in the text, i have tabled a number of amendments and i hope that the members will support them.
the sea, mr president, is a great asset with enormous wealth and we must take good care of it. this is precisely why the integrated approach of this green paper is so important. but it must be practical and workable so that we do not miss our goal. i agree with the rapporteur that environmental protection and sustainability are important. i also pay tribute to his report. but i must say that it was very difficult because of the procedure for closer cooperation. we now have a 147-page christmas tree. what i find important and regrettable is that the importance of maritime transport seems to be somewhat underestimated under the new measures. it is precisely from the point of view of sustainability that maritime transport is so important. we already have a lot of regulations when it comes to safety or environmentally friendly transport. but implementation and enforcement by the member states are still very poor. i would really like to call on the commission to make implementation and enforcement of the existing rules a priority. the european union must also have more coordinating powers and, if necessary, be able to enforce enforcement. the commission was to launch a feasibility study on a european coastguard by the end of 2006. it's now mid-2007 and we've still got nothing to show for it. i would urge you to make this work. the commission, mr president, must call on the member states to ensure good spatial planning and coastal zone management. but it must be the member states themselves who bear the responsibility for this. local, regional and rural policy also has a responsibility for the sustainability and conservation of these areas, and i believe that subsidiarity must apply here, particularly where land is concerned.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the railways. this is true even for long-distance crossborder transport. the rail system is suitable for mass transport and in some cases special freight lines have long been built for this purpose, especially in and around industrial areas in germany. the recently opened bethuel line in the netherlands, part of the no 1 freight corridor from rotterdam through germany and switzerland to genoa, is also a special line that is completely unsuitable for passenger transport. despite this favourable starting position for rail, the share of goods transported by rail has declined and in some cases even the volume is declining. much of this transport has been taken over by the lorry and the large growth in the volume of goods is largely handled by the motorways, which are becoming increasingly congested. a significant part of this shift is infrastructure related. in the past, the railways had a very fine network of branches. they opened up villages in the countryside as well as ports and factory sites in the cities. in addition to freight lines, there were also transshipment stations where freight cars were collected and redistributed among different trains. many companies had their own connection to the railway network. this meant that a freight car could travel directly between a harbour quay and a distant factory without any intermediate transshipment of goods. everything that is sought to be achieved by multimodal systems today was already achieved then. unfortunately, governments have chosen to reduce the railway network because it was considered to be loss-making. many small rail lines have been dismantled and many connections to businesses have disappeared. on the other hand, billions have been invested in the expansion of the space-hungry network of highways. today, the only way to transport goods from door to door is by truck. it is indispensable for the pre- and post-transport and it seems easy to make the much longer intermediate section by road. restoration of business links and transfer stations could make an important contribution to the recovery of rail freight transport. the first railway package is based partly on the assumption that rail transport should take over the mode of operation of road and air transport. international companies then arrange the cross-border transport from the point of origin to the point of destination, and that is the most attractive for those who have something to transport. my group has always stressed that this is not the only possible solution. there is an alternative. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the railway infrastructure. they should not see each other as competitors but as partners in a comprehensive european rail network. it is precisely by encouraging them to compete that cooperation will be more rigid. so far we have seen no positive results from the course taken. the free market is often not the solution to problems, but the cause of them. the rapporteur rightly advocates the use of the new standard security system, ertms. we agree with him. we also note that the start-up of this system is taking much longer than expected and that a second old system will have to be maintained alongside the new system. however, due to the average low speed of freight transport, ertms is less problematic than high-speed passenger lines. finally, we agree with mr cramer that air and road transport are being made artificially cheap and rail transport artificially expensive. if we don't change that, the least environmentally friendly transport will continue to win.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. halfway through the planning horizon of the white paper on transport, it appears that the objectives are not yet within reach. the commission's decision to adjust the targets is courageous and wise. i must compliment you on that. the change in course, however, means that a large number of boards must be checked at the same time. the aim is no longer just to move cargo, but to improve every modality. in some cases this requires considerable international action and perseverance. there is also plenty to do within europe. adequate infrastructure, use of new technology, specific action programmes for sub-markets and further introduction of the 'polluter pays' principle. it is very right that these elements have been included in the report by mr barsi-pataky. i can therefore largely agree with the content of her report. there are two points on which i have tabled an amendment. this parliament makes a lot of transport legislation and then it is often up to the member states to implement that legislation. it has been found that the same legal text is interpreted and enforced in different ways in different member states. this means that, for example, drivers who drive through several member states comply with the same legislation in one country and not in the other. with all the consequences of that. that is unacceptable to me. amendment no 4 by my own hand calls attention to this and calls on the member states and the commission to achieve a joint interpretation of legal texts where necessary. the amendment does not provide for common sanctions for infringements. the second point concerns urban transport. although i welcome that, i believe that this is and should remain a competence of the member states. the european union's role can therefore only be facilitative. in conclusion, i would like to thank mrs barsi-pataky for her efforts and congratulate her on this result.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the market economy is not a one-way street. there are too many irregularities in bilateral trade relations for that to be the case. i am thinking, for example, of the many violations of intellectual property. i have also heard from your excellent report, commissioner, that after your meeting with chinese foreign minister bo xilai on 12 june, the dialogue on intellectual property rights has been resumed. i'm glad to hear that. i also believe that the eu is doing the right thing - unlike my liberal colleague - by dealing directly with the united states in such matters within the wto. the united states will ultimately remain our most important strategic ally. and then, commissioner, i would like to draw your attention to another important aspect of our trade relations with china, namely the european export of secondary raw materials such as waste paper. that's a big deal today. last weekend the dutch press published a major article on this subject. as you know, the new european rules on the export of these raw materials will come into force in two days' time. the commission's mistakes have meant that no agreements have been concluded with important countries such as china and india. as a result, as of 12 july, waste paper and other recyclable materials are considered the most hazardous waste. you will understand that this has a major impact on the export of these products. i call on commissioner mandelson to ask this parliament to grant a transitional arrangement until 1 january 2008. during this period you can conclude agreements with those countries with which this has not yet been done. this will prevent exports from stopping and these products from accumulating in european ports such as rotterdam. i hope, commissioner, that you will take real action, because this is a matter which the dutch public finds very bad for europe.
member of the commission. mr president, bearing in mind your probable desire for this house to rise before midnight, i would just like to say that overwhelmingly the contributions to this debate have been very balanced and very realistic. i think that mr calabuig rull and mr carnero gonzález, for example, made appropriate observations in stressing the fact that china is an opportunity for europe, as well as a threat. another appropriate observation was that of mr fjellner that we need to take the longer-term political view in our relations with china, but he also referred to the growth of protectionism, of a sort of chinese nationalism which is becoming more prominent in china’s trade practices. my view is that china’s nationalism does not sit comfortably with the enormous benefits that it is deriving from the internationalist trading system that is based on reciprocity. without reciprocity china will not be able to expect in the future the continued goodwill and balanced approach that it has been receiving from europe. the fact is that china is falling below our expectations. it is not that it is failing to comply with or implement all its wto obligations and commitments, because in many respects it is. but in certain other key areas of economic activity in china, in financial and other services, it is failing to implement fully the commitments that it made upon its accession to the wto. one or two concerns have been expressed about textiles. this house will recall that in 2005 i negotiated a voluntary agreement with china that gave european industry an extra period of two-and-a-half years to restructure and to adapt to the new global situation in the textile sector. it was a once-and-for-all, bilaterally concluded agreement and the commission therefore has no means simply to seek an extension of the agreed levels that have been operating beyond the beginning of 2008. however, i will be putting in place means to monitor and to keep under surveillance any sudden and large-scale changes in imports of chinese textiles that threaten to disrupt our market in europe, and i will consider what, if any, action i can take in the light of disturbances to our market. this is quite distinct from the use of trade defence instruments. the textiles agreement was based on fair trade. it was not unfair trade. it was just a disturbance that we were unable to take on on such a scale at this time. in other respects, the use of tdi is not against china’s natural competitive advantage but against unfair competition, and we will retain the right and the full use of our trade defence instruments and employ them as and when necessary. in conclusion, others have referred to the need for china to consciously build up the standards it operates in relation to social, labour and environmental standards. that will always be a feature of the discussions that i undertake with chinese officials. i am personally very committed to growing technology transfer with china on climate change projects. this is a key way to ensure chinese interest in tackling climate change. it is a win-win situation. the eu can export energy-efficient, clean technology and china can make a contribution to tackling its environmental problems. this is at the heart of our dialogue with china on climate change and it is something in the trade realm that i intend to build up in the future. that concludes my remarks in response to this debate, which i greatly welcome. as i say, it has been very balanced, and i will certainly ensure that i and my services take into account fully the remarks and observations that have been made by members of this house this evening.

president, it became clear as early as the autumn of 2006 that the ahtisaari report would go in the direction of independence, with special measures for the protection of serbian and other minorities. it was then clear that serbia would not want to agree to this and was counting on a russian veto in the united nations security council. since then, the question has been whether such a veto would lead to unilateral recognition of kosovo as a state by other states, including the united states and european union member states. that is the controversial model which was used in 1992 to recognise the independence of slovenia and croatia by germany, before any european or international decisions were taken. do you expect this model to be re-applied if necessary? is it possible to avoid the application of the contested model if the area north of kosovska mitrovica, where the serb population of kosovo is concentrated, is returned to serbia? in that case, can we get agreement from serbia and the russian federation?
the former federal republic of yugoslavia has disintegrated into six internationally recognized states, and kosovo is likely to join them soon. it is remarkable that the european union has adopted a separate approach for each of these seven states. slovenia has been a member for over three years, negotiations with croatia are being successfully concluded and macedonia has been a candidate country since 2005 without any negotiations. with the other regions, there is only talk of stabilisation and association, with serbia and bosnia suffering long delays because they have not been able to meet the conditions laid down. this is in stark contrast to the simultaneous admission in 2004 of estonia, latvia and lithuania, which were occupied by the soviet union until 1991, and the czech republic and slovakia, which were a common state until 1993, on the one hand, and the czech republic and slovakia, which were a common state until 1993, on the other. i have always advocated that the accession process of croatia and macedonia should at least take place as simultaneously as possible, and i regret that by delaying the negotiations, macedonia is now two years behind croatia. that does not mean that i think macedonia is now fully ripe for accession. the serious environmental pollution and the complicated status of trade unions do not fit into what is normal in europe. the commission has also been asked to provide information on the implementation of the new rules on the protection of personal data. the role of the state in the kidnapping of a german citizen for american interrogation in afghanistan has not yet been clarified. the current government, in its initial phase, placed a disproportionate emphasis on economic growth, including through extremely low taxes that could ruin the country. these and other problems will have to be solved in the coming years. however, two other issues dominate public opinion and publicity inside and outside macedonia. the community's relations with the countries of the mediterranean are characterized by the internal relations between the various groups of the population and the relations with the neighbouring countries, particularly greece. other european countries, notably belgium and switzerland, but also spain, italy and finland, show how to deal effectively with the equality of citizens, despite major internal differences in language and culture. in the first years of independence, too much has been made of the impression that macedonia is mainly a state of people who use the macedonian language, which is related to bulgarian and to a lesser extent to serbian. fortunately, it is now generally recognised that education and administration in the mother tongue are very important for the large albanian population, which constitutes the majority in the north-west. it also seeks to develop close relations with the future neighbour, kosovo, with which many of its inhabitants have family ties. the recent agreements between the largest governing party and the largest party of the albanian-speaking population, which boycotted the parliamentary sessions for months, give hope of reconciliation and an increasingly equal position of the albanian population. members of the many smaller groups, the most prominent of which are the roma and the turks, also have the right to equal treatment, participation in decision-making and the exercise of public functions. macedonia is now presenting itself as a multiethnic state and that creates obligations. macedonia is the designation of a geographical area with an ancient and long history of changing inhabitants, parts of which now belong to greece and bulgaria. the use of the same name for a state that includes part of the territory has caused controversy. bulgaria, the state which in 1878 was forced to give back to turkey most of the part of macedonia already allocated to its territory and later had to watch this area being taken over by serbia, has reconciled with its independent neighbours and was the first state to recognise the constitutional name of the republic of macedonia. on the other hand, since its independence in 1991, neighbouring greece has seriously objected to this constitutional name. today, the greek position is that the name macedonia is acceptable, provided that another concept is added to it that makes clear that it is only a part of the historical region of macedonia. the use of that name without any adjectives, especially when accompanied by the use of symbols from greek-macedonian history, arouses suspicion in greece that the territory of three northern greek provinces bearing the same name is claimed. i am not surprised by the invitations that my reporting has produced to take sides unilaterally in favour of one of the conflicting positions of the two neighbouring countries. i remain neutral and believe that both sides should work together to find a solution as soon as possible to deal constructively with their long-standing disagreement. this includes agreements to avoid and reverse all provocations involving symbols or maps. public opinion on both sides of the border, while appealing to the national pride of its politicians, also wants peace and cooperation. without good agreements, there is a danger that macedonia's accession to the european union will be unnecessarily delayed, even though both domestic and greek public opinion wants accession.
the european parliament has been asked to make a statement on the situation in the middle east. every year, millions of people, especially in africa, are denied the treatment that could save their lives. not because there are no drugs, but simply because they cannot afford them. and that is, of course, a terrible scandal, whatever the exact cause. since 2003, there has been a temporary wto arrangement for countries that do not have the capacity to produce cheap medicines for their own people. it is correct what the commissioner said. in doha in 2003, we had a great victory when we made the breakthrough on this point. the eu also deserves enormous credit for this. we can debate the causes for a long time, but the sad thing is that this system has not yet worked in practice, and so all those people, those millions of people who are not receiving the medicines we so much want and for which we applauded the breakthrough at the time. now you are asking us as the european parliament to give our consent and actually make the agreement permanent. mr president, before this parliament can sign off on this regulation, which has so far proved unworkable, with all the causes and complexities behind it, we naturally want to have firm guarantees. the commissioner and the council have tried to say a number of things. firstly, it is essential that we should seek to make use of a practical arrangement. this simply requires much greater assistance - practical, legal and political - from the countries concerned. we just know that many of them find it very complex to actually use it. my suggestion would be to do what we have done in the trade negotiations: set up a european low-cost medicine team, the european union a-team, so that you can provide, so to speak, direct assistance. not just money, but actual assistance and knowledge of legal matters. secondly, i understand that you have promised that as the european commission you will not include trips+ in bilateral agreements. that would be a very important element, if it is clearly confirmed by the council. thirdly, the only long-term solution is to allow developing countries to take over production. that, of course, is the development angle. that knowledge is there. a number of countries do. look at tanzania. i think that same european a-team could do that again very well. if you go that way, we can sign the papers.

president, commissioner, economic growth in the new member states since 2004 has been about 5.3%, 2% higher than in the old member states. exports have doubled. investment is increasing rapidly. these results, which, as mr potočnik has said, have been strongly supported by cohesion policy, are excellent. statistically speaking, the number of poor regions is decreasing. however, regional disparities within the member states themselves are still very high. the report by mrs geringer de oedenberg rightly calls attention to this. and i think we need to be more critical of the efforts made in the member states themselves. too much concentration in central urban areas does not bring the peripheral regions any further. the member states themselves, together with the european union, must also adopt a territorial approach to their cohesion policy. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. much more politically, because we are not, in the end, accelerating economic development and cohesion in poor regions. so a recalibration is necessary. the fourth cohesion report provides the framework for discussing this. the ppe-de will take part in this. we will hold a hearing on this matter in parliament on 8 november. in conclusion, there are good results, good opportunities for cohesion policy, provided we make greater use of synergy at european, national and regional level for the benefit of those poorer regions and oppose the europe of multiple speeds. poor regions must also be fully integrated into the knowledge economy and globalisation.
rapporteur. – mr president, at the start of the vote on my report, i have to inform the house of two issues. first, when the vote was being prepared in the afet committee, all political groups agreed to use in this report only one term for the state which calls itself the republic of macedonia. it is the long sentence ‘former yugoslav republic of macedonia’, without any abbreviation, such as the english fyrom or the french arym. i therefore propose that if parts of the final adopted text include any other terms, those terms should be replaced on the basis of this broad agreement. secondly, the ppe-de group proposed that i should merge two amendments on paragraph 13 into one – my amendment 22 and the pse amendment 6. with this is mind, i should like to move an oral amendment to my amendment 22. as the vote on amendment 22 is due to be taken after the vote on amendment 6, this solution is only possible if amendment 6 is rejected. i should therefore like to ask that the order of voting on the amendments be changed. mr pinior of the pse group said yesterday evening that he would be in favour of this arrangement.
in writing. the macedonia i first came to know in 1962 had many features of previous centuries, with traditional country houses, mud roads, horse and wagon transportation, dress codes, and the living side by side of different ethnic groups. the macedonia of 45 years later is the result of profound developments after that time. i think that the european union made serious mistakes in the 1990s in the inevitable break-up of yugoslavia. for too long this division has been denied, and once it has been recognized, it has been chosen to ignore the remaining ethnic diversity. in some cases the wrong borders have been recognized, unnecessary military means have been used and wrong approaches have been adopted. in macedonia, the damage was relatively limited. as rapporteur, i hope to contribute to peace, democracy, reconciliation and progress for that country. the 558 votes of this parliament in favour of my report represent a step forward towards the earlier promised but hitherto stagnant accession of macedonia to the european union. i hope that in the next annual report we will be able to note that important obstacles to accession have been removed and that the council has started accession negotiations.
the rapporteur. - mr president, i would like to take up the excellent speech by mr lévai. it is a good thing that we in this parliament look at the quality of regulations and legislation at least once a year. the rules and legislation are the european union's calling card. it is the framework within which citizens live/work and businesses must operate. that means we have to pay a lot of attention to that quality. how are you doing right now? it's going in the right direction. the commission has taken a number of initiatives. there is an impact assessment board, and it's doing a lot of good work. but that's not enough. i 've said it before . we need more transparency and i would again call for an external expert panel to assess whether the european commission's impact assessments are transparency, and i think any kind of transparency is a great thing for government. an added benefit: i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter, and whether it is not in a position to do so. an important step in the right direction is that we have an expert panel on the problem of administrative burdens. that is a very good development which can also play a role in the area of administrative burdens. of course, it's very important who gets to sit on that panel. it should not be a parade, a sort of pseudo-parliament, but a small body of experts, not captains of industry, not officials, not politicians, but simply experts who know what impact assessment is, who know what administrative burdens are and who have had a lot to do with it in their national context. the commission has set itself a clear objective: a 25% reduction in administrative burdens is to be achieved by 2012. that is a very good thing, but it must of course be a net objective. that means that from now on a reduction of 25% will indeed be achieved and that new administrative burdens will be calculated and deducted from them, otherwise, of course, the vacuum will continue to be opened. i would like to add one more point to which the commission must pay particular attention. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information provided by the member states. the cost of compliance is the cost that businesses and citizens have to pay to comply with legislation, and that's also a huge cost. if we talk about the cost of regulation, we also have to deal with the cost of compliance, which is not being considered at the moment.
madam president, i should also like to begin by thanking all the rapporteurs for the extremely interesting reports. it is very important that parliament pays close attention to this subject and i fully share the commissioner's view that a lot of citizens in europe have the impression that all bad legislation comes from brussels. all too often, too, national parliamentarians say that they cannot help it either, that it is brussels that imposes it on them. we must do as much as we can and as quickly as possible to defuse this. it is the first time that i have spoken on a subject of the committee on legal affairs and i am speaking in this debate primarily because the subject interests me very much. i am a member of the committee on budgets and the committee on budgetary control. in the committee on budgets we approved last year a proposal from me to create a body, a pilot project, to evaluate the administrative burden outside the commission. this means that parliament considered that the internal evaluation in the commission was not sufficient. i fully agree with mr doorn that there should be a body outside the commission which assesses the effects of legislation. the text contained in the budget, which has now been adopted by the council and parliament, should be the commission's guide to action. the committee on budgetary control also drew up a report which was adopted by this parliament in july. we support the 25% reduction target and of course - the commissioner and mr doorn have already confirmed this - we cannot have new legislation coming in through the back door which we have just abolished. it is important that we review all legislation before 2013 to see whether it is superfluous or not. the commission should regularly publish a report or other communication stating what has been achieved. it must be clear what the commission is doing and how we are managing to reduce the administrative burden. perhaps there should be an internal reorganization of the commission. finally, i would like to thank mr verheugen for the excellent cooperation i have had with his services on the preparation of a pilot project adopted by this house last year.
the proposal, as has already been said, provides for the updating and also the consolidation of the existing directives and decisions on the transport of dangerous goods by land. the commission has also proposed a new regulation on the transport of dangerous goods by road. to be honest, i wonder who could resist this now. i cannot imagine that anyone would doubt the usefulness and necessity of measures to ensure that such transport takes place in the best possible conditions of safety. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. at commission level we have agreed with the majority of the rapporteur's amendments, which are mainly technical. the rapporteur has clearly worked closely with the council in this, and that is generally very efficient for a directive like this. the rapporteur's desire to reach agreement at first reading quickly is therefore to be commended. but the introduction of amendment no 45 has surprised the alde group and is very easy. why not just accept the text as it is in amendment no 16, which we all agreed on? it should be clear that each mode of transport, in its own way, provides excellent safety and efficiency. why should the market players not be able to determine this themselves? we have already decided this with amendment no 16 and i would therefore ask you to look again at amendment no 45 and perhaps reconsider your position. i thank the rapporteur for his efforts, but i regret that he has been led to re-introduce the provision on the use of regulated means of transport through this back door. i would ask you again to reconsider your position on amendment no 45.
in its communication, the commission, not for the first time, argues that logistics play an important role in meeting all sorts of new challenges, for example in the field of the environment. the role of logistics is of course equally important when it comes to the union's competitiveness. optimising the european transport system, addressing existing bottlenecks and using advanced logistics solutions, therefore deserves priority. action is needed in that respect. how can we improve the efficiency of individual modes of transport and their combinations? that is the question. i would like to make it clear that the expected growth in freight transport requires, in the first instance, the most pragmatic approach possible. in order to accommodate this expected growth, all transport modes must be used to the full. so it's very emphatically not either/or, but and/and. it was a pity that the house's reaction to the communication threatened to become a political debate on whether or not to allow those long heavy vehicles or the ecocombs or the gigaliners, as mr savary had just announced. after much deliberation, an agreement has been reached on a compromise that is as neutral as possible. as a supporter of these big, long heavy vehicles, i would like to say one more thing about it, and that is this: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. based on research results, that's my motto. i would like to stress once again that the expected growth in freight transport will require the utmost of us. priority should be given to the existing obstacles for short-term results. we must also overturn the sacred houses, including those that concern, for example, long heavy vehicles, or those that concern, for example, the proliferation of driving bans in the european union, the sacred houses around cabotage and many others. i hope very much that this will be clearly stated in the european commission's action plan. i have to thank the rapporteur for her efforts. i'm leaving in a few minutes, not because i'm rude, but because i have an important meeting elsewhere.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the transport of goods by road is not subject to the same restrictions as those imposed by the community. this transport continues to grow, mainly because it has become relatively cheaper over the years. businesses are increasingly organising unnecessary transport. there are two reasons for this: the extraction of raw materials, the various stages of processing and the sale of finished products take place in very different places, requiring unnecessary transportation between them. the establishment of main ports and other large distribution centres also makes goods travel unnecessarily long distances. in sparsely populated lands with little or no railways, especially in america and australia, huge trucks have been developed, megaliners. they reduce the cost of road transport, but they cause unnecessary inconvenience to everyone else. we should keep such trucks out of europe. the transport of goods must be reduced and the remaining transport needs to be carried out as much as possible by rail or water.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. it is the task of all concerned to ensure a smooth logistical process. governments optimise the conditions and entrepreneurs devise concepts that respond to market demand. i am pleased that the rapporteur has mentioned this separation and has highlighted several areas of concern. a major topic during the discussion of this report was the long heavy trucks or ecocombs. although national field tests have shown fairly positive results, opinions on these vehicles are divided. i think the compromise is a good starting point for further discussion. i look forward to the european commission's input into this discussion, both in the action plan for freight logistics and in the study on weights and measures which will be published shortly and the results of which are expected next summer. in conclusion, i would like to thank mrs ayala sender for her report and i hope that the path we have taken can be followed later this year.

the european commission estimates the total economic value of alcohol abuse in europe at eur 125 billion per year and estimates that alcohol abuse is responsible for 25% of all violent crime. a few more numbers: drinking during pregnancy is the leading cause of mental and physical impairment in children. about 1% of the european population suffers from the effects of foetal alcohol syndrome. that's almost 5 million europeans, and that's when fetal alcohol syndrome is completely preventable. 60% of people who suffer from fetal alcohol syndrome end up in prison or in psychiatric institutions. this afternoon we had a meeting with eurocare, with dr nico van der lely from my country, the netherlands. he said that the number of people treated for alcohol poisoning at his hospital, the reinier de graaf gasthuis in delft, had doubled in five years and that there was a sixfold increase in the number of children between the ages of 10 and 15. especially "coma drinking", drinking until you literally drop, is proving to be popular. in the first half of this year, more than 80 children had to be admitted to the reinier de graaf gasthuis in delft because of excessive drinking. many measures are possible and necessary. price and availability are probably the most important, and of course education and information. the only question is: who can do that best? the commissioner has rightly pointed out that there are very wide differences between the member states. so very often there will be local help in municipalities and it will be the doctors or the member states who can take the most effective measures. the treaty does not allow for much else. it is important to have an ambitious european alcohol strategy, but not to get ahead of the member states. europe must be defined and defined clearly. alcohol abuse needs to be tackled vigorously, especially among young people, and europe must give all possible support to the member states in this regard. i do have great hesitation in announcing legislation which we cannot then bring about, because it does not increase confidence in our institutions. i have already mentioned fetal alcohol syndrome and childhood alcoholism. the commissioner rightly says that this is a european problem. i therefore believe that the member states must take their responsibility in this area and that they must take it at european level. i think they should set clear targets at european level for reducing foetal alcohol syndrome and childhood alcohol abuse. it is also in the resolution. i think it would have been better if it had been more concrete than it is now. but if the member states are prepared to make such an agreement, then at least we will have some concrete results somewhere. because to shout that subsidiarity is important and then do nothing about it is not something i believe in. finally, i am very curious about the results of the labelling. in france, labels now warn against the consumption of alcohol by pregnant women. if we have the results and if it proves to work, i think we should consider such a measure at european level. there are also internal market entities to do that. we are allowed to do that and we can do that. but i think we should only do it if it is effective, and then i am willing to consider it.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, alcohol-related harm is a growing problem. the numbers speak for themselves. 5% of men and 1% of women are addicted to alcohol. 25% of deaths among young people aged 15 to 30 are alcohol-related. child abuse is often traced back to excessive alcohol use. behind these figures there is a lot of suffering. addiction, medical problems, social misery, abuse, it's all well known. less well known are the serious effects of alcohol consumption during pregnancy. unborn children can suffer serious harm. children with fetal alcohol syndrome are doomed. there is also growing recognition of the harmful effects of alcohol consumption on children and young people. alcohol use at an early age can cause lasting damage and adversely affect learning performance and iq. clearly, measures must be taken quickly to discourage drinking among young people and pregnant women. one of the effective measures may be health warnings on the bottle. i therefore call on the member states to come forward as soon as possible with proposals to make health warnings compulsory. as long as there is no european regulation, each member state is free to take measures. warnings on the bottle are effective and research in the united states has already shown this. i also call on the alcohol industry to take responsibility for itself in warning young people and pregnant women against alcohol consumption. better to warn now than face all kinds of claims in ten years. warnings are also a normal part of the social responsibility which the alcohol industry must also have. finally, i call on the commission to come forward with appropriate proposals as soon as possible. it is difficult to understand why tobacco warnings have not been followed up with alcohol warnings. i therefore urge the commission: the commission is therefore proposing that the european parliament should be consulted on the matter. it is important that we put public health above the interests of the wine industry.

the council has not yet taken a decision on the matter. is it possible that the council makes mistakes or is it all-knowing? do you think that all the measures taken in recent years in the fight against terrorism have always been the right measures? and is it really beyond doubt that there have been no disproportionate violations of civil rights? well, if the council is infallible then of course there is no need for an evaluation, but if you believe in possible progressive insight, then yes. i would also like to hear from you in the course of the debate whether you think there are certain measures missing, certain measures which you think we actually need now but which have not yet been taken. are measures now being taken? under european law, infringements of civil rights are permissible, but they are justifiable only if they are necessary, proportionate and effective and if people can also defend themselves against abuse. i would like the council to evaluate all the measures taken in recent years and to examine them against these criteria. can you promise that right now? let me give you some points that merit careful consideration. first of all, on the point of abuse: can't civilians be the unintended victims? i would like to mention, for example, the creation of lists of terror suspects. the question is not just how you get on it, but how you get off it. because suppose your name is on one of these lists. all assets and bank accounts are frozen, but the authorities do not automatically prosecute. so there's no trial where the evidence is involved. the principle of innocence until proven guilty is being abandoned. but i'll tell you what, i can imagine you freezing bank accounts before you tell people, or else that money's gonna fly halfway around the world. i cannot imagine, however, that we will accept that people continue to be listed and therefore punished without any proof of guilt. that is a reversal of the burden of proof in criminal law that is unacceptable. the court of first instance has already stated on a number of occasions that the procedure should be adjusted. on 11 july, it was already judged that the al-aqsa foundation and josé maria sison had been unlawfully listed. i would like to hear from the council what lessons it has learned from these court decisions and whether it is prepared to change or review the decisionmaking on the composition of these lists and also to look at the possibilities for individuals to claim their rights. now about the effectiveness. are the measures effective? let me take the measures on liquids as an example. more than a million people in europe still wash their hands with plastic bags before boarding their plane. they put the 100 ml bottles in there very nicely - they often end up in the waste bin because you forgot what the rule was - and civil rights are not being violated very seriously and abuse is also quite possible. but is the measure effective now? is the council prepared to assess this on the basis of scientific evidence? i'll get to the cup proportionally. are the measures proportionate? it is also an important point for evaluation. let me take the example of passenger data, the agreement with the united states. my group can also imagine that countries want to know who is coming in. the big problem is not so much that too much data is required, but that it can be requested for a wide variety of purposes. the council is always justifying such a measure by saying that all this is necessary in the fight against terrorism. but the data can also be used for other matters "according to us law", as stated in the agreement. so it's theoretically possible that it's not just about counterterrorism, but also about tackling bike theft or bank fraud. is it proportionate to use it for that? are you prepared to challenge this agreement on that basis? the last is necessity. for example, my group was against the storage of traffic data for a period of up to two years, but the majority of this house was in favour of it. but all the data i've seen so far shows that data up to three months in particular is very effective and necessary. are you prepared to investigate whether data stored for more than three months has actually been used? in short, are you prepared to make an evaluation on the basis of the criteria of effectiveness, proportionality, necessity and also to examine whether there is a possibility of preventing abuse? the last point for evaluation is the council's attitude to human rights violations within the european union in relation to the cia's activities. we have done our best as the european parliament and i find it scandalous that we have never heard a comment from the council. you can say what you think, what you think is right and what is not, and what action you want to take. are you prepared to respond to the fava report or will you continue to remain silent?
