mr president, there is a lot to say about this financial crisis, but i will use my two short minutes to focus on three areas where i think we need to take action. the first is the lack of regulation. several members have said this already. complex new products, and particularly the diversification and securitisation of risks, is something which is a key element in this crisis. it was sold to us as a perfect method to get rid of risks, but it is now clear that diversification has also meant a proliferation of risks and of the insecurity that is now everywhere. the second element concerning regulation is that these operations largely take place outside the regulated market: over-the-counter activities and hedge funds, as has been mentioned already. i really think that we have to investigate where these blind spots are leading. i know mr mccreevy does not agree, but i think it is really high time now to look into possibilities of regulation – not to over-regulate, not to panic, but really to conduct a serious study. that is what the commission should do. another element is the lack of information and transparency. this has also been mentioned by several people. i want to mention the ratings agencies as well, and the accounting rules which mrs berès also mentioned – the fair value issue. this is linked to my third point, which is the lack of coordination and cooperation. that is an element which we have seen now in the actions that have been undertaken by the ecb, which correctly provided liquidity but did not have the information and does not have the tools to punish those who caused the crisis. there should be a much closer link between the ecb and its knowledge and the supervisors. a very important element is cooperation and the strengthening and reinforcing of european-level supervision, which really has a grip on these phenomena at the top of the market and these new phenomena. i have mentioned several times that we need such european-level supervision. people then tell me, even if they agree, that you have to wait for a crisis to appear because the sense of urgency is not there. i think now it is high time, and the crisis is there, so now we should act.
the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster is a good one. it is therefore good that policy-making is concerned with this. in fact, desertification has two causes: a natural and a human cause. drought as a natural cause can lead to poor soil conditions, especially when combined with human causes such as poor management of agricultural land, poor forest management and poor water management. the risk of desertification is therefore increasing, especially in the mediterranean regions of europe. with many climate scientists predicting a warming climate with a greater risk of extreme droughts, it is good to take preventive measures to limit the effects of drought, such as desertification. i believe that policy should address both the natural and the human causes of desertification. as far as natural causes are concerned, it is important to use the water available as effectively as possible. water retention should be applied where possible and soil drying should be avoided. the latter can be achieved, for example, by ensuring good forest management. good water management is also important. the existing water legislation can also be used in this context. in my own country, the netherlands, there is a national coordination centre which takes action whenever there is a threat of drought and which implements measures at regional level. the framework can be set at european or united nations level, but implementation will still have to be mainly national and regional. i also think that voluntary cooperation between the problem areas to learn how to deal with the problem is an effective way of doing this. this can be done in the form of a knowledge platform with both political and scientific input. the implementation of the high water directive also involves such initiatives and seems to have a positive impact.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. the promotion of human rights in countries with which the union has relations is a task which it must carry out in a responsible manner. structured human rights dialogues are indeed of great importance in this respect. that is why it is painful and sad to note that the expert seminar with china did not take place last may. the maintenance of good sino-european relations, however, requires mutual commitment. recent alarming reports from amnesty international, psy, human rights in china and compass direct, for example, have made me question this commitment. so-called opponents, including religious minorities, but also journalists are being sent to psychiatric hospitals for political purposes. the infamous ankang system is a chinese reality, especially in the run-up to the olympic games. that's a bad one. i therefore ask the council and the commission to keep a watchful eye on the human rights situation in the middle kingdom. a harmonious relationship with this strategic partner, the people's republic of china, is important, but not at any price, so that what the rapporteur states in her report, 'that human rights dialogues are an essential part of the union's overall strategy to promote sustainable development, peace and stability', will be put into practice alongside theory.
mr president, the widespread attention to human rights is relatively new. in the past, this attention was mainly from a socialist or a liberal inspiration. the left was concerned mainly with the right to housing, income, amenities, participation, a clean environment, peace and freedom from colonial occupation, not only for those who already have the right, but especially for all those who cannot yet exercise that right or who cannot exercise it sufficiently. for liberals, it was mainly about individual freedoms and the right to enterprise. for some other political forces, human rights were less important. even now, with more agreement than ever before on the importance of human rights, there remains a gap between ideal and reality. the most prosperous and democratic states tend to support, for the benefit of the rest of the world, regimes which can guarantee the supply of gas and oil and which seek to destroy movements at home which conflict with western interests. the two-pronged approach is an invitation to dismiss justified criticism of some other countries as useless, arrogant meddling. the valenciano martínez-orozco report rightly points out that the endless pursuit of human rights dialogues with unwilling governments cannot be an objective, nor should such dialogues be bogged down by a lack of transparency and democratic control. there is broad agreement on this report. the amendments to it come mainly from three flemish members of the its group. the shortcomings they have identified are largely justified, but their wording and omissions give the impression that they are not seeking solutions but are complacent about the lack of results. it is an illusion that europe can better isolate itself from the rest of the world in order to guarantee peace, prosperity and security in its own territory. we will not support any amendment which does not contribute to equality and democracy for all and to solidarity with the poor and hungry of the world.
the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire is a very good one. fossil fuels are finite, and the use of renewable energy reduces greenhouse gas emissions. it also provides the necessary diversification to reduce our dependence on single sources of energy in the future. i wholeheartedly support the rapporteur's wish to use biofuels only in a responsible way. the eu should work towards international agreements to use sustainability criteria for biofuels in the wto context. the type of biofuel we use and how it is grown and used are very important. biofuels are only sustainable if the entire cycle from producer to user shows optimal environmental performance. this means that both the production and the combustion of biomass will take place in a sustainable way. production involves, for example, replanting of harvested crops. the emission standard for combustion should not be less stringent than for other fuels. in addition, more far-reaching strategic choices are needed because of undesirable effects such as the destruction of primary forests for production purposes and competition with the food supply. in particular, in poor countries, the production of biomass is putting pressure on agricultural land and raising food prices. what policy is the commission pursuing to counteract these undesirable effects in practice? in conclusion: the rapporteur's aim is to take account of the different situations in the member states. one member state has more potential for solar energy, for example, while another has more potential for wind energy. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the aid is not used to finance the production of the products concerned. a support system is most effective when it is tailored to the renewable energy sources with the greatest potential, and that varies, mr president, from one member state to another.
president. - mr thomsen has written an excellent report on renewable energy and i wholeheartedly support her commitment and ambition of 25%. climate change is a huge challenge, but fighting climate change also offers great opportunities. opportunities in the field of new technologies and clean and sustainable energy. in addition to established technologies such as wind turbines and solar panels, there are likely to be energy sources such as one kilometer-high wind, solar mirrors, tidal energy, wave energy, geothermal energy and algae-based fuels. it is an exciting development and i welcome it wholeheartedly. investing in renewable energy has more benefits than just reducing co2. it reduces dependence on third countries, it breaks the dominance of the big energy giants and it leads not only to enormous innovation but also to new jobs, some 1 million jobs. that, ladies and gentlemen, is all very well, but it does require good policy. three points are important. firstly, the need for disengagement; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the current crisis. it may take a long time before a wind farm can be connected to the electricity grid, and that is unacceptable. i hope that the proposals put forward by the european commission this week on this point can be strengthened a little more, that the pressure of the energy monopolists can be resisted and that the proposals can be prevented from being watered down. secondly, the biofuels. there are good biofuels and bad biofuels. everyone seems to be focusing on the bad biofuels these days. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the failure of the member states to implement the directive. some biofuels have a very negative impact on biodiversity and food prices. but under strict conditions, biofuels can make a good contribution to a more sustainable energy supply, but then there must be guarantees that tropical forests are not cut down to fill our cars. i support the paragraph in britta's report in which she says that 10% biofuel blending should be linked to clear criteria. i have made proposals in my report on the fuel quality directive and i look forward to your reactions. finally, the subsidies. unfortunately, subsidies for the production of fossil fuels are still being provided. the european coal sector has received some eur 50 billion in product-related aid over the past 10 years. kerosene is exempt from excise duty, a disguised subsidy. the trend is now towards generous subsidies for renewable energy. that's not the way. we must end fossil fuel subsidies as soon as possible. instead of providing subsidies, we should oblige energy companies to make a large part of their production sustainable. then there's no need for subsidies. we must be careful not to trade the oil addiction for a new subsidy addiction.
president. - the three reports which are being presented again at third reading as the third railway package deal with very different matters. the most urgent of these is to improve the ability of train drivers to cross national borders instead of having to be replaced by their foreign colleagues. from the railway staff's point of view, all three versions were open to improvement. nevertheless, my group has been able to follow the compromises of the rapporteur, mr savary, and we will also agree to the third version. in the field of passenger rights in crossborder rail transport, we have missed an opportunity. one of the areas where the european union can do useful work is to protect as much as possible rail passengers crossing borders from the negative effects of competition, unnecessary fragmentation of companies and national short-sightedness. the european commission has put forward a good proposal which will ensure that ticket sales and information, as in the past, will provide a good long-distance service. competition and cost constraints are increasingly limiting supply to individual member states and their immediate surroundings. only in germany are these facilities well regulated and it would be good if all railway undertakings followed suit. a majority in this parliament has moved the emphasis considerably on the proposal of rapporteur sterckx. much more attention is now being paid to compensation for delays in domestic rail traffic and much less to the continuing deterioration of international rail traffic. the commission is now responding to complaints from passengers who are unnecessarily losing time by having to buy new rail tickets after crossing the border by saying that it had a good proposal but that the european parliament has made that impossible. since no better alternative is available at the moment and we support other parts of these proposals, we will also accept this final result. we shall continue to insist on supplementing it with the unfortunately missing improvements which were at the heart of the original proposal. the third point remains the most controversial for my group. not only now, but also in the two previous railway packages, the rapporteur jarzembowski is advocating more competition and market operation. this starts with cross-border freight transport but eventually covers all rail transport including domestic passenger transport. the lower working conditions in road and air freight transport are thus also being absorbed into rail transport. my group believes that the reverse should have happened, namely better protection and regulation of road and air transport. we do not share the view that competition is more likely to solve the problems of cross-border freight or even passenger transport than good cooperation between national railway companies. we shall not support these proposals at third reading either.
mr president, in my previous contribution to the debate on this third railway package, i indicated that i would not be happy with every outcome of this dossier. we are now a few months further along and i conclude that the agreement reached with the council represents a constructive step towards further completion of the european railway market in important respects. the creation of the internal rail market regularly faces practical problems, some of which are being solved by this package. it has been uncertain for a long time whether parliament did not want to move too fast to allow an agreement between council and parliament, or whether it wanted to tackle problems which did not exist in practice or whose approach would create new problems. i am thinking in particular of the degree of market opening in national passenger transport and the scope of the rules on the certification of personnel. the final agreement between the council and parliament strikes a good balance between addressing existing problems and presenting a sensible future scenario in terms of extending the scope of the current agreements. the commission has also taken account of the fact that the market access dossier is based on existing contracts. this avoids mandatory breach of contract and the associated claims. the idea of the levy to finance public service obligations is acceptable under present conditions. in contrast to the agreements on market access and certification, the agreements on passenger rights are in principle the broadest possible. i think that is a good thing, given the agreements that have been reached in the sector. the agreements reached are largely a reflection of voluntary agreements in the sector, and i welcome that. as a keen cyclist, i am particularly pleased with the provisions on the transport of bicycles by train. i hope that they will encourage a concrete contribution to the solution of the congestion problem. it is a good example of the commodity in passenger transport. in conclusion, i would like to thank the rapporteurs for their efforts and congratulate them on the result achieved. their efforts have helped to bring the common european railway market a step closer. i would also like to thank the german presidency and commissioner barrot.
i would like to thank the rapporteurs, mr president, and also vice-president vidal-quadras roca, for their efforts to bring this whole complex third railway package to a successful conclusion. president. - some rapporteurs have had to suck it up because they would have liked to have achieved more, but i think the result is quite justifiable. it is a positive development that there are now basic rights for all rail passengers in europe. liability and the right to transport for people with reduced mobility are now much better regulated. the fact that additional rules are being made compulsory in long-distance transport is also a good thing and that there are rules which do not fit in, for example, urban and regional transport; so it is good that an exception has been made and that they are going to be covered by a different regime. mr president, we have had a very good debate on the liberalization of rail transport. not only with the council, but also here in parliament. i'm satisfied with the outcome. compulsory liberalization of international rail transport, but not of national rail transport. the differences between the member states are too great to make national liberalization of internal communications compulsory from europe, because regional networks which are subject to pressure are vulnerable and must be able to be tendered out by hand. that too is a form of market operation. i'm happy with that result. it's prudent market action, but i think it's appropriate to these complex networks. you indicated that you would like to carry out further research into further steps towards liberalization of domestic passenger transport; i would ask you to include the private procurement of networks and the extent to which this contributes to market functioning in your further studies.
vice-president of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. toys are not products like any other, they are designed for the most vulnerable consumer group, and it is therefore perfectly clear that we must have the highest standards of child safety in relation to toys. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i would first like to point out that in both cases the products in question were not authorized for the european market under european law. toys with lead-based paint are, of course, unlike in the united states of america banned in europe for a long time. toys with magnetic or other dangerous parts that could come off have, of course, been banned in europe for a long time. so what we have here is not a problem of the law in force - although i will come to the law in force, which also needs to change - but it is a problem of the enforcement and respect of the law in force. there is no 100% safety in the products we use every day. the best legislator in the world cannot guarantee that there will be no mistakes in production. the best legislator in the world cannot guarantee that there will not be a problem somewhere in the supply chain, which is now organised worldwide. the best legislator in the world cannot prevent fraudulent and criminal acts. i would remind you of the case in greece where three people died because of the use of a defective steam iron. this iron had not only the european ce mark but also a special german safety mark. however, the device was not safe, and that was because it was counterfeit. no legislator in the world can do anything about it. that is the responsibility of the manufacturers and it is the responsibility of those who have to ensure that the manufacturers comply with the existing rules. these are the market surveillance authorities of the member states. there is no european system of market surveillance. this is a matter for the member states alone. i would like to point out that we are dealing with a problem which is very closely linked to economic globalisation. we are dealing with globally organized supply chains today. we are dealing with companies which are under cost pressure which leads them to put their suppliers under cost pressure and to offer them ever lower prices. the risk is very great that this will be to the detriment of security in some places. that is why we have always said that the more international and extensive the supply chain, the greater the responsibility of companies to ensure that the existing rules are respected at every stage of the supply chain. this was not the case, for example, in the case of lead-based paints in toys of the american manufacturer. here a chinese supplier supplied a paint which it should not have supplied. that's the truth of the matter. the first is the responsibility of enterprises: they are responsible for ensuring that their product complies with the european regulations in force. the member states are responsible for ensuring that market surveillance is effective. and then we as european legislators have to ensure that our legislation is up to date and that potential risks are identified and addressed in legislation in good time. this is why the commission began more than two years ago to make a fundamental revision of the existing toy directive. the toy directive is one of the oldest directives to follow the so-called new approach. it is clear that the toy market has changed dramatically over the last 20 years and this new directive will therefore address a whole range of newly identified risks and hazards and ensure that hazards are avoided by very strict rules for example on the use of chemicals in toys. the directive will be submitted to the legislator before the end of this year. we also presented to you at the beginning of this year a whole package of measures to strengthen market surveillance and to better protect the ce marking. let me say something about the ce marking at this point: the ce marking means that the manufacturer, whether a european manufacturer or a manufacturer from outside europe who wishes to sell in europe, gives a full guarantee that his product fully complies with the rules in force in europe. the manufacturer guarantees this with the ce mark, that is the ce mark. in a number of cases, the manufacturer is not allowed to use the ce marking on the basis of self-declaration and self-testing, but must have it certified by a third party, namely whenever the malfunctioning of the product could pose a risk to the user. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of the products is not compromised. the functioning of these bodies, which we call conformity assessment bodies, can be significantly improved, and we have made the appropriate proposals, which are before you as the european legislator. in the case of toys, we now have a very difficult question for the commission and for you as legislator to decide: do we want to move to a situation where every single toy that comes onto the market is certified by a third party, or do we want to leave it to the manufacturers to take the guarantee and therefore the liability with the use of the ce mark and i must add that under the rules in force today, manufacturers must at all times prove by extensive documentation that they have used the ce mark lawfully or do we want to go a third way, perhaps by defining in the new toys directive certain types of toys for which we will require certification by a third party? i do not want to conceal my deep sympathy for this final solution. so let me give you an example: what do we do with all the toys that have chips in them? chips, when kids put them in their mouths, are a dangerous thing. it must therefore be ensured that such parts of toys cannot, under any circumstances, endanger children. if we don't want to take away the game boy from the kids -- and i don't think anyone has the intention of doing that -- then i think the only way to do that is to mandate third-party certification at this point. the commission's thinking is in that direction and i hope that this debate will give me some idea of what parliament thinks on this matter. the last point i would like to make is a request to producers and consumer organizations: producers must be aware of the fact that even such a high cost pressure does not relieve them of the responsibility to fully control every single part of their production process and supply chain. the more international production and supply chains become, the greater the responsibility of the companies operating on the market. the consumer organisations would also do a great deal of good by raising awareness that cheap does not always mean good. this is not only a question of safety, but also a question of quality, because how many times have you seen it - especially with toys that the child unpacks under the christmas tree - that the child plays with it once and it is already broken. it was cheap, but not good! cheap doesn't always mean good, and cheap doesn't always mean safe. this means that consumers must be aware that the price of a product is linked to its quality and safety. and this is a matter of growing concern to me with the low-priced ideology that is spreading in at least some member states. i can summarize by saying that the european legislation we have today is certainly better than what we have in other parts of the world, that it can be improved and will be improved, and that the strictest standards must be applied and must be applied where toys are concerned.
member of the commission. madam president, it is really an honour and pleasure for me to stand here with vice-president verheugen to address parliament on the critical issue of dangerous products and product safety. the commission is united, as you see, in tackling this issue and happy to give you its position from two complementary points of view. our open society and our open economy offer citizens choices that were unaffordable and/or unimaginable only a generation ago. this is major progress, but it comes with a challenge, as we have seen in recent months. i refer, of course, to concerns about the safety of products and, in particular, children’s toys. citizens need to know that their safety is underpinned by sound rules, efficient and vigorous authorities and responsible business. i will start this brief exposé with a few words about law and its enforcement, because it is crucial. i believe that the current regulatory framework for consumer product safety in the eu is fundamentally sound and will be reinforced by the pending commission proposal on the new approach directives. i refer in particular to the general product safety directive with its rapex system referring to a fundamentally sound framework. we already have the legal framework to deal with dangerous products made in china or anywhere else. the challenge of ensuring an internal market of safe goods lies primarily in the effective enforcement of the present legal framework, and, if we read the directive again carefully, this enforcement power belongs to the member states. the member states’ market surveillance authorities and customs controls have a duty to ensure that dangerous products are not placed on the market, or are withdrawn or recalled. the member states must have in place effective, proportionate and dissuasive penalties for infringements, and it is written in article 7 of the general product safety directive that it is really the responsibility of the member states. of course, enforcement by public authorities is only complementary to businesses respecting their legal and ethical obligations, as commissioner verheugen stated just minutes ago. it is the responsibility of manufacturers, importers and distributors to make sure that they place only safe products on the market and take necessary measures where they become aware of a risk to consumer safety. just a few words about recent product recalls, which, of course, are our major concern. on the issue of these recent dangerous product recalls, the commission would be more worried by silence than by activity, and transparency builds trust. that is why i can only encourage the national authorities to enhance their surveillance and to inform the commission about dangerous goods and not just to keep silent for one reason or another, such as not wanting to bother people before christmas or just trying to tackle the problem in silence. of course, questions remain. are operators notifying problems sufficiently quickly, and are their internal audits up to scratch? how can we ensure earlier and more effective intervention by the market surveillance authorities and better monitoring of product recalls? nevertheless, the recent recalls have shown that improvements can and should be made on the entire product supply chain, encompassing, amongst other things, product design, suppliers of contractor management, manufacturing process controls and finished product testing. i would like to talk for a while on relations with china and the united states. the commission also questions whether china is doing enough. during my recent visit to china, my main message was that europe does not and will not compromise on consumer safety. i stressed that and obtained the commitment of the chinese authorities on specific progress targets. there is no argument from the market which could compete with the safety of products and the safety of people’s lives. i stand prepared to bring them to task should they fail to deliver. in october, i expect the report from the chinese authorities, and i am really doing everything possible to assure them that i will not hesitate to take measures if they fail to deliver. constructive cooperation with china has already produced results – withdrawn export licences of certain substandard products. i have to say that i spent four days in china and, within these four days, there were recalls of the licences of the two biggest toy manufacturers. this puts europe well ahead in comparison with other developed countries facing similar issues. as you know, on 16 september, the working group established by the us president issued a report with quite similar measures to those europe has already taken on board to secure the safety of goods. we are also in contact with the us consumer product safety authorities and policy makers with a view to mutually reinforcing our messages to the chinese government. i have already talked with the chairwoman of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection (imco), ms mccarthy, about the results from their visit to the united states. next week, when i am in the us, i will check how we could cooperate. the aim is to give a crystal clear signal to the chinese. adequate safety guarantees will not work as a bargaining chip in relations with one or another economic block; they are a sine qua non for access to either. i would like to conclude with the next steps which we envisage. the commission is committed to making every effort to ensure that toys sold for the christmas season will not lead to unpleasant surprises, and this commitment is very clear. maybe we need a kind of christmas pact with producers, with importers, with all the authorities in the member states, which should ensure vigorous implementation, and consumer organisations. the commission will update parliament regularly on developments, in particular on the conclusion of the stocktaking exercise reviewing the strengths and weaknesses of the consumer product safety mechanism currently in place in mid-november, and following the forthcoming eu-china summit at the end of november. at the beginning of next month, on the third, there will be a gathering of all the national authorities in brussels at which we will compare our views on how the directives on safety are implemented everywhere. we have already had extensive talks with all the toy makers, including mattel, involving the sharing of practices. as i recently informed the imco committee, this is no time for knee-jerk reactions. together with vice-president verheugen and a number of fellow commissioners, we will ensure the necessary, proportionate and appropriate response to the issues that have surfaced with regard to public confidence in the governance of global product safety. although commissioner verheugen was really quite exhaustive in his exposé, let me say that as regards revision of the so-called new approach package, taking special care of the health and safety of european citizens requires us to preserve the specific and successful system established by the general product safety directive. by the end of this year, we will produce the first of the reports we are obliged to produce every three years according to the directive, and you will see that this mechanism is really an effective one based on community, solidarity and proportionality. i am convinced of your view as well in asking to put safety before one-size-fits-all streamlining and to consider favourably the draft regulation as originally proposed by the commission. we need also to be aware that this is not happening for the first time. if you compare how we have been through all the unpleasant events which happen on the market regarding food safety, we can really enhance our institutional comparison and do our best on product safety because in this globalised world we cannot just be an island. we need to exercise our control and to assure european citizens that the attention from community authorities is the same everywhere and complements the efforts of the member states.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. mr president, i am pleased that there are even two commissioners here, so that is a very forward-looking approach to a problem that has recently arisen with mattel. this problem is of course twofold. and that's also shown by the fact that it's less serious when it comes to china, and that mattel has also made a mistake. this problem has a two-fold purpose, as i said. first, i think - and i repeat clinton - that politicians should think first and then act. president clinton said that at a campaign rally last year, and that we should not shoot immediately into stress like a toy when a button is pressed. i too am sometimes guilty of the fact that when something happens, we politicians get stressed out and immediately want tougher measures, stricter regulations. i think we should think before we act. fortunately, we did that with our resolution. then i come to the second point, why this mattel problem has a positive consequence as well; we are going to think very specifically about the importance of the ce marking, a safety mark, and how we can improve it. i fully agree with what commissioner verheugen says about the fact that 100% security and safety will not be achieved, but - i agree with mr gebhardt - we must strive for that 100% security. if it is a ce mark, then there is a 100% liability. this is of course very important for the consumer. if there is a ce marking, then the consumer knows that if something goes wrong with the product i have bought, i have a right of recourse to the manufacturer or importer. the resolution on which we are voting tomorrow, mr president, deals mainly with this. i think we have prepared an excellent resolution; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the controls are carried out in a fair and efficient manner. we cannot leave it to the individual member states to start checking in third countries. i think that a coordinated european approach in cooperation with the member states is necessary for this and i hope that the commission will work towards that. secondly, mr president, consumers must be clear about the quality labels. we have the ce marking and we are also calling in the resolution for a safety mark for toys, preferably to replace all existing national marks. it is not that there is a choice of labels from which the consumer cannot see through the trees and therefore cannot make a clear choice, because then i think the objective is not self-evident. something has to be clear, less is better. i think that's the most important thing. i hope that we can then introduce these labels on a voluntary basis, because the market will force it. if we are going to make it compulsory, we as a government must monitor it and i think that the market, the consumer, will choose safety and a clear label above all. if that happens, i will be very happy with my group. we are also looking forward to the revision of the toy directive, and you have already said a few things about that.
the european union and china are important trading partners. this means that china can be held responsible for the quality of the products supplied. the european union cannot accept unsafe clothing or unsafe toys. the union must never allow its economic interests in china to prevail over the interests of european consumers. this debate shows once again that these consumers are not only served with low prices, but much more with quality and safety, as commissioner verheugen has also pointed out. this is especially true when the safety of children is at stake. i do not want to look at the importers alone in this debate. i'm definitely looking at the chinese manufacturers, too. security is not a topic that is just a matter of products reaching europe. safety thinking should be a guiding principle at the earliest possible stage in the production chain. after all, security is not a luxury or a nice extra, not here and not in china. i call on the council and the commission to convey this message to our chinese trading partners in a clear and forceful manner and what commissioner kuneva has already said in particular gives me full confidence that this will happen. i wish her a lot of perseverance in this difficult task.
it is good that we in parliament are making our voice heard in the debate in the media, because product safety also affects our citizens and our children very directly. i am here, mr president, with a positive attitude, because international trade contributes to greater prosperity, here and elsewhere in the world. china is a major trading partner. that is why we must invest in good contacts and a fair trade relationship that is good for china, but also for our citizens in europe. we cannot and must not tolerate the products of our trading partners not meeting the safety standards we have laid down in legislation. about half of the warnings for dangerous imported products are for chinese products. not just toys, but toothpaste, body lotion, beds, paint scrubs, dog and cat food, even pacemakers. we must not only improve our regulations and controls here in europe, but we must also make a real effort to ensure that safe products are produced in china and in third countries. that is of course the responsibility of the producers themselves who must not only go for the lowest price, but it is also the responsibility of our commerce commissioner, mr mandelson. i very much appreciate the presence of two commissioners here, but would you also ensure that commissioner mandelson puts product safety much higher on the agenda of the trade talks, because that is where we can enforce measures and that is where we have to show our teeth. of course we must also provide technical assistance, but product safety must be an important part of trade agreements such as with china; if necessary, we have a stick behind the door, namely sanctions. so, commissioner kuneva, use the tough hand we have in our trade relations, involve commissioner mandelson in some of this, because unfortunately he has not shown any sensitivity to this subject so far.
in writing. since our party, the dutch socialist party, attaches great importance to renewable energy sources and believes that much more extensive efforts should be made in this field, we did not vote against this report. however, there are also some very negative things. firstly, we strongly reject nuclear energy as a sustainable renewable energy source. we also oppose the idea of liberalising energy markets at european level. finally, we believe that solar, wind and hydroelectric energy are better alternatives to biofuels.

state to make its own mark on eu policy during the presidency. however, i insist that this space be filled only with due regard for the continuity of the current eu policy agenda. i share the concern interregional agreement. i would therefore ask the commission what initiatives it is taking to alleviate these concerns in countries such as argentina, uruguay and paraguay. trade relations with these brazil, as the natural leader of the continent, is seeking to make its mark on the world stage. this is reflected not only in an assertive role within the wto but also, for example, in the desire to have a seat on the security council. this has made brazil less aware of its own role on the continent. mercosur is a customer of only 10% of brazil's exports. it also seems less willing to invest in other mercosur countries. brazil thus indicates that its role in the region has outgrown its role in the world. the other side of this brazilian policy is the threat to the stability and political direction of mercosur. with the withdrawal of brazil, venezuela is trying to take the lead of mercosur and thus of the entire continent. using oil money and anti-western rhetoric, chávez is trying to increase his role in the region. the venezuelan government is pursuing a protectionist, inward-looking economic policy, which threatens to renationalize important economic sectors. there is no room for free trade and the opening up of markets. i can assume that the council and the commission are aware of the impact of these developments on the countries of south america, and certainly on the economic and trade interests of the union in this region. i call on the council and the commission to use the reality of the bilateral partnership with brazil to reverse the trend i have just described. the union should encourage brazil to use the new political weight of the bilateral partnership with the eu to show new political leadership within mercosur and the continent. this is to prevent the isolationist policies of countries like venezuela and bolivia from spreading further across the continent. finally, mr president, i would like to make a comment on the content of the partnership. i believe that the eu should focus its partnership with brazil not only on issues such as bioethanol but also on the smooth running of the doha round. the role of brazil has been too much dominated by its own successes in agriculture, while it is not really prepared to compromise on nama and services.
