the rapporteur. - mr president, i should like to begin by thanking the many colleagues who have contributed to this document. seven years ago i was co-rapporteur on the directive we are now discussing the evaluation of. all this time we have been drawing parliament-wide support, except for a few dissenters who, as you will see, have tabled amendments. we have had many and sometimes heated discussions, for example on the sharing of the burden of proof, but fortunately we have never lost sight of the common objective and i hope that you will see this report as a joint project. in 2000, we voted by a large majority in favour of the directive which can rightly be described as revolutionary. discrimination was prohibited throughout europe in the labour market, social security and housing and equal treatment was made compulsory. i was, and i'm extremely proud of it. it was a good job done by the commission, the council and the european parliament. but that does not mean that reality has changed radically. there is still a lot of discrimination. in recent years, the number and severity of discrimination and racism have actually increased. unemployment among migrants is significantly higher in most countries than among the rest of the labour force. there's more racist violence. legislation is very important in combating discrimination, because it also has a normative effect. it is a symbol of what the european union stands for and how we want to deal with each other and what cannot be tolerated. legislation also gives people tools to seek redress against discrimination. the european commission is now very busy checking whether the member states have properly transposed the european agreements into national legislation. i have also spoken to commissioner špidla about this this week and i see that the commission is of good will and in good consultation with the member states it wants to see the legislation implemented. i really commend the commission on that. but i cannot entirely escape the impression that there is a certain amount of pepper in it, a certain amount of agency. these laws should have been implemented in 2004. it is being shielded with sensitivities and difficult details, but this commission said when it took office that anti-discrimination policy would be at the heart of its policy and you are already halfway through your mandate. i absolutely respect what you have done so far, but i hope you will start to take a harder look at the member states. it's much needed. less than half of the countries have fully implemented the directive. for example, in many countries the definition of direct and indirect discrimination, of intimidation and of the burden of proof has not been properly translated. there are also a number of member states which, although prohibiting discrimination in the labour market, consider it a step too far to prohibit discrimination in the supply of goods and services. yet there are many problems there. take education, for example. according to the new eu fundamental rights agency, children from roma families are often wrongly placed in schools for mentally handicapped children. or look at housing; apartments are regularly advertised with the remark that the apartment building is free of foreigners. that cannot be accepted. the prohibition of discrimination, including outside the labour market, must be implemented immediately. mr president, a law is only effective if citizens know their rights, but according to eurobarometer only 35% of those questioned think that there are anti-discrimination laws in their country. even a lower percentage say they know what to do when they have been the victim of discrimination. how will the commission ensure that the law becomes a reality in people's lives? the directive includes an obligation to inform people, but how will the commission do this? president. - even if people know that there is a law, the road may still be full of holes, because sometimes there are demands that are problematic. in some countries, for example, the period for lodging a complaint is very short, for example only 30 days. the rest of the procedure, on the other hand, can sometimes be absurdly long and extremely complicated. infringements of anti-discrimination policies should be addressed through effective, proportionate and dissuasive penalties. all that is well and good in that directive; that's what we agreed on. yet very few countries have adequate penalties. maybe it's time for a positive note. the good news is that almost all countries now have equal treatment bodies. that is an important point, and in many countries it is not just about racial discrimination, but it is about broader grounds. i applaud that. but unfortunately not all bodies have the funds to carry out their tasks and i ask the commission to take this into account when assessing the implementation of anti-discrimination legislation; can the commission comment on this? president. - the next item is the report (doc. i'm proud of the legislation. progress is being made in implementing national laws, but it is not yet sufficient. many countries have not yet done so fully. but even if they have done so in the strictest sense, we still have to ensure that people are also informed about their rights, because if people do not know what rights they have, they will never be able to get justice in the end, and that is extremely important in combating discrimination.
president. - mr president, we are making laws here in the european parliament, but laws alone cannot eliminate racism and xenophobia. statements by politicians and other opinion leaders contribute to a climate of hatred and intolerance, in which discrimination is commonplace and so the law remains a dead letter. i would like the european commission to be as vigorous in denouncing discrimination in europe as it is in denouncing violations of market rules, for example. we must protect human capital in europe as well as monetary capital. if we can tackle bill gates, we can certainly tackle corporations and governments that discriminate. we know that 14 governments are still not complying with the equal treatment directive on certain points, but the commission seems to be in no hurry to enforce the rules and is giving countries far too much time to comply with the rules. that law should have been implemented years ago; bill gates wouldn't get away with it. in the fight against terrorism and crime, we must not push our boundaries either. some measures are exclusive to certain groups and appear to legitimize discrimination. for example, stop and search practices, profiling methods, etc., etc. i am glad to see that the previous government, for example, wanted to introduce integration measures in my country, which were subsequently banned by the courts because they were discriminatory. among the amendments, i noted one, amendment no 4, which states that the protection of minorities and the enforcement of anti-discrimination legislation is a national matter. if there is one thing that is not national, it is our common european values, and it is therefore europe's prerogative to ensure equal treatment for all citizens of europe, because that is the aim of european integration and the market is only a means to that end. finally, freedom of religion is also a great good and a fundamental right, but it should not be abused to justify discrimination on the basis of ethnicity. for example, a catholic school cannot, under the guise of religious freedom, reject children of a certain ethnicity because they actually prefer a white school. conversely, criticism of islam is not automatically racist. finally, mr president, the laws are important and it is very good that we have this law, but we are all here to ensure that the climate remains such that discrimination no longer occurs.
i would like to thank mrs buitenweg, the president, for the excellent work she has done on this report. whether it is discrimination and racism in the labour market, in sports clubs or in schools, on all fronts it is a violation of the values we stand for in europe. what we need is an integrated approach at all levels, european, national and local. legislators, politicians and victims of discrimination themselves have an important role to play in this. we must make european citizens aware of their rights and of the means they have to assert them. i would urge the commission to take up its responsibility and ensure that the equal treatment directive is implemented swiftly and adequately. the directive is a step in the right direction, but its effectiveness depends on the member states' implementation. i therefore ask the commission to show the much-needed moral leadership that barrosso himself announced earlier in 2004. in 2007, the year of equal opportunities for all, discrimination is still a major issue in europe. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i therefore join the call on the commission to extend the measures to all possible forms of discrimination.
president. - thank you, mrs kauppi, and congratulations on the report, but i should like to turn now to the commission. the commission, while regularly demonstrating its commitment to gender equality by word of mouth, has again failed time and again in practice to translate its good intentions into concrete action. in fact, instead of taking measures to reduce gender inequality, to combat poverty among women and to help women solve the daily problems they face, the commission's policies often worsen their position. a good example of this is the so-called flexicurity and the relaxation of the right to dismiss. if the commission's proposals in its green paper are implemented, it will be women who will suffer the most, who are already often working on temporary contracts on a temporary basis and for relatively low pay. it is women in particular who have a lot of problems with things like pension building, health care costs and combining care and work. and what is the commission doing? instead of offering women, and workers in general, the security that would make their lives a little easier, the commission wants to create even greater insecurity. we note in this report on inequality between men and women that there are still very large differences in 2007. the fight against this should be given much greater priority, not only in words but also in deeds. gender equality should be automatically integrated into all commission policies. i think it is very sad that we have to remind the commission of this again and again.
i would also like to thank the rapporteur, mr president, for an excellent and very balanced report. several points have already been made. as a member of both the committee on women's rights and the committee on agriculture, i would like to focus on the situation of women in the agricultural sector. the first thing that stands out is the invisibility of many women working in the agricultural sector. 40% of agricultural employment is filled by women, but many official statistics, including those of eurostat, commissioner, look only at female workers on the one hand and female managers on the other, and overlook the category of cooperating partners, whereas this is the largest category of female labour force, particularly in the many family farms in north-west europe. these women make a considerable contribution to the management of the company and often also to the expansion of the company's activities in the countryside. i therefore call on eurostat to make this large group of women visible in official statistics. i call on the commission to take account of this large category of women in its policy. furthermore, it is precisely the category of cooperating partners that in the daily practice of some member states is faced with the necessary problems, for example in the accumulation of pension rights, access to social security, problems in the event of termination of business or divorce, and access to sick leave or maternity leave, although i am glad that action is being taken in the netherlands on this point. i would like to draw the commission's attention to the 'you deserve your pay' project, which in my country, with the support of the esf, is seeking to identify these obstacles for those partners who are cooperating. i hope that the commission will take the conclusions of this study into account in future policy making so that we can maintain a strong european agriculture and a vital rural area with these indispensable female workers.
mr president, i note with great regret that citizens’ rights are clearly not at the heart of the new treaty. unfortunately, the charter of fundamental rights has been sacrificed to the dutch government’s fear of a referendum. the charter of fundamental rights must be legally binding in full and for all because the charter of fundamental rights is the application in practice of our shared values. so, what does an opt-out mean? is it an opt-out from those shared values? or, does it mean that poland and the british government simply pay lip-service to those values but then deny their citizens the means to enforce their rights? what does an opt-out mean? are we not creating a dangerous precedent here? let us ask ourselves, will future countries, future member states, have the same right to an opt-out? if turkey, a country that i very much want to join the european union, asks for an opt-out from the charter of fundamental rights, will it be granted the same right? the other issue is that, between the constitution and the treaty, an additional, lower standard of data protection has somehow been created – a lower standard that applies to the area of the common foreign and security policy. i predict that in the future the member states will try to circumvent data protection rules by labelling, for example, counter-terrorism measures as common foreign and security policy instead of police and justice cooperation. finally, i would ask the commission and the member states to consider, as soon as the treaty has been signed, acting in the spirit of the new treaty and involving the european parliament as a full co-legislator on matters of justice and home affairs, and rapidly closing the democratic gap that we have been living with for so long.
the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force. even cancer patients are not allowed to leave the area. israel says they live in a hostile entity. only food, medicine, a few humanitarian supplies are coming in. but people live off more than flour, lentils and medicine. the israeli definition of humanitarian goods is absurdly restrictive. medical equipment parts are not allowed in, the water main can't be repaired. there is a constant threat of israeli disruption of energy supplies. the economy of gaza has collapsed, the population is unemployed and desperate, there is no money left. malnutrition is starting to show. this blockade breeds despair, anger and hatred, not peace. there is an unacceptable humanitarian crisis in gaza. the demand for aid is growing and the cost of aid is also rising because of the blockade. the member states should therefore increase their financial support to unhra and the other organisations on the ground. the european commission has already taken this step, and some member states have also done so. the european parliament also voted yesterday to increase aid. my group urges the council to support this decision. but help alone is not enough. the presidency will remain here tonight, discreetly. but the european union can no longer assume the moral responsibility of providing aid but not taking political action against the blockade. on behalf of my group, i therefore call on the presidency, the council and the european commission to urge israel to end the blockade immediately. in gaza, a population is being collectively punished. that's illegal under article 33 of the fourth geneva convention. moreover, this method is unworthy of israel. the european union should say this to israel instead of remaining passive, precisely because the union is an ally. this gives meaning to the association agreement with israel. only by acting against the blockade does the eu take the human rights clause seriously.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the basis of the proposals contained in the report. the american peace negotiator dennis ross advised us at the time to provide very practical help to the palestinian people in their precarious existence. a network of reliable, politically independent ngos is needed. the council and the commission, to what extent do you see real possibilities there, both for the west bank and gaza? in the meantime, i believe that the current internal and external crisis in gaza is rooted in the actions of hamas. it invariably delegitimizes the state of israel and legitimizes violence against israelis and so-called dissident palestinians. the recent brutal murder of rami ayyad, a palestinian christian of only 30 years of age, underlines the plight of this minority in gaza. i hope that the council and the commission will pay attention to and support the christian minority in all the palestinian territories.
i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals. i should also like to thank the rapporteur for his work. european citizens want to know how their tax money is spent. the publication of the names of beneficiaries of agricultural subsidies is essential for the transparency of european policy, especially given the enormous share of these subsidies in the eu budget. but, more importantly, such publicity can be the beginning of a much-needed, really fundamental reform of the common agricultural policy. when the subsidies in the netherlands were made public in 2005, we found that a disproportionate share went to a small group of rich people. we give large subsidies to multinationals and large landowners, while thousands of ordinary farmers are barely making ends meet. first of all, there should be a maximum amount of grant that one person or company can receive, and not eur 300 000 as recently proposed by commissioner fischer boel. my proposal is: no more than an average annual salary. most subsidies are intended to provide income support. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right to vote. it is also important to make public where member states have poorly functioning control mechanisms so that farmers and their interest groups can appeal to public authorities if they are harmed. of course, this is only the beginning of a whole series of transparency initiatives by parliament. the distribution of the structural funds should also be made public. i hope that parliament will support this report as widely as possible, because it would make our very primary task of monitoring so much easier. a transparent agricultural policy is a first step towards a fair agricultural policy.
i would also like to thank our colleague, mr president, for this thorough report, which at first sight seems to deal with very technical rules, budgetary rules. but what we are really talking about here are rules which could affect the functioning of our farmers - the legitimate and indispensable producers of our daily food. i want to go over a few of them without starting a very broad discussion about the health check or whatever. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. but, you know, let's just put that aside. first of all, i would like to support the commission's proposal to be able to punish the member state in question more effectively in the event of shortcomings, to be able to cut short, possibly by suspending or reducing interim payments. i think the commission should look not only at the seriousness and the nature of the non-compliance, as it wrote, but also at its duration. for example, in the case of long-term infringements, the commission would have to increase these discount rates again each year. we must also ensure that the new rules we are introducing do not lead to an increase in administrative burdens. finally, the sensitive issue of the announcement of the beneficiaries of the european agricultural fund. my country is already doing a great deal of this and the commission is now proposing to introduce this throughout the eu in order to ensure transparency and the legitimacy of this expenditure. i can support this effort, but i do so with two reservations which are also contained in amendments. first, the disclosure of such information may affect the rights of the persons concerned. we must therefore ensure adequate data protection to prevent data from falling into the wrong hands or being used for the actions of, for example, radical animal activists, a phenomenon that has already been faced by several eu member states. and finally, the commission believes that this proposal will also improve budgetary control. to a certain extent that may be the case, but i think that the introduction of national declarations on the budgetary resources managed at national level is even more beneficial for budgetary control. so i would like to use my last second to call on the commission and the council to speed up the introduction of these national declarations.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). the proposals on the inclusion of the sector in the greenhouse gas emission trading scheme, the agreement with the united states and the revision of landing rights are some examples. the report by mr jensen adds a new dimension to that list: the organisation of aviation on the ground. it concerns the services offered, but also the sound production and the available capacity. as far as ground handling services are concerned, we must indeed await a new evaluation by the commission involving all the member states. i share the suggestions to the commission for future considerations. i'm a little more critical of the capacity clauses. the text calls on the commission to do great things. the master plan should be given a central position in addressing the ground-based aviation capacity problem. a master plan does indeed give a good overview of the existing situation and can identify bottlenecks. i do not think it is desirable, however, that the commission should, with such a plan in hand, force or impede the member states in taking certain steps. a master plan should, above all, promote international cooperation between all those involved, so that, in good consultation and with respect for existing powers, comprehensive solutions to specific and existing bottlenecks can be found. i am glad to see that some of the paragraphs contain useful comments on this. i therefore conclude, mr president, that i can support mr jensen's report. i want to thank her for the pleasure of working with you.
in writing. the distribution of seats in the european parliament among the member states is not based on objective population figures but on past commitments and on the win or loss of negotiations. as a result, the large countries france, italy and great britain always had the same number of seats despite their different populations, as did spain and poland. the same applies to greece, portugal, belgium, the czech republic and hungary. some countries are under-represented, like the netherlands, which in 2000 was foolish enough to settle for fewer seats in parliament in exchange for more votes in the council. we support the rapporteurs' proposal because it is closer to a fair distribution than previous proposals. we reject any amendment which would favour any less fair distribution or which would make a distinction between 'citizens' and other inhabitants in the calculation by country. we support amendments which link the allocation automatically to the population of the member states in future. we reject any future reductions in the number of seats per member state if they are not due to population decline but to the 750 seat limit or to the search for space for electing part of this parliament on transnational lists. we shall vote in favour with that reservation.
draft report by the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy. mr president, i think that one minute for two opinions is very short. anyway, i'll try to keep it as short as possible. pesticides are useful and necessary, and without them it will be difficult to feed six billion mouths. however, much remains to be done to reduce the harmful environmental and health effects of pesticides. the introduction of new products on the market is a long-term and increasingly costly process. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's financial instruments are not used to finance the creation of new jobs. the industry is increasingly focusing on the big five crops: rice, grain, corn, soybeans and cotton, because they can earn a lot of money back. but of course it is important that small farms also have good protection and the committee on industry has made some good suggestions to that end. the committee on industry also makes some good suggestions for introducing incentives to produce more sustainable products. that is also very important. i have one more comment on the use of pesticides. the mandatory buffer zone of 10 metres from surface water is not a good idea for some member states. i therefore welcome the amendment tabled by the pse group on this point. surface water is now one of the test criteria for approval and member states have the right to establish a buffer zone for resources themselves on this basis. all in all, i think that parliament's position is well balanced and workable.
in the debate on pesticides, mr president, i have always tried to adopt a realistic and balanced attitude between the interests of the environment on the one hand and the interests of producers and consumers on the other. i should like to go into some of the aspects of the reports before us. one of the proposals is to base policy on reducing consumption. i am very sceptical about this. you can reduce the total amount of pesticides, but often the most common pesticides are the least dangerous. the risks remain virtually the same. i see a lot more in policies based on risk reduction. the netherlands has been applying this since the year 2000 and it has been shown to reduce environmental risks by 86%. another negative effect of reduced use is that fewer pesticides are available, which leads to more rapid development of resistance. another point is the establishment of buffer zones around watercourses. the environment committee has approved an amendment to create a 10 metre buffer. for a country with a lot of water, like the netherlands, that's a problem. that means that 35% of the agricultural area cannot be irrigated. i would like to conclude by saying that not only is a norm important, but also that it is of undeniable importance to enforce it. at the moment, this is lacking, as evidenced by the exceedances of standards that we regularly encounter in products intended for consumption. even in the european parliament.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of the disease. the big question is always, if we in europe take certain measures, what impact does that have on our competitive relations with the rest of the world? if we are too strict in europe and cannot impose the same rules on the rest of the world, then that means that we can consume products in europe that we are not allowed to produce here and that seems to me to be a wrong situation. the essential thing is, of course, always to continue to evaluate the harmful effects. but we cannot exaggerate. and i agree with everyone who says that we should focus on risk and not on reducing quantity. the 10 metre buffer zones will also be disastrous for the netherlands and should be removed from the regulation. the last point, mr president, is to inform the neighbours. if something's approved, why do the neighbors have to be informed? that is a completely unnecessary regulation.
mr president, when i leave my village, i am in the middle of a unique agricultural area. the green heart between big cities like amsterdam, rotterdam and utrecht. it is still green and agricultural, but the 10 metre buffer zone proposed by the environment committee would make farming in this area virtually impossible. and it's agriculture that keeps that area green. i took the test on the sum last week, but i could barely find any land between the many locks that were 20 meters wide. so, anyone who knows the netherlands a little bit knows that the green heart is not alone in this. dutch agriculture is characterised by many locks between the meadows. this means that the 10-metre buffer zones for the netherlands amount to the loss of 800,000 hectares of agricultural land. that's 35% of our agricultural area. i would therefore urge my colleagues not to support this proposal, also in view of the agricultural production needed in europe and in the world, as already said by the chairman of the committee on agriculture. that does not mean, however, that i do not share the objective of protecting nature and public health. i say to the colleagues that we all share that ambition. i just think that ambition can be better achieved with a risk-based approach and risk reduction. the alternative - a reduction in consumption - may sound simple enough - a 20% reduction overall - but it does not offer the best protection for the consumer, because it only looks at quantities and not at the final effect, the final risk. in this context, i also find it difficult to understand the painful arguments of the liberal group - which opts for both a 20% overall reduction and a risky approach. my last two points, mr president. the authorisation system: i will simply say that three climate zones are preferable to 27 member states, taking account of national circumstances, of course. finally, i would ask the commission not to overlook small farms in all these major areas of disagreement.
president. - mr president, i have been able to see a great many representatives of the automobile industry over the last few months. i don't think there's ever been a report that's been lobbied so hard. the automobile industry has tried to prevent or at least delay the introduction of measures to reduce co2 emissions by all possible means. it seems that they have persuaded a large number of my colleagues, including the rapporteur, although fortunately the committee on the environment was aware of its role. let me be clear: the automobile industry has to thank itself for this commission proposal. attempts to reach a voluntary settlement have failed miserably. it turns out that only the setting of mandatory standards works. and to complain that it all has to be done so quickly, i think it is rather hypocritical, because the industry has known for years that these measures have to be taken. we must take drastic measures to protect the environment now. we owe it to ourselves and to future generations. i therefore call for a vote against amendment no 42, tabled by mr callanan and mr davies. the amendment aims to increase the maximum co2 emission limit and to move the agreed date from 2012 to 2015. this is putting commercial interests ahead of environmental interests and we cannot allow that to happen. mr. davies, what are you doing to us now?
mr president, road transport is a major source of pollution. in 2005, transport, cars and trucks, accounted for 27% of total greenhouse gas emissions. if we want to tackle climate change, we can't bypass the auto industry. without action, emissions will rise and not fall. unfortunately, the automobile industry has failed to take the initiative. years ago, the car manufacturers themselves made a covenant to reduce emissions. the industry has built better and safer cars in recent years. there was a lot of progress on this. but unfortunately, the efficiency of engines has been seriously neglected. the 140 grams target for 2008 will therefore not be met, and certainly not on a voluntary basis. unfortunately, to achieve efficiency, legislation is needed. and the legislation should aim to reach the average emission of 120 grams in 2012, not in 2015. a long-term target of 80 grams per kilometre is necessary because we have set ourselves an ambitious target for 2020 and it must also be reflected in the automotive industry.
rapporteur. – mr president, let me first make two preliminary remarks. one is that, obviously, this subject of statistics is not a very sexy subject and is always placed at the end of the agenda. but what concerns me a bit is that there are politicians, meps and others who often like to complain about the provision of statistics. but then, if there is an opportunity to really improve something, they are not really actively involved in making the situation better. the second remark is that i hope the commissioner, at least, will appreciate that we are having this debate. ms bowles and i have said that, for us, it is not really necessary to have the debate. but it is important to underline the fact that you have done something on statistics, and that issue deserves some attention. so i hope you will not blame us for keeping you here at this hour. let me, then, congratulate you on the very concrete measures that you have taken vis-à-vis these problems with statistics, which have appeared several times in recent history. i know that you had to do this in the political room for manoeuvre that is granted to you by the council, because we are aware that the european commission cannot oblige member states to establish independent statistical offices and prescribe to them how to work and how to deal with figures and data. ministers and meps who are the loudest and firmest in complaining about the lack of reliability of the figures of others are also the ones who do not want you to mix in their business and infringe on their autonomy and their way of dealing with statistics. this is the problem you have to deal with. but, in this context, i think it was wise of you, commissioner, to take a pragmatic, bottom-up approach, to focus on peer reviews and a code of practice, and to reform the governance structure of the statistics system by creating a new high level governance board, which ms bowles was speaking about, and by reforming the existing european advisory committee on statistical information in the economic and social spheres (ceies), which dealt with economic and social statistics, into a more streamlined and smaller advisory committee with a broader mandate. in ms bowles’ and my report we have tried to improve this even more, and to reinforce and strengthen the approach of the commission. at the same time, we have also made sure – in accordance with your proposal – that these two new bodies in the european family can function properly and with more independence from the commission and eurostat. this may not have been completely in line with the commission’s intentions; however, i want to express my appreciation to the director-general of eurostat for very generously accepting the fact that we took away his voting rights in the system that we designed. i also want to express my appreciation to the portuguese presidency, and to ms cordeiro, for the efficient way she chaired the council working group and negotiated with us a first reading agreement on this issue. this was a very efficient and very time- and bureaucracy-saving procedure. so, mr hans-peter martin, who has also asked for the floor, can see an example of how we can deal in a very efficient way with this type of co-decision procedure. let me very briefly give some of the elements of the statistical board, which i will do in writing. if you read the dossier, you can find where we made the changes, so i will not repeat this here but will stick to my wish that these two bodies will really bring some consciousness and some improvement in the way the statistics systems function, and will help us to really have reliable figures and base our economic activities on reliable data.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr barón crespo, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). the new treaty will allow the european union to move forward. the union is becoming more decisive and more democratic. the european union is back on its feet. the eu can now fully concentrate on its substantive task, once again with an agenda ranging from the further development of social europe to a better coordinated foreign policy. that is also the best way to bring europe closer to its citizens. in my country, the netherlands, there has been much discussion about the nature of the treaty now approved and how it differs from the constitutional treaty rejected in a referendum in our country. the conclusion was that the differences were such that a second referendum was not necessary. the new treaty does not have the constitutional character of the original constitution and so we do not need a second referendum. so it becomes a simple parliamentary ratification. the reforms now decided upon have our full support, because, without substantially altering the balance between the member states and the european institutions, great progress has been made, particularly in the area of the rights of the european parliament. a larger union requires different and broader rules. i hope that all the member states will now press ahead with ratification so that we can all start working on the new rules in 2009 to further complement the substantive role of the union in all areas where our citizens have expectations.
too many gifts were given away behind closed doors in lisbon to agree on the reform treaty. italy will have an extra seat in the european parliament, bulgaria will have the cyrillic 'euro', poland will continue to block the decision-making process for a number of years, austria may ban german students from its universities. this trade in cows is medieval and contrary to our desire for greater transparency in the union. but we can be satisfied that after years of debate there is at last an agreement tout court. because this treaty is better than the current situation. it is not a beauty prize, but it does bring democracy to europe. the european parliament and national parliaments will have more influence, giving european citizens more direct influence on policy. the need for a common energy policy and a common foreign policy is now being met. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting is a good one, but it is not enough to make the euro more attractive to consumers.
in writing. the european parliament has not today responded to the wish of environmental fundamentalists to establish unworkable rules for the sustainable use of pesticides. the environment committee's proposal to ban the use of pesticides within 10 metres of the waterfront was the main stumbling block. for a water-rich country like the netherlands, this would have disastrous consequences for managers of public space and recreational land such as golf courses. the result of the vote is a compromise between environmental protection and workable rules for governments and businesses. we have a shared responsibility to eliminate risks and, of course, the 'less is the better' approach to pesticide use, but the rules must be realistic and workable. if alternatives are available, they must be used, but it is wishful thinking to ban all use of pesticides by law and to think that this has no impact on our economy and society.

the commission's green paper and mr florenz's report are timely. it is high time that tobacco was brought up again in the european union, because it has been silent on this subject for too long. the florence report rightly focuses on smoking among young people and the harmful effects of excessive smoking at work, in public buildings and in the catering industry. for young people: research has shown that 90% of smokers start smoking as children, before the age of 18. i am very pleased with the ambitious targets in the florence report to reduce smoking in this age group substantially. after all, young people are susceptible to peer pressure, and the harmful effects on health are greater among them. i therefore hope that the member states - who of course think that they can do this themselves and that we should not interfere too much - will take up this house's call to commit themselves to halving the harmful effects of smoking among young people by 2025 and actually take action by agreeing among themselves that they will do so. as regards the smoking ban and the general ban on smoking: a good liberal principle says that one's personal freedom ends where another's freedom begins. and where is that principle more clearly illustrated than when it comes to unwanted merchants. smoking is a major public health problem and a cost to the european economy. more than 79 000 adults die each year in the member states of the european union as a result of secondhand smoke and there is evidence that secondhand smoke at work in the european union led to 7 000 deaths in 2002; exposure to secondhand smoke at home was responsible for another 72,000 deaths. there are, of course, also ample experiences in countries such as ireland, scotland and italy with this kind of smoking ban. and those experiences are actually all positive. what you hear from those countries - even from people who were very sceptical about this kind of measure beforehand - is ultimately positive. it is therefore tempting to introduce a smoking ban from europe as well. this house recently voted in the committee on the environment, public health and food safety not to opt for a european smoking ban at this time, but to leave those measures to the member states. i think that is the right choice, because such a controversial decision will have to be taken as close as possible to the citizen, and therefore by member states or by regions, for the time being. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european charter is fully implemented. what is extremely good - and i commend the commissioner for his green paper - is the fact that this debate has been stimulated. because that's what happened, undeniably. it is a subject on the agenda in all member states. finally, mr president, a few words on the product directive. the florence report also looks forward to its revision. i agree with everything you said about the warning labels on cigarette packs. it is tragic that so few member states have taken advantage of this and i would personally be in favour of legislation on this point. these places could also be used in other ways, for example on a leaflet with information on how to stop smoking, as is done in canada; let us leave out of this debate all hypocrisy, ladies and gentlemen. each of us knows, or should know, that there are no easy answers to this diabolical dilemma. on the one hand, there is the awareness, the knowledge that every country which has suffered 50 deaths in the last month must do something to respond, and at the same time there is the awareness among many of us - i think also among the turkish government - that large-scale military operations are not the solution. they do not eliminate the pkk, they cause great diplomatic and political damage and they make it - and this is the most important thing - much more difficult to find a solution to the kurdish problem in turkey. we must therefore hope that all the efforts now being made to find a diplomatic and political solution will be successful. because in the end, the problem is not in the iraqi mountains, the problem is in turkey. but in solving that problem - the kurdish problem - it is not: turks against kurds. i think that on the one hand there are those in turkey, the turks and the kurds, who know that there is only a political solution to this problem - the akp and the dtp - and on the other hand the radicals, on the turkish side and the kurdish side, who have no interest whatsoever in a political solution and who think that military force can help: the turkish side is the army and part of the opposition, and the kurdish side is the pkk. because let me be very clear: the current attacks by the pkk are of course aimed at the turkish state, but they are also an attack on the kurdish party dtp in parliament, which is seeking a political solution to the problem. that is why it is so important that this parliament, while strongly condemning the pkk and its terrorist attacks, should at the same time continue to express its support for all those on the kurdish side and on the turkish side who seek a peaceful political solution to this problem.
i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs oomen-ruijten, on her excellent report. i would like to reply to mr langen's comment: the main thing for my group is that the negotiations are aimed at membership of the european union! the resolution we are discussing today is an encouragement to the turkish government to continue along this path. prime minister erdoğan has received a strong mandate from the turkish electorate to do so. this is what enables him to give impetus to the reforms. we expect results to be coming quickly now and we expect the commission to come forward with a progress report in that regard soon. of course, urgent issues must not be overlooked. i think it would be a very important step symbolically, but also a very significant one, if the turkish government did something about article 301, if it withdrew it or changed its wording. that would create the necessary openness in turkey for the debate, including on issues of the past, and that brings me to the armenian question. we think it is very important that there should be an internal debate in turkey on this subject, but it is primarily up to turkey itself to organise it and not so much the european parliament or the us house of representatives to try to force it. it is primarily an internal matter, and we can help, but it is not very useful for us to insist on it from outside. the new constitution has been announced. we believe that this will also provide an opportunity to finally seek a political solution to the kurdish question. i share the view of those who say that we must do everything to prevent a military escalation in northern iraq, but we can only do that if we first of all renounce the pkk's terrorist attacks ourselves and demand that the pkk stop them. secondly, we must also support turkey in its dialogue with iraq and the iraqi authorities, but also in its dialogue with the regional authorities in kurdish iraq to cooperate in practice to stop these attacks. we welcome new diplomatic initiatives, but we are particularly concerned with practical cooperation in the region itself to stop and reduce violence.

in writing. i remember that in the early 1960s there were reciprocal visa requirements for citizens of the countries of the then european communities and of the socialist federal republic of yugoslavia. shortly thereafter, those visas were canceled. this meant that citizens of the seven countries that then made up yugoslavia could travel freely throughout much of europe. only for eu member slovenia and candidate country croatia has this not changed. the inhabitants of all other areas, including those of the applicant country macedonia, have been cut off from eu member states since 1992. the young generation that has grown up since then has hardly been able to leave their own country. for example, today you will find large signs with a large number of strict obligations in the embassies of eu countries in the macedonian capital skopje. only criminals can easily comply with it, but not students, researchers and journalists. i support the improved admission for these groups from 2008 and the reduction of the visa fee to € 35. unlike some members of my group, i do not see the introduction of biometric registration as a reason to reject this improvement. i regret the linking of the sale with the readmission policy, which does not sufficiently guarantee the safety of the refugees concerned.
in writing. the decision of the parliament's bureau to no longer report on plenary debates in each language went unnoticed in 2006. parliament has now reversed that decision. i agree that too much money and time is spent on translations of debates and documents into the 23 official languages of our union. it is a pity that there was no intermediate solution on the table, whereby the debates could be translated into english and french, so that, in addition to the audiovisual data, written documents would still be available.
in writing. the davis report deserves support because of the political message it sends. all sectors will have to do their part to tackle the climate problem, including the european automotive industry. that is why i wholeheartedly support the compromise to reach a maximum of 125g co2/km by 2015. for the netherlands, the davis report has an additional dimension which is undeniable. the problem of particulate matter in the netherlands requires effective source measures at european level. if this does not happen, it will be impossible for transport-intensive areas such as the port of rotterdam and schiphol airport to comply with the current and/or tightened fine dust standards. i am in favour of better information to consumers about the environmental performance of cars, as is now done with refrigerators and washing machines, for example, but i voted against the proposal to make 'cigarette-like' warnings mandatory in all advertising and marketing materials for the automotive industry. in the field of advertising and marketing, i see more value in self-regulation than in all sorts of european legal obligations.
president. - as in any country which has lost territory and influence in recent wars, there is a strong sense of nationalist superiority in serbia. instead of isolating or punishing serbia for the disappearance of war criminal ratko mladic, a future perspective must be offered, including guarantees for the ethnic minorities in vojvodina, sandjak and presevo. my group supports the kacin report's approach to rapprochement, with the prospect of serbian membership of the european union. unfortunately, the paragraph 8 added by the afet committee is ahead of the decision on kosovo. it is likely that after 10 december, kosovo will be unilaterally recognised as a state by the united states and some member states of the european union, and then serbia will also unilaterally take back the northern part of kosovo. we therefore share the conviction that the future status of kosovo can only contribute to stability and integration. in maintaining this unfounded optimism in paragraph 8, my group withdraws its support for this report.
president, members of the house, we are trying today to highlight the positive developments in serbia. and let there be no misunderstanding about it, there's nothing wrong with that. it is important for the european union that serbia develops in a democratic direction and it is important that we, the european union, recognise and, where possible, support that process. it is the same effort that is being made in other eu institutions, particularly in the council. but, colleagues, we must not exaggerate. even if serbia does everything that is called for in this report, but at the same time it plays a destructive and negative role in the negotiations on the future of kosovo, that will undoubtedly have negative consequences for serbia's pace towards the european union. and that, ladies and gentlemen, is right. that is why i find it incomprehensible and wrong that the link - president. - mr kacin's solid and comprehensive report gives a balanced account of the socioeconomic development of serbia. light and shadow side coming out: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. foreign direct investment is essential for serbian development and it is precisely this that is needed at the present time. the causes are obvious. they are mainly due to problems with the image of belgrade. what primarily deterred foreign investors was the continuing political uncertainty - kosovo as the code word - as well as the markedly diminished momentum for market-economy reform on the part of belgrade. which brings me to a crucial question. serbia is in the way of itself? the kacin report opens with the statement that serbia's future lies in the european union; the european conditions on this road are well known to belgrade. so too does european cooperation in this field. finally, the serbian authorities themselves must answer the pressing question: serbia is getting in the way of itself?
the report on the state of relations between the european union and africa is now before us. it is a joint vision of africa and europe for future cooperation to promote development in africa and to combat poverty. the strategy must be more than just an endorsement of current policies. it is a vision based on shared values and principles, on mutual respect, and on the well-being of people. africa and europe have a long history together. however, the proportions have changed: it can't be one-way traffic anymore. it is now about an equal partnership to tackle together issues affecting both continents, such as security, trade, migration and climate change, for example. in 2005, the european union wrote a european strategy for africa. i was a reporter at the time. in our view, this strategy lacked two important elements. it was a strategy too much for africa, but without africa, and the european parliament and civil society were too little involved in the creation of this strategy. i am glad that we are now talking about a joint eu-africa strategy and that the involvement of the european parliament and civil society has increased. the cooperation is promising for the future. the strategy must give us the structure and the direction for joint action in the future. the fight against poverty and the millennium development goals must remain at the heart of this. and while the latest mod figures allow for some optimism, in sub-saharan africa 41.1% of people continue to live on $1 a day. this situation cannot be improved by development aid alone. economic growth must also be promoted. the economic partnership agreements currently being negotiated can be a good instrument for this, provided that - and i emphasize this - sustainable development is at the heart of the matter and provided that it is more than a european trade agreement. i would like to hear from the commissioner on the state of play with regard to a possible extension of the deadline of 1 january 2008. mr president, the african governments are, of course, first and foremost responsible for the development of their own countries. they have become more independent politically and economically. africa is developing rapidly, not least because of the emergence of new institutions, such as nepad and the african peer review mechanism. europe is no longer the only and exclusive partner for financial and political support. other countries are beginning to exert their influence in and on africa very strongly - think of china, for example. we can no longer take our relationship with africa for granted. the report emphasizes three priority policy areas: peace and security, good governance, economic growth and investing in people. the report stresses the importance of greater coherence between development activities and other policy areas such as trade, agriculture and migration. only if europe can use its aid more coherently, coordinate it better and have better financial accountability will the european union's development policy be better and more efficient. peace and security is a big problem in africa. the report stresses the importance of an integrated approach to conflict situations. this must be centred on our responsibility to protect people and to contribute to conflict prevention and resolution, as well as to reconstruction. it goes without saying that good governance, a functioning rule of law and sustainable democracy are prerequisites for stability and development. capacity building in these areas is crucial. we support the commission's ambitions in this respect. president, in december the second eu-africa summit will take place in lisbon after seven years. the joint eu-africa strategy and action plan will be adopted there. there is a lot at stake, and it is in all our interests that this summit be a success. and while the situation in zimbabwe is extremely worrying, we must remember that this is an eu-africa summit and not an eu-zimbabwe summit; it's about a people-centred approach and not a president-centred partnership. it is good that the european parliament and the pan-african parliament have the opportunity at the summit to express the parliamentary view of the joint strategy. last week the delegations of the pan-african parliament and of our parliament met to prepare a joint statement. i hope our presidents can present these to the heads of government at the summit. mr president, there is of course more to be said, but i see my time is up, and i shall therefore leave it at that, but not without thanking my colleagues and the european commission for the good cooperation.
the relationship between the european union and the african countries is under unacceptably high pressure as a result of the negotiations with the acp countries on the economic partnership agreements. if these negotiations fail, cotonou will be a black hole for some countries from 1 january, because our trade relations will be much less favourable than the general system of preferences. but it would be a shame if the poor countries were punished in this way, because they feel that the eu and the acp countries are not equal partners at the moment. give poor countries the right to market protection of sectors that are weak and would collapse in the storm of open trade. these negotiations are in stark contrast to the good principles and objectives of the maria martens report. i share those goals. we as socialists would like to add that the eu's trade and agricultural policies must not stand in the way of development policy. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is up to the european commission to make it a concrete policy. the commission will be closely monitoring this.

the european union has a duty to help the people of iran, and to help the people of iran. first, the terrible human rights situation, created by a regime that does not believe in democracy and the equality of people, but in their own interpretation of the will of god, which gives them the right to imprison, torture and kill other people. secondly, the regime's attempt to gain mass support despite its role as a violent minority. it's an artificial attempt to create a sense of national pride. the reason for this pride is the possession of nuclear energy and possibly even nuclear weapons, like india, pakistan and israel, which have not signed the non-proliferation treaty. thirdly, it is the opposition to this regime. the moderate opposition, which wants a somewhat less strict regime - as was the case under the previous president - and the genuine democratic opposition, which lives at home among workers and students and also among the intellectuals who have fled abroad. and fourthly, it is the american threat to apply the same model to iran as in iraq and afghanistan, by means of a military invasion or at least by bombing. such an attack by foreign adversaries is counterproductive. it would encourage the people of iran to rally behind the hated regime and its nuclear weapons, if any, out of patriotism. my group rejects both a military invasion of this regime and the pursuit of friendship with this regime for its own sake. our solidarity is with the victims and the opposition. the regime must not in any way be given the impression that it can negotiate restrictions on the rights of the opposition in exile in europe, and it must not be allowed to propagandize that democratic countries support its misrule. the european court of justice's ruling to remove the people's mojahedin organisation from the terrorist list should be fully respected so that this organisation or other opposition groups are not subject to legal or financial obstacles.
mr president, the situation of the roma in europe is getting worse. the developments in italy prove that to us. we have all seen that unchallenged xenophobic and racist hysteria can overcome common sense. the crime of one single person has fuelled an unprecedented media lynching. this moved us towards restrictions on the fundamental rights of european citizens based on popular racist and xenophobic hysteria. we must not let that happen. criminal issues are for the judges to deal with, not for politicians or civilians. however horrible and condemnable the crime that took place in rome is, it should not lead us to collective responsibility of a whole nation. the events in italy did uncover the reality of the campo nomadi, in which so many roma live. the fact that tensions and instability arise from such situations is not questionable. that is why we must act. my first assistant is a roma. integration can proceed quickly. in 2005 we adopted a resolution that asked for concerted eu and national action for speeding up the inclusion of roma. therefore, i propose that we, as meps, show that we care, and urge decision-makers to join us. i propose that the ep establish a permanent european parliament rapporteur on roma issues, who would monitor the situation of the roma in europe and come annually with recommendations to the commission and governments. in addition, we should call upon the commission to establish an eu roma inclusion fund, together with an eu strategy for roma inclusion and a roma unit within the european commission. this will ensure proper management of the eu roma inclusion policies.

madam president, on behalf of my colleague mr blokland, i would like to say the following. the debate has again shown that the proposed soil policy is highly controversial. the commission has already proposed a strategy for the protection of the environment, and the council has already proposed a directive on the protection of the environment. at least it seems that way, because nothing is less true. sustainable soil management is already mentioned in 33 european directives, for example in the water framework directive. why then do we come up with overlapping legislation which also imposes additional burdens, especially for member states where there is already a well-functioning soil policy? i shall support the thematic strategy of mr prodi. i believe that member states which do not have a soil policy at present should develop one on the basis of the strategy. the framework directive, on the other hand, is disproportionate and, in my view, also contrary to the principle of subsidiarity. unlike air and water, soil is not transboundary and policies are often implemented at regional or local level. that is why i - and therefore mr blokland - co-signed the amendment rejecting the commission's proposal.
president, commissioner, i am in favour of the soil strategy outlined in the prodi report, but i am against the soil directive. this is clearly based on the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. i therefore tabled an amendment to reject it, but at the same time i tabled another to opt for another instrument, namely open coordination. the objectives can be fully achieved. it is obvious that it is a question of stimulation, knowledge exchange and monitoring. everything is possible, including financial support from the union. there is still the danger of duplication of work: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of water. the citizens of my country, the netherlands, do not understand why unnecessary legislation and regulation should be established from above, from the european level. as i said, there are already more than 30 directives dealing directly or indirectly with soil quality. why central regulation when it can be decentralised? i do appreciate mrs gutiérrez-cortines' wish to make the scheme more subsidiary. finally, with 40 signatures, i have tabled a large number of proposals, amendments to put even more emphasis on soil quality and protection and to respond to new challenges such as climate change, but there must be no legislation at european level. i call on my colleagues to follow my amendments on this.
president. - thank you to mrs gutiérrez-cortines, but i must say that we regrettably disagree with her. in many places in europe the soil is polluted or there is erosion or other soil problems. these problems must be resolved as soon as possible. i think we all agree on this. the commission proposes to solve soil problems at european level and that, in my view, is the problem. in fact, soil problems are often local and only in specific cases have a genuine cross-border impact. european cooperation is desirable for soil problems with cross-border implications. solidarity and cooperation are important. however, there is no need for a european policy on local and national soil problems. many member states already have good national policies to solve and prevent their soil problems. their policies provide a level of protection at least as high as that currently available. the soil directive should take account of these member states, they should be exempted from european obligations and therefore we propose to vote against this directive.
president. - i should like to begin by congratulating mr liese. he delivered a wonderful report. i also want to pay tribute to our shadow reporter, matthias groote. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the union's climate change policy and will continue to monitor the implementation of the union's climate change policy. we can't meet the climate target of minus 20 or minus 30 percent by 2020 without everybody putting money in their pockets. the airline industry in particular does not want to be taken too hard, but we hear that from all sectors and if we appease everyone, climate policy will not be any good. it is therefore good that the commission wants to include aviation in the ets, but the commission has been a little too generous with the number of emission allowances it wants to allocate to the sector. i think 80% of the emissions really should be the upper limit. the environment committee rightly wants this ceiling to be gradually lowered in line with the european climate objective of minus 20 or minus 30%. aircraft operators have pledged to increase the efficiency of aircraft engines by 50%. the environment committee wants to keep them on this, and that is good, because the more economical, the cleaner. a third important point is the auctioning of rights. as far as i'm concerned, we're auctioning off as much as we can. twenty-five percent is really the minimum. the proceeds of the auction should go towards fighting climate change, but also, and above all, adaptation to climate change in developing countries. the least developed countries contribute little to climate change, but they do bear the brunt of its effects. we can do something about that with this legislation.
mr president, on behalf of my colleague mr blokland, i would like to make the following contribution. the proposal to integrate aviation into the emissions trading system should be a clear signal from the european union to contribute to the ambitious reduction targets agreed earlier this year. in this context, the committee responsible in parliament has expressed its support for the commission's proposal and even called for it to be tightened up. in the past week, there seemed to be some interest in an agreement at first reading. this expectation was reinforced by the presidency of the council. it was a false expectation, because no mandate could be obtained from the council. this week, i think it is important that we do not settle for a watered-down version of the commission proposal, but that we aim for a proposal with clear ambitions, to be consistent with the line taken this spring on emission reductions. i therefore wholeheartedly support the line taken by the committee on the environment.
mr president, instead of all sorts of ineffective taxes and charges, the commission proposes the present market-based instrument. a choice i wholeheartedly endorse. it is environmentally effective, economically efficient and well suited to wider applications. at least, that's how it could all be. it was clear fairly quickly that there was a great deal of division in this house. the environment committee was facing the transport committee. my colleague, mr holger krahmer, has said all that is necessary on this subject. the compromise amendments have been drawn up and tomorrow's vote will determine whether emotion is the key. the climate is hot and green voting is extremely sexy. there is nothing wrong with that in itself, but i do not think that it is the intention to strangle the european aviation industry and the jobs it creates. the attempt to artificially reduce one specific mode of transport, in this case aviation, in favour of another, is counterproductive in my view. all indications are that the demand for transport is increasing at a faster rate than the capacity available. we need all the modalities, and we can't do it with trains and buses alone. the aim was to develop a model as workable as possible that could be extended and/or copied worldwide. and yes, if we really want to be environmentally efficient, then the latter is crucial. if not, we are stuck with that one tiny drop on the familiar glowing plate, and that is what i would like to avoid. as mr rack has just said, if we are to be effective, we must persuade the rest of the world, because only then will our action be effective.

in writing. communicable diseases threatening europe, such as sars and avian influenza, demonstrate that far-reaching european action is needed to halt a possible pandemic in europe. unilateral action by member states is not sufficient to address the risk of such diseases. a european approach and coordination are needed, with knowledge of the level of protection in the member states being essential. it is therefore essential that comparable and up-to-date data exist at community level in the field of protection against potential pandemics and communicable diseases. the main considerations are the stocks of antiviral medicines and vaccines in the member states. i would have preferred that there were already stocks of antiviral drugs and vaccines at european level. without new powers, the commission will be powerless to react to a possible pandemic.
on behalf of the pse group. mr president, on behalf of my group i too would like to congratulate the people of the nine new member states on the portuguese christmas present they will receive on 21 and 22 december. i fully agree with the secretary of state, the presidency, that this is indeed the final removal of the last vestiges of the iron curtain that we have been so concerned about. the free movement of citizens without internal controls is a great asset and one of the most important freedoms of the european union and it is very good that the inhabitants of those new member states can start to use it. the schengen convention, however, is more than just the abolition of internal borders. it also means that the external borders of the union will henceforth be guarded by these new countries, in the interests of all the other member states. it is important that the schengen enlargement does not compromise security. that is what the commissioner and the secretary of state have said. efficient and effective border surveillance is essential to this end. the council and commission's evaluations show that the nine countries are in principle ready to take on this task, but there are still a number of areas where improvements can be made, for example in the area of airport staff and infrastructure, and we are very much looking forward to the evaluations and monitoring that will be published on this and we also hope that parliament will be informed in due time. schengen is not just about border controls, of course. it also concerns the intensification of police and judicial cooperation between the schengen countries. we are pleased that the portuguese presidency has found a solution in the case of these nine member states with a kind of sis 1+, but we do insist that progress is now being made as quickly as possible with the sis 2 system. i welcome the commissioner's commitment that this will be done by december 2008 and we will of course keep him to that. we understand that bulgaria and romania will have to wait a few years, but we also hope that they will work so hard, with our help, that they too will be able to join the schengen area within a few years. the same is true of cyprus. it is a pity that the problem which has been a mortgage for so long is now leading to the country's being unable to join the schengen area. this is yet another argument for working together to find a solution to the problem that has been plaguing the island for so long. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. of course, a solution is also needed for the people of cyprus themselves. finally, i would like to refer again to yesterday's discussion. it is rightly pointed out that schengen enlargement and the whole schengen system is a matter of mutual solidarity between the member states. as i have said before, the task of guarding the external borders is a task which concerns the security of all of us and which will now also involve the nine countries concerned and which we will also address to them. this solidarity and the need for cooperation also apply when problems arise in the future, for example in the case of internal migration flows. we must also agree that we will not try to solve problems unilaterally, but will also seek forms of cooperation with all the countries belonging to the schengen area, to seek together solutions to problems which will arise in the future and not, as i said, as is happening in italy now, where one country is trying unilaterally to solve something which is actually a common problem.

in writing. soil protection is urgently needed to prevent the destruction of natural soil life, erosion, poisoning and desiccation. without these measures, plant growth in densely populated areas with intensive land use will be impossible. in many parts of europe i see neglected land, which has lost all use to nature and human use. yesterday i voted with the majority of this house in favour of the prodi report, which expresses the need for active measures to protect the soil. today we are discussing what such soil protection measures should look like. i support a system which encourages member states which have not yet taken the necessary initiatives on their own to take the necessary steps now. their failure to do so has negative consequences not only for themselves but also for neighbouring countries, for example by increasing erosion debris causing river silt deposits and flooding. on the other hand, eu rules should in no way lead to member states which already regulate this matter properly being faced with the obligation to increase bureaucracy or obstacles to their good approach. i support amendments which allow those who do the same or better to continue. on the other hand, it is irresponsible to reject, delay or limit the soil protection package as proposed by the largest group in this parliament.
