- a writer. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, if we are really concerned about fundamental rights, we are concerned about the precarious position of christian communities in the middle east. this resolution calls on all the european institutions to do so. a recent visit by a delegation from our parliament gave us a better insight into the daily lives of lebanese christians. they fear being relegated to a sort of second-class position, like their fellow believers in virtually all the countries of the region. they are directly forced to choose between personal safety and personal dignity, religious belief. the lebanese christians of today want to preserve both. meanwhile, the actual political violence of recent years is affecting christians in the cedar country very directly. in the words of a spokesman in lebanon just this week: "although not primarily aimed at christians, the majority of politicians killed are from christian homes and the same is true of the journalists targeted. this is intimidating the christian population in lebanon".
the christian communities in the middle east date back to the early years of christianity. they are older than christianity in europe and older than islam in the middle east. nevertheless, they are often seen as a foreign element in this now predominantly muslim environment. this is not just a result of religious intolerance within parts of islam. europe is also to blame. three times in history, europe and christianity in that area have evoked revulsion and hatred, first by the crusades in the late middle ages, when occupying armies brought places sacred not only to christians but also to jews and muslims under european control. the second time followed the collapse of the ottoman empire at the beginning of the last century, when egypt, sudan, jordan and iraq came under british colonial control and syria and lebanon came under french colonial control. we are in phase three at the moment. the positions taken from europe on israel, palestine and iraq are provoking great resistance in the middle east. europe is suspected of being concerned mainly with its own energy supply, with securing its own transport routes and with favouring ethnic or religious minorities who are most sympathetic to europe. the result may be that the christian minorities in the middle east cannot sustain themselves and are doomed to flee to europe. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the process of european integration.
the president of the republic of uzbekistan, mr. zhao, has been very kind to the people of uzbekistan. the attempt was thwarted on 13 may 2005 by violent attacks on demonstrators, which left hundreds dead. since then, the outside world has been remarkably quiet. after our previous urgent debate on 27 october 2005, little has happened initially. however, after the collapse of the soviet union, authoritarian regimes have come to power not only in better known states such as ukraine, georgia and belarus, but also in turkmenistan and uzbekistan in particular. it was not democracy that had won there, but a group of people who had gained experience in the old state apparatus and the security services. these people had no other goal than to remain in power themselves. they privatized state-owned enterprises, distorted election results, restricted opposition to the extent possible, hindered free organizations, restricted the press, and used violence against their people when necessary. unfortunately, this situation is still continuing in uzbekistan. until now, europe's attitude towards uzbekistan has been too much determined by economic and military interests. it seemed that the dictatorship in uzbekistan was allowed to continue because it had made itself useful for the military intervention in afghanistan. such an attitude would make europe's pretensions to human rights and democracy utterly unbelievable. europe must not subordinate the rights and freedoms of the uzbek people to other considerations.
author. mr president, since 1991, the state of somalia has effectively ceased to exist, at least as far as the territory of the former italian colony is concerned. only in the north, the former british colony of somaliland, is there a form of state authority, but that authority is not internationally recognized. the state has been replaced by a number of warlords who alternate between fighting and cooperating. the army of neighboring ethiopia has emerged as their joint protector. on the other hand, their common opponent is the union of islamic courts, which wants to reunite somalia on the basis of traditional islamic rules and without those warlords. all attempts to date to establish a generally accepted unity government and to restore somalia to statehood have failed. the commission has provided financial support to the federal transitional institutions set up in 2004. this seemed justifiable at the time, but has led to the accusation that the european union is taking sides in an armed conflict and is working with ethiopia against the forces that would prefer a strict islamic regime in power. such a european policy, however well-intentioned, cannot succeed without the support of the people of somalia. it is therefore right that the resolution which will be put to the vote calls for an end to foreign military interventions and for dialogue and reconciliation at home.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women's rights and gender equality. mr president, the women's committee was almost overlooked in today's debate too. luckily, she'll be on the verge of a fall. more than half the population in europe is made up of women and these women are already over-represented on the labour market in terms of temporary and part-time contracts. so women are already facing greater insecurity, poor pension provision and inadequate health care. if, as the commission and the dutch government want, the right to dismiss is relaxed, this group will only sink further into oppression and deprivation of rights. i was therefore very pleased that the committee on women's rights unanimously came up with a series of proposals which could improve the commission's proposal on this point. unfortunately, the rapporteur has chosen to adopt only a few of these widely-held proposals. in doing so, he is mocking the commission on women's rights and ignoring the real inequalities that exist. i therefore call on all members to support the amendments tabled to avoid this disparity when we vote tomorrow.
the epas have become a very controversial subject in africa and increasingly in europe. it touches on fundamental differences in understanding of the possibility of combating poverty through sustainable economic growth in acp countries. clearly, financial aid alone has not really helped to reduce poverty. we believe that these trade agreements can provide a window where we can look past years of ineffective aid. the acp countries' share of world trade has been reduced. it is now less than 1%, and the millennium development goals are not being met in africa. it's got to be different. europe has a moral duty to help the acp countries to grow economically and to increase their share of trade. the epas should contribute to this. the benefits of trade and economic integration are clear, mr president, especially in a globalising world. competitiveness, a good investment climate, market access, manufacturing industries are essential for economic growth in the acp countries. we must be flexible and pragmatic, but we must remain within the framework of the wto. the signing of the full trade agreements is no longer expected before the deadline of 1 january 2008. however, some african countries in east and southern africa have reached interim agreements. they're goods only agreements. but they must not be a step backwards for regional development. we must start quickly with the technical support for the strengthening of those countries and then eventually come to full agreements, including for example services.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the european railway agency can play a useful role in the further development and application of the new ertms safety system and in reducing the dependence on manufacturers. but in other areas the need for the agency is mainly caused by increasing scale, liberalization and competition on the railway. these developments require more and more bureaucracy to ensure that everything is properly coordinated. long before the european union was created, this coordination took place in a very different way. good agreements between national railway companies which jointly organised very long distance train services, together with the compagnie internationale des wagons lits. i doubt the new model is an improvement. in principle, rolling stock approved in one member state should henceforth be automatically authorised in other member states. this situation does not yet exist even at the small-scale level of the urban electricity companies; because the sharpness of curves, the location of stops and the space between the two tracks sometimes differ, some types of trams cannot travel all routes. i expect that the railways will also make frequent use of the safety exceptions. so in practice, little will change.
i would like to begin by thanking the court of auditors for the new system of reporting to the european parliament. for years we have asked whether it was not possible to quantify the errors in the various chapters a little more. for years the court of auditors has said that this is not possible and fortunately, for the first time it is now clear that the court of auditors can also change its mind; it now produces a system of reporting with red, yellow and green. we welcome that very much and we hope that in the coming years that system will be further refined so that we can compare what the improvements are. the commission has a duty to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. because three years ago, only 6% of the expenditure was recognized as legitimate. the commission has already made a number of proposals. that's a step forward. the big question is, is this progress sufficient in the eyes of public opinion, yes or no? well, i can assure you, the answer is no. it is not possible to explain why, after so many years, this reliability report is still negative, and that is a matter of great concern. the structural funds are a bigger problem. of course, 12% is far too high. it would be good if the court of auditors could state in a future report what exactly is the situation with regard to the 12%. is it because of the wrong filling of forms, are they real mistakes? when i hear in the field how difficult it is to get access to the structural funds, it is quite incomprehensible that this control is not sufficient, because i hear more and more, leave my share to someone else, because it is far too complicated to participate in these funds. i hear that in some provinces in the netherlands. the commission's discharge procedure is now under way and i believe that the following is the key point: what action has the commission taken on the previous recommendations in parliament's discharge resolutions? for does not the treaty say that the commission must do its utmost to implement the discharge resolutions as far as possible? i think there are two things that need to be clarified - not only in a resolution but also in the treaty - with regard to the multiannual budget, namely the declarations by the member states - other people have already pointed this out. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it means that parliament does not have to take responsibility for the commission as a whole, but that each commissioner is responsible for his own budget. i'd like to hear more about that. finally, the new treaty says that the commission and the member states are jointly responsible. in the light of these statements by the member states, it would be interesting to see what preparations the commission is making to implement this new convention now and in the future.
in writing. in states where different peoples live together, there are often no parties operating throughout the country. in the former austria-hungary there were various social democratic, catholic and liberal parties, each for the individual peoples, bound to their own group interests and their own language. in belgium too, national parties have been replaced by flemish and walloon parties for some decades. why should there be european parties in the eu? our party, the socialist party in the netherlands, sees parties as organizations from below, as movements of citizens who fight together for their vision of the organization of society and for the interests of the groups that give a party their confidence. parties are not part of the state apparatus and should not be created with taxpayers' money. on this basis we have rejected previous decisions on the financing of european parties and we are also against increasing that financing to 85%. we also think it unrealistic to promote european campaigns instead of national campaigns in the next european elections. that's 27 national elections under different circumstances. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we are in favour of international cooperation between peers, but this does not require eu funding.
in writing. 'the peoples of europe, by forging an ever closer union among themselves, have decided to share a peaceful future based on common values.' for example, eu leaders signed the first sentence of the preamble to the charter of fundamental rights of the european union in nice on 7 december 2000. and that is the core truth of this sentence. in our union we have common values and standards, which are now enshrined in the charter as rights: the european union is a party to the treaty of rome and to the treaty of rome. i think it would have been better if the eu had simply joined the european convention on human rights of the council of europe. but in the end, this is an acceptable solution. it is finally an explicit recognition of fundamental rights in the eu.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environment is protected. the air has become cleaner over the last 30 years, but there is still far too much air pollution. the current guidelines are not sufficiently respected. to better tackle pollution, a revision of the air quality directive is needed. last week parliament and the council reached agreement on the new directive. i am satisfied with the outcome and i thank holger krahmer, anja weisgerber and the other shadow rapporteurs for the constructive cooperation they have shown over the past two years. i would like to emphasize three points. first, the more stringent standards. my group has consistently advocated the setting of more ambitious standards for particulate matter and other pollutants harmful to man and the environment. i am therefore pleased that we have achieved a new standard for the smallest particles. that's pollution that you can't see or smell, but it's particles that cause the most health damage, causing hundreds of thousands of europeans to die prematurely. there are large numbers of people with asthma and other ailments. the new standard will allow us to tackle this health damage in a much more targeted way. so this agreement is good news for the health of the citizens. parliament has not acquiesced to the lazy ambition of the commission and the council. we asked for a bigger bet and we got it. the pm2.5 standard was lowered from 25 micrograms to 20 micrograms. this, according to scientists, will increase the average life expectancy by another 4-5 months. but even with the new standards, we're not there yet. further improvements are needed and more attention to children is also needed. and finally, we have to come up with a standard of no more than 10 micrograms of particulate matter per cubic meter of air. that's the upper limit recommended by the world health organization, and that's the guarantee of truly clean air. the directive now before us is a good step in the right direction. the second point is that europe must not only set firm standards but also develop instruments to achieve these goals. because europe is often good at setting general policy objectives, but much less good at creating the right instruments to actually achieve them. without cleaner cars, ships, agriculture and industry, air quality standards will remain a paper tiger. we have just voted on euro 5 and euro 6: cleaner cars from 2009 and even cleaner cars from 2014. this kind of legislation should be introduced soon for trucks and buses. parliament has urged the council and the commission to adopt rapidly complementary policies to tackle the sources of pollution. this has led to a commission statement listing all the measures to be taken in the coming years. the declaration will be annexed to the directive, with a reference to the directive. it is a clear political signal to the people that europe is taking cleanup action in all kinds of sectors. a third point of discussion was the flexibility of the standards. the standards of the existing directive cannot be met by 24 member states. i don't think that's a good example of good legislation. my position has always been that we should give member states who are taking all possible measures but are still not able to meet the standards some extra time to tackle the hotspots, provided that we get much stricter standards in the medium term. it is very important that the legislation we produce here is also workable and enforceable. today we are showing that the european parliament takes seriously the complaints from the member states about impossible legislation. experience shows that the air will not be cleaner if we do not take care to implement the directive. the new air quality directive is an improvement on the old directive. better and stricter standards are good for public health, for local authorities and for construction companies. we can breathe again!
on behalf of the ind/dem group. president, on behalf of my colleague mr blokland, who is currently attending the bali climate conference, the following contribution: after long negotiations, an agreement on air quality is finally reached. that's quite an accomplishment. it is also very important because air quality has a direct impact on human health and the environment. i would therefore like to thank the rapporteur, mr krahmer, for all the work he has done to achieve this. this directive makes a real contribution to improving the environment and health. air quality has already improved significantly in recent years and will continue to improve as a result of this directive. however, a number of countries still find it difficult to meet strict standards at an early stage. i am therefore pleased that the agreement allows member states to have a delay in bringing their air quality up to standards if they make sufficient efforts. it is also important that measurements are not required in places where the public has no access and which are not permanently inhabited. this flexibility is particularly important for port areas. i am also very pleased that reference has been made specifically to source measures. in my view, source measures such as the new euro 6 standard for lorries are absolutely necessary to achieve good air quality. in conclusion, i am therefore in favour of the agreement in question and will support it in the vote.

mr president, sometimes it is a pity that we do not have an open debate, then i could have responded to geoffrey van orden, but i will not. the global campaign against landmines may be a great success. thousands of square miles of land are now cleared of land mines thanks to the work of people who take great risks in bosnia, in angola and in many other countries. but ten years after the ottawa convention there is still no reason to rejoice. it is true that 156 countries have signed the convention against landmines, and that trade has declined - has declined drastically - but there is still investment in the production of landmines, including by financial institutions in europe. dutch and other banks and pension funds have also been guilty of this. it is good to point out to these institutions the consequences of their investments in landmines. because since 1975 one million people have been killed by land mines. every year, tens of thousands of people fall victim to landmines. during this debate, at least three people are killed, 70 a day. 200,000 square kilometers of land have been made inaccessible by land mines, and there are still 250 million land mines in circulation. the governments of burma and russia are still laying mines at the moment and that, mr van orden, is something other than "terrorist clubs", although... the government of russia... there are still 13 countries in the world that are producing them or reserve the right to do so in the future. in the us, it's companies like alliant techsystems and textron that continue to produce mines unhindered, on orders from the us government. president. - mrs kuneva, belgium prohibits investment in landmines, but there are companies operating in the rest of europe. a colleague pointed to rheinmetall and thales. that must stop and that is exactly what our resolution is asking for, asking for again, because in july 2005 the same demand was already made by the european parliament. the eu member states must agree collectively that no european company or financial institution will in future invest in companies that develop and produce landmines. the same is true of cluster bombs and anti-vehicle bombs, which explode as soon as someone gets close. the arms industry is not impressed by ethical arguments. the threat of a shutdown could help. let the eu then take the initiative and make an exception to the rule that politics does not interfere with the investment policy of business. for matters prohibited by international treaties, political intervention is a moral and political obligation. and that should be incorporated in european and national legislation. for a world without land mines and without anti-vehicle mines, as mr beer rightly said. we'd like to hear your opinion on this, mrs. kuneva. the european commission must finally show initiative in this area. and finally, do more to clean up the mines. the money is there, the know-how is there, but the european commitment is lagging behind.
- i'm the reporter. mr president, after the commissioner's introduction, there is not really much need for debate, but i would like to explain the position of the committee on agriculture. first of all, cross compliance. i do not know how it is in other languages, but in dutch it has become a dutch word. it's common knowledge among farmers. we do support it - as the commissioner has just said - it is an essential part of agricultural production and we think it should be defended because it makes demands on agricultural production that go beyond what is necessary to ensure that the products of agriculture are fit for human consumption. we are continuing with all kinds of directives, bird and habitat directives, environmental directives, animal welfare standards, etc. the big question in all of this is: how should the control be carried out? as regards those checks, i think that the need for a broad support and understanding of the cross compliance system among farmers must also be present in the context of increasing reforms. and that is not encouraged if the methods are too bureaucratic, as they are at present. what could we change about that? firstly, there are too many control bodies in each member state at the moment and, secondly, they are coming on different days. we are clear in this report that the number of inspection bodies should be kept to a minimum, that inspections should be carried out on a single day and that the farmer should be informed of them in good time. how much that should be, that will be decided by the vote today. we also believe that the authorities should not be too serious about de minimis, for minor offences, for minor offences. we cannot put salt on all the snails and, moreover, there should never be double punishment for something which is covered by european and national legislation. that would be a double fine, and we are against that. a major point of the report is - and i think the commission is also in favour of this - that a lot of things that are controlled by the government under cross compliance are also controlled by private industries. why does it have to be both? why is it that when private industry controls something, the government would say "well, that's fine, we control that private industry from time to time and if that's good, then we don't have to control the farm itself anymore". that can mean a lot, even from the farmer's point of view. so, a very good collaboration, i think, with private industry. the commission and the council have now agreed to abolish the ten-month rule. i think that the deadline for submitting applications in the member states is a lead date for the aid application. we in the committee on agriculture think it is reasonable that the new member states should also be given extra time to adapt and we have argued that if they eventually receive 100% agricultural subsidies, they must also comply with the rules of cross compliance 100%. we don't know what the future of the system will be, but we do know that it is incredibly expensive at the moment. would it be possible for the commission to make a rough cost estimate of what it costs in each member state to implement it, how much it is in comparison from one member state to another and how that compares with the total costs associated with cross compliance? and so there are all sorts of options in that study of how it could be cheaper by working with the private industries. at the moment, it is roughly estimated that there are some eighteen directives which a farmer must comply with in order not to be deprived of income supplements. maybe there'll be more. there is a soil directive in preparation, etc. the commission must examine whether it is not possible to reduce this or that. and then i have one or two guidelines in mind. it is not possible for us to have both a nitrate directive and a groundwater directive. the groundwater directive says we must have 50 mg of nitrate per litre of groundwater (if you go above that, you are punishable), the nitrate directive says 1.7 livestock units per hectare. it's one or the other, but not both. that's too much of a good thing. one of the two must go, and i would say the nitrate directive.
in writing. the vvd euro group voted today in favour of the agreement on air quality. the agreement sets strict standards for fine dust. in 2015, the air may contain up to 25 micrograms of pm2,5. in addition, it was agreed that certain areas with high concentrations could be granted deferral if they could demonstrate that they had made every effort to improve air quality and that effective source policies had been put in place. this is very important for the netherlands, since it does not stop construction projects. the protection of human health and the environment goes hand in hand with economic development. the vvd nevertheless considers that the compromise target of 20 micrograms pm2,5 for 2020 is unworkable. it is more prudent to wait until the 2013 evaluation period before formulating subsequent targets.

president. - i call the commission to order the report (doc. mr mandelson will recognize that. if we are received in china, we will do so with an official symbol, the flag. today it is being removed from the treaty and i call on all countries to follow germany and to recognise it officially as a symbol again, so that we can be welcomed with an official symbol anywhere in the world.
in writing. the council and the european parliament reached agreement on the additional funding for the galileo satellite system and on external affairs expenditure, including the police mission for kosovo. to this end, they increased the multiannual financial framework for 2007-13 and made use of the flexibility instrument. in addition, there have been readjustments within the existing budget lines. those readjustments could have been more far-reaching. i have particular difficulty in adjusting the previously agreed ceiling for eu funds until 2013 and using the flexibility instrument for this purpose. their use should be avoided and in any case limited to very exceptional circumstances. if there is a change in expenditure, it must be primarily offset by reductions in other budget lines. i am thinking in particular of expenditure which does not fall within the central objectives of the european union and of budget lines which are already lagging behind the planned expenditure.
in writing. in 1918, independent montenegro voluntarily chose to join the neighboring countries of serbia, croatia, bosnia-herzegovina and slovenia. after the breakup of yugoslavia, voters in 2006 democratically decided to no longer be bound only to serbia. this made montenegro the forty-ninth independent state in europe. it is important now that montenegro becomes a normal state, not a big monaco, not a paradise for rich foreigners who like to pay little tax, launder money and build palaces. montenegro must do more to combat environmental pollution and cigarette smuggling. i am pleased that the afet committee has adopted my amendments on housing and employment for refugees from serbia and kosovo. the stateless cannot remain stateless forever and montenegro must respect the council of europe's agreements on this. my proposal to restore the north-south rail link to niksic on the bosnian border and to shkoder in albania has also been accepted. increasing road traffic is not the answer. fortunately, unlike in his previous annual report, the rapporteur, mr vernola, is not calling for rapid accession to nato, nor for an economic policy that is even more neoliberal than is usual in the rest of europe. nato membership should not be a precondition for eu membership.
- a writer. i would like to begin by expressing my solidarity with these women, on behalf of all of us, i think. i'm glad this is on the agenda, because it's been a long time coming. for five months the european parliament has been debating whether or not human rights for women are really a priority. mr dillen, who is now absent, has just said that oppression of women is inherent in the koran. well, that's a load of crap, because the issue of comfort girls shows that men don't need the koran at all to oppress and abuse women. i am also a little disappointed in the attitude of the european parliament. as i said, there has been a long hesitation about this, and i hear arguments from colleagues in the european parliament, such as 'well, 90% of these girls did it voluntarily' and 'oh, that's the culture, you have to understand that'. i find that, frankly, repugnant. this is rape, and that's just always a crime in all times and all cultures. i understand that the school books have been changed in japan. but then i get a message from the japanese embassy that the story has to be told very carefully, because schoolchildren don't know much about sexuality yet and they could suffer psychological damage. no one's talking about the psychological damage of the comfort girls themselves. i find that quite shocking, to be honest. but i am glad that it is now on the agenda and that we are going to accept it immediately. i think it's important that apologies be sincere and unequivocal. it's not a formality. and if the previous prime minister abe has again questioned the whole issue, it is not enough for the current government to say "oh, but we are sticking to the previous policy". no, honest and unambiguous. and when i hear the arguments we have heard from the japanese embassy in recent days - and i do so in recognition of all that has been done - i think there is still much to be gained in the field of sincerity. i hope that this resolution will be a sign of solidarity with the victims.
author. madam president, we propose inserting the words ‘as is the moral duty of all countries’ after the first part of the first sentence, i.e. it would read: ‘encourages the japanese people and government to take further steps to recognise the full history of their nation, as is the moral duty of all countries’.
